“Plural without merger,” Nemi said.

“Plurality remains a model.”

“An increasingly employed model.”

Iona let the label stand on the glass.

By the time they resumed ordinary exchanges, the translation cradle no longer resembled a device for producing words. It resembled an argument conducted through test masses, each side correcting the other's assumptions with measurable force. Their shared vocabulary remained small. Its limits had become as informative as its successes.

When the cradle raised one bead and lowered another, the Eidos return reversed both. When Nemi repeated the relation, the channel produced three pulses: same, then different, then a long low pressure trace that neither woman could classify.

“Question?” Nemi suggested.

“Or correction.”

“Let us offer choices.”

They assigned two response wells at the base of the cradle. Left represented question. Right represented correction. Neither label traveled across the channel. The process would receive only two distinct acceleration relations paired with prior examples.

Nemi replayed the ambiguous trace.

The return moved the bead into the left well.

Iona's skin tightened along her forearms. The motion remained mechanical. A force crossed a paired dark-lattice channel and selected between two options defined seconds earlier.

“Question,” Nemi said.

“Probable question function.”

“You are going to call first contact a probable function.”

“We have contact with a selector. We do not know what supports it.”

“Then ask.”

Bex entered before they could construct the test.

He wore laboratory gray instead of ministerial clothes, but the Continuity seal at his collar made the disguise pointless. Two engineers came with him. One carried a hard disconnect for the channel tap.

“Report,” he said.

Iona showed him the bead choosing the left well. She described the physical relations, the repeat results, and the remaining uncertainty. Bex did not interrupt until Nemi displayed the two-column grammar.

“You included plurality,” he said.

“The returns contain simultaneous interval families,” Nemi answered. “They overlap without phase collapse.”

“That could describe echoes through several layers.”

“Yes.”

“Why give it a semantic label?”

“Because the source distinguished one gradient from several when we tested count.”

Iona opened that trace. A single bead received one pulse. Three beads received three asynchronous returns. When Nemi forced all three into one rhythm, the channel pushed them apart again.

Bex watched the replay. “You have established a preference against synchronization.”

Nemi stared at him. “You crossed out plurality in your head while watching three answers refuse to merge.”

“I crossed out what has not been proved.”

“Now I understand why you hired auditors.”

Bex almost answered, then looked toward Iona. “What test comes next?”

“Source support,” she said. “We ask whether the replying process occupies equipment, fluid layers, or another physical substrate.”

“No.”

“You asked for the next test.”

“I asked so I could decide whether to authorize it.”

Nemi moved between him and the cradle controls. “The channel has been trying to describe depth since Day 0.”

“Or our interpretation keeps selecting depth because Eidos contains ice worlds.”

“Then let us falsify it.”

Bex glanced at the engineer carrying the disconnect. “One passive decode. No new modulation.”

Nemi's anger converted instantly into work. She selected a stored return pattern recorded after a low-amplitude clearing twelve hours earlier. It contained a descending amplitude ladder followed by a sudden broad pulse and an irregular tail.

The cradle rendered the sequence through its plates. The first bead descended in measured steps. A second moved sideways under increasing force. At the broad pulse, both struck the lower plate. The irregular tail sent the lighter bead through a series of diminishing circles.

Nemi mapped the pattern onto known words: remote depth, increasing pressure, abrupt change, dispersal.

Iona added source uncertainty and replayed it through a different physical arrangement. The relationships remained.

“Fracture under pressure,” Nemi said.

“Pressure change followed by lost structure,” Iona corrected.

A fresh return entered the channel tap.

The tap should have remained receive-only under Bex's order. Nemi's replay used local plates, not the lattice. Yet the new pattern aligned with the cradle's rendering as though the external process had followed it through leakage in the paired field.

All six plates activated at once.

The laboratory floor dipped. Iona gripped the test table. The tungsten beads lifted from Nemi's palm and arranged themselves at different heights inside the cradle.

“Disconnect,” Bex said.

His engineer reached for the tap.

“Wait,” Iona said.

The beads began to move. One descended through five pressure steps. Three others shifted around it, each following a separate interval family. When the first reached the lowest point, the surrounding beads scattered against the cylinder wall.

Nemi recorded every force. “It is repeating the fracture relation.”

“Using our mass units,” Iona said.

A second arrangement formed. This time forty-three tiny pulses pressed through the plates in overlapping groups. Nemi's software could not render them separately, so the cradle translated amplitude into a vibrating band around the central bead.

The band contracted.

The central bead struck the lower plate.

No mystical recognition entered the room. The pattern did not speak in a voice or project an image. It demonstrated a force history with laboratory masses. Many remote gradients converged on one structure, compressed it, and destroyed its arrangement.

“Our clearings,” Iona said.

Bex's face held still.

Nemi pointed to the forty-three grouped pulses. “Source plurality. Forty-three.”

“Coincidence,” Bex said, too quickly.

Iona called up the Republic clearing signature. Every system applied a routing preamble before debt entered a sink. The preamble identified source count for load distribution. Forty-three inhabited systems had impressed that pattern into Eidos for more than a century.

“It learned the number from us,” she said.

The return moved the beads again. One local pulse. One remote fracture. Repetition. More fracture.

Humanity did not need to be named inside the channel. Its infrastructure supplied the identifier.

Nemi lowered her voice. “It identifies the recurring wounds with the forty-three-source pattern.”

Bex told the engineer to step away from the disconnect.

They built one final passive mapping from the new return. The external process offered layered pressure relations, simultaneous interval families, and a stable subset that assembled only while the exchange continued. Nemi rendered the subset as temporary coherence rather than individual body.

“A speaker,” she said.

“Functionally,” Iona said.

The cradle asked for a distinguishable reference through two wells: continuing pattern or temporary pattern. The return chose temporary, then gave a sequence of pressure and interior relation.

Nemi tried six renderings. The fifth preserved distinction without pretending equivalence to a human name.

“Aru-in-Pressure,” she said.

The external force moved the bead into the correction well.

Nemi changed the phrasing order: pressure-containing-Aru.

Correction again.

Iona placed pressure as the condition in which the temporary relation cohered, not a container around it. “Aru, formed in pressure.”

The bead entered the question well, then shifted halfway toward correction.

“Aru-in-Pressure,” Nemi repeated, keeping the joins audible.

This time the bead settled in neither well. It remained between them under balanced remote force.

“Accepted?” Bex asked.

“Not rejected,” Iona said.

Aru-in-Pressure sent the fracture relation once more. Behind it came a count longer than their cradle could resolve. Each interval carried slight differences. Not copies, Iona realized. Distinct events linked by the same external source signature.

Recurring wounds.

Nemi asked through local acceleration, “Source knows harm?”

Bex seized her wrist before she completed the plate order.

“No response,” he said.

Nemi pulled free. “We have spent five hours responding.”

“We have characterized an intrusion. From this point, containment applies.”

“A selector beyond Eidos just identified us as the source of repeated destruction.”

“An unknown process identified our routing pattern beside pressure changes. Your moral interpretation outruns the signal.”

Iona closed the active controls but left the receive tap open. “What does containment permit?”

“Passive recording. No semantic tests. No publication. No change to network operations without continuity authorization.”

“Clearings continue?”

“At reduced amplitude.”

“Aru has linked them to damage.”

Bex looked at the suspended beads. “And seventeen billion people remain linked to receivers. I will not cause one known catastrophe because an unknown intelligence presents a claim we have translated for less than an hour.”

“Intelligence,” Nemi said. “You used it.”

“I used the operational category.”

The cradle plates clicked as Aru changed the field. A bead moved to local, another to remote. The local bead split the relation into several asynchronous pulses. The remote bead answered with one compressed surge.

Nemi kept her hands off the controls. “It is asking about plurality again.”

Iona watched the sequence repeat with a new ending. Many local patterns. One damaging output. A pause in the question well.

“Does the source contain separate selectors?” she said.

Bex ordered the receive tap closed.

Before the engineer reached it, Aru shaped the closest translation the cradle had yet produced. The words appeared from Nemi's grammar one uncertain relation at a time:

Do the senders contain dissenters?

Chapter 5 — Borrowed Weight

The archive door refused the word empty.

Iona entered it under search purpose, and the Curvature Office lexicon returned six hundred twelve categories, none approved for a physical condition. Vacant required a survey standard. Uninhabited required evidence. Unassigned described only an account. Empty appeared in public sink certificates, policy speeches, and clearing schedules, but the live archive treated it as rhetoric.

Nemi leaned over Iona's shoulder. “Try historical usage.”

The terminal asked for ministerial clearance.

They stood in a records vault beneath the Nacre receiver hub while containment staff dismantled the translation cradle two decks above. Bex had allowed one final audit of Eidos certification, perhaps because stopping it would have required a written order. Two security officers watched from beyond a mesh wall. They could see every display but not hear ordinary speech through the vault's vibration baffles.

Iona selected historical usage and laid Bex's temporary key against the terminal.

The archive opened.

Eighty-eight years of certification forms appeared as stacked layers. Each renewal preserved the prior survey and added a current authorization. Eidos changed from candidate sink to provisional sink, then to certified vacant receiver complement. Twelve years later, a policy editor shortened the phrase to empty system. No new observation supported the change.

“Language drift,” Nemi said.

“Authority drift.”

“Same letters, different department.”

Iona began at the first survey.

The Republic team had observed Eidos for thirty-one days using three telescopes and two remote gravimetric passes. They searched for atmospheric disequilibrium, radio emission, coherent light, industrial heat, moving megastructures, chemical waste, artificial magnetic fields, and engine signatures. Every life criterion required electromagnetic evidence. The team found none.

The gravimetric instruments served another purpose. Their operators mapped large subsurface oceans because sudden debt pulses could eject liquid through an ice crust. The survey did not treat ordered pressure layers as possible life. Its standard assumed metabolism needed chemical exchange fast enough to alter atmosphere, surface, or heat balance.

Nemi opened the committee notes. “Here. A reviewer asked about computation in solid-state defects.”

The answer occupied one sentence: No plausible biosphere persists without detectable free-energy throughput.

“Did they model superfluid phase structures?” Iona asked.

“No. They cite conventional crystal defects at ninety kelvin.”

“Eidos strata run colder under higher pressure.”

“And the review knew that. See the temperature appendix.”

Someone had placed the right physical facts in separate documents and never allowed them to meet.

The separation continued into instrument design. Iona opened the original survey probe specification and traced each sensor to its destination field. Radio measurements fed the life panel. Spectral chemistry fed it. Waste heat received a probabilistic score. Gravimetric anomalies went only to sink safety, where reviewers asked whether the ice could accept force without ejecting debris.

No data crossed between those panels.

A deep pressure shift could lower a safety score, but it could not raise the probability of life. If the same shift repeated after a clearing, the system classified it as accommodation and increased the modeled capacity of the sink. Repetition made Eidos look safer precisely because analysts had excluded the possibility of response.

Nemi pulled up a training simulation from the certification course. “I took this twelve years ago.”

A model ice body appeared above the terminal. Trainees had to assign a debt cap from limited observations. The simulation included a regular subsurface migration after each test pulse. Its approved explanation called the migration plastic settling.

“What happens if you flag it as possible organization?” Iona asked.

Nemi restarted the exercise and chose that option. The program marked her answer unsupported, then deducted points for anthropomorphic inference.

“They trained us out of the question,” she said.

“They trained us against false positives.”

“Using a standard that made this positive impossible.”

Iona opened the revision history. The exercise had entered training one year after Eidos certification and used anonymized Eidos survey data. Thousands of auditors had learned professional caution from the very anomaly they should have investigated.

She ran the simulation herself. Instead of selecting possible organization, she requested additional gravimetric passes. The program denied them on budget grounds. She requested a reduced debt cap pending evidence. That answer received partial credit but warned that unnecessary restriction could endanger receiver continuity.

“What answer earns full credit?” Nemi asked.

Iona chose stable deep-layer accommodation and retained the proposed cap.

The program congratulated her for balancing evidence with operational need.

Nemi reached for the close control. Iona stopped her and saved the result beside the live cradle finding. One showed what the Republic's instruments could observe. The other showed what its auditors had been rewarded for concluding.

Iona copied the life criteria into a fresh comparison. Electromagnetic output: absent. Atmospheric chemistry: not applicable to rogue ice bodies. Thermal excess: below remote sensitivity. Mechanical activity: recorded, excluded from biology review. Pressure history: not studied. Responsive behavior through the debt channel: not part of any standard.

The certificate did not prove absence. It proved silence in frequencies humans had chosen to monitor.

“Empty meant electromagnetically silent,” Nemi said.

Iona marked the sentence as a finding. “And mechanically useful.”

A survey budget file showed why the distinction vanished. The network expansion needed four new sinks before a scheduled habitat wave entered operation. Eidos offered deep ice, low orbital traffic, and no diplomatic claimant. A longer survey would have delayed receiver activation across three systems. The committee accepted the electromagnetic standard because every known organism left electromagnetic consequences.

“They asked whether Eidos resembled known life,” Nemi said. “Then converted no into no life.”

“They asked the available instruments.”

“You are defending them again.”

“I am locating the failure.”

“Does location matter after Aru showed us the fractures?”

“Yes. We cannot correct a standard by accusing its dead authors of refusing knowledge they lacked. We correct the decision that kept using it after contrary evidence arrived.”

Nemi turned toward the restricted annex marker. “Six years.”

The annex still required a second ministerial key. Bex had not supplied it.

Iona traced every later renewal first. Eidos's debt cap rose fourteen times. After several increases, remote gravimetric records showed subsurface redistribution. Reviewers called the shifts settling events consistent with accepted load. No team revisited the original life criteria. Certification became self-supporting: because Eidos carried empty status, any response from Eidos entered the category of nonliving mechanics.

A return pattern thirty-two years ago contained eleven prime-spaced intervals. The clearing engine discarded it as phase noise.

Twenty-one years ago, a large transfer produced three matched counterpulses. Engineers treated them as channel rebound even though dark-lattice theory prohibited rebound after acceptance.

Sixteen years ago, the Meridian emergency transfer touched Eidos for less than a second before routing changed. Its log contained a weak precursor return.

Iona stopped scrolling.

The date matched Sella's final shift.

Nemi saw the name before Iona spoke. “Meridian used Eidos?”

“According to this routing fragment.”

“The inquiry report says the emergency load never left the local receiver.”

“It says the transfer failed before sink acceptance.”

The raw route showed otherwise. Eidos accepted part of the pulse. A structured return entered the Meridian network thirty-seven milliseconds later. Then someone redirected the remaining debt to another sink and removed the Eidos code from the incident summary.

Iona's hands remained on the terminal rail. She could feel Nacre's receiver hum through the metal, a vibration below hearing that shifted whenever the hub cleared a local load. Her body wanted to pull away. She held the rail.

“Open the precursor,” she said.

Nemi displayed it. The return amplitude stayed far below the catastrophic event that followed. Its intervals showed a short prime ladder, then a pressure rise. The pattern resembled Aru's warning before the cradle demonstrated fracture.

Sella could not have known that grammar. No one at Meridian had studied the return as communication. Yet Sella had manually restored spin to a nursery ring while the receiver console reported a local operator fault. The official inquiry blamed her for bypassing a safety interlock.

Iona searched her sister's maintenance entries. Most remained sealed under casualty privacy, a restriction she had challenged twice and lost. The Eidos route record carried a cross-reference to one suppressed note, then replaced the link with an operator-error code.

“Request the cross-reference,” Nemi said.

“It will alert Continuity.”

“They already have two officers watching us.”

“Not to this thread.”

Iona saved the address without opening it. Containment logged requests as well as access, and the sealed reference might matter more than the answer she wanted immediately.

She returned to the routing fragment. Beside the precursor lay a handwritten engineering annotation digitized from the console pad: Load has destination even when account refuses it. The handwriting did not belong to Sella. An inquiry engineer had paraphrased her spoken warning.

Memory supplied Sella's original words with cruel precision.

Weight goes somewhere.

As children, Iona had heard a rule about bolts, spin, and dropped tools. After Meridian, she had treated it as a private warning against careless systems. In the archive vault, the lesson became an accusation aimed at the entire Republic. For more than a century, clearing clerks had named every local benefit and left the destination inside one word: empty.

The word had not removed destination. It had removed the obligation to inspect it.

Nemi touched the interval trace. “This may be the first structured response tied to Meridian.”

“First surviving one.”

“Do we tell Aru?”

“Containment forbids response.”

“I did not ask what Bex permits.”

Iona closed the Meridian file. “We do not yet know what the pattern says. Giving Aru an event we cannot characterize could convert Sella's death into evidence for a claim we have not tested.”

“She died inside the same mechanism.”

“That does not make her death ours to translate badly.”

Nemi looked away, angry but listening.

They continued through the renewal chain. Ten years ago, Eidos returns increased in frequency after Nacre doubled receiver capacity. Seven years ago, a sink analyst named Pori Sal flagged prime intervals across twelve clearings. Her report recommended suspending Eidos assignments pending a deep gravimetric survey.

The recommendation disappeared from the next certification packet.

“Where did Sal go?” Nemi asked.

Iona opened personnel history. “Transferred to comet mass accounting. Retired four years ago.”

“Alive?”

“Yes.”

“Can we contact her?”

“Not under containment.”

Nemi wrote the name on the orange cord attached to her stylus.

Six years ago, the comparative interval study began. Its visible custody shell listed fourteen analysts, three receiver engineers, two geophysicists, and one continuity observer whose name remained hidden behind office authority. The study ran for nineteen days. Researchers compared returned pulses from seven certified sinks. Only Eidos showed interval selection that changed after outgoing load variation.

The abstract had been removed. Its statistical appendix remained because an archive migration misclassified it as calibration support.

Nemi opened the appendix before the system could correct itself.

Tables filled the display. Experimenters had varied clearing amplitude within operational tolerances. Eidos returns changed not merely in scale but in sequence. When transfers stopped for three hours, the channel sent repeated low-amplitude patterns. When transfers resumed, the patterns grew more complex. A blind classifier distinguished Eidos returns from natural sink noise in 99.8 percent of trials.

“No semantic test,” Iona said.

“They did not need ours to know it responded.”

“Agreed.”

A final table compared four hypotheses: instrument error, geological settling, autonomous machinery, and unknown selecting process. The study rejected the first two. It could not distinguish the latter pair.

Someone had written the disposition in a narrow field beneath the statistics: No action. Operational dependence outweighs unverified source-status risk. Reclassify returns as deep-layer accommodation.

The signature remained masked.

Nemi copied the appendix to their temporary audit workspace. The system blocked export. She began transcribing key values into her cord, using knots between digits.

“You will run out of sleeve,” Iona said.

“I will request a longer stylus.”

The vault door unlocked.

Elian Bex entered alone. He had removed his laboratory coat. His shirt cuffs showed gray dust from the translation cradle plates.

“You opened the migration appendix,” he said.

“It fell within certification authority,” Iona answered.

“It fell through a storage error.”

“The data remain evidence regardless of filing competence.”

Bex looked at the Meridian trace on the side display. For the first time since Perihelion, anger crossed his face without disguise. “Why did you open that incident?”

“Eidos appears in its raw route.”

“It does not belong to the present contact finding.”

“A structured return entered Meridian before Sella's receiver failure.”

“You do not know that it caused the failure.”

“I know the inquiry omitted it.”

“Because the return carried less force than the plate malfunction.”

“Then omission protected nothing except the finding.”

Bex approached the terminal. “Close the incident file.”

Iona kept her hand on the rail. “Unlock the annex.”

“No.”

“Then I will record that Continuity refused access to a study proving responsive behavior in a certified sink.”

“You are already under containment. That record will remain here.”

“It will also attach to every future certification bearing my authority.”

“You can be removed from certification duty.”

“Then my removal becomes part of the chain.”

Bex studied her with the exhausted concentration he had given the cradle. “You learned that from Sella.”

“She taught me to name destinations.”

“She taught half the Meridian engineering corps to make insubordination sound like maintenance.”

“You knew her.”

“I reviewed the incident after her death.”

“That does not answer the tense you used.”

His gaze shifted to the precursor trace. “I met her once during a spindle retrofit. She objected to a receiver shortcut and made me crawl through forty meters of bearing access to see the load path.”

“What shortcut?”

“Unrelated to the incident.”

“Was she right?”

“Yes.”

The admission changed no record. It changed Iona's measure of the man. Bex had allowed Sella to remain an operator-error code while remembering the exact quality of her dissent.

Nemi pointed at the disposition field. “Who wrote no action?”

Bex did not answer.

“Who reclassified the returns?” Iona asked.

“Continuity authority.”

“Which officer?”

“The decision belonged to the ministry.”

“Institutions do not press keys.”

“People acting under office do.”

Iona brought the study's timeline beside Bex's career. Six years ago he had served as Deputy Minister for Receiver Stability, with authority over sink-risk classification. His old engineering key appeared in the annex custody shell as a masked credential family.

Nemi enlarged it. “Same key generation.”

“Thousands of engineers used that generation,” Bex said.

“Thirty-one held deputy-minister scope,” Iona said. “Four worked in Continuity. One had Eidos clearance.”

Bex reached past her and closed the Meridian window. He did not close the comparative study.

“Unlock it,” she said again.

“What do you expect to find?”

“Observation records. Names. The reasoning behind continuation. Any attempt to identify the responding process.”

“You have the reasoning. Operational dependence.”

“That phrase does not calculate consequences. It hides them.”

Bex's control thinned. “At the time of the study, twenty-nine billion people lived under Republic receiver coverage. Abrupt suspension of all questionable sinks would have produced orbital drift, ring desynchronization, hospital failures, and transport losses before replacement surveys could finish.”

“All questionable sinks?” Nemi asked. “The study identified Eidos.”

“It raised the possibility that our certification standard could fail elsewhere.”

“So you knew the word empty could not carry its claim.”

“I knew our standard had an unresolved edge.”

“You knew Eidos answered changing load.”

“I knew a selecting process answered. Machinery remained plausible.”

“Did you send a survey?”

“No craft could reach Eidos within a useful policy window.”

“Remote gravimetry?”

“Would have required more debt-channel modulation and might have worsened whatever we observed.”

“Reduce Eidos assignments?”

“We shifted eleven percent to other sinks. Tidal limits prevented more.”

“Publish the uncertainty?”

Bex looked through the mesh at the guards. “Markets would have treated uncertainty as shutdown. Habitats would have hoarded fuel. Systems with alternate sinks would have abandoned those without them. The Republic could have broken before we knew whether a machine objected to load.”

Iona heard the practiced architecture in his answer. He had not invented it in this room. Six years of policy had accumulated around the first concealed decision, each later danger offered as proof that the earlier concealment had been necessary.

“You made continued ignorance part of continuity,” she said.

“I kept clearing capacity while engineers searched for alternatives.”

“Where are the alternatives?”

“Insufficient.”

“After six years?”

“Every local solution costs mass, fuel, or habitat volume. Assemblies approve distant sinks and reject visible sacrifices.”

“You never gave them the actual choice.”

“I gave them survival.”

A low vibration crossed the vault floor as Nacre's receiver cleared another batch. Iona watched the status panel. Destination codes cycled past. Eidos did not appear, but two other systems carried the same antique vacant certification.

“Does Aru's demonstration change your classification?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“What changes operationally?”

“Reduced amplitude. Accelerated alternate modeling. Containment until we can prevent panic.”

“Clearings continue.”

“They must.”

Nemi untied the stylus cord from her sleeve and set it on the terminal. Knots preserved the appendix values in a line of orange fiber. “You keep treating known harm as a risk and familiar dependence as fact.”

“I can count the people under receivers.”

“You could count the replies.”

“That does not count lives.”

“We did not know to count them because you sealed the evidence.”

Bex turned toward the archive door. Iona blocked his key from closing the session.

“One finding remains,” she said. “The comparative study established structured response six years ago. I need the responsible observer's testimony.”

“You have my policy account.”

“I need the fact without office language. Did you personally review the raw Eidos sequences?”

He could order both auditors removed. He could let the masked credential stand and force them to prove a signature through restricted systems. Instead, Bex placed his palm beside Iona's on the terminal rail. The receiver vibration passed through both hands.

“Yes,” he said. “I saw them.”

Chapter 6 — The Republic's Balance

The child in Cylinder Twelve died at forty-three seconds.

Elian Bex stopped the continuity model and moved the time index back to zero. The transparent cylinder hung above his desk, a handspan model of an orbital city rendered in amber light. At his touch, its inhabited rings began to turn again. Hospitals brightened. Water climbed through spoke pipes. Freight platforms moved outward under spin. Twelve million simulated people resumed lives the model had just ended.

He cut their receiver feed a second time.

Nothing happened for six seconds. That delay had misled three Assembly observers during the morning briefing. They had watched the stable rings and mistaken stored momentum for safety. Then load compensation vanished from the industrial spine. A flywheel cradle shifted against its mechanical restraint. The program painted the first injuries in small red marks.

At nineteen seconds, an elevator entered a spoke with the wrong counterload. At twenty-seven, the hospital ring began an emergency despin to protect a surgical deck. The change altered water pressure in two residential arcs. Cylinder Twelve had built its nursery near the hub, where the apparent gravity ran low and the receiver supplied most of the local down. Forty-three seconds after shutdown, an infant ventilator lost coolant circulation.

Bex paused the death again.

The Ministry office around him stayed quiet except for the cooling channels under the floor. Nacre's blue-white star filled the outer window through three layers of radiation glass. A shade crossed it on schedule, and hard light softened to the gray used for administrative mornings. Bex had approved replacement bearings for that shade when he still carried an engineering bag. He knew which contractor had lied about the first alloy batch. He also knew how long the city could survive if all six shade trains stopped.

This knowledge had once felt useful.

He opened the population layer. Across the Forty-Three Systems Republic, 31.4 billion lives appeared as density rather than faces. The display marked 17 billion in structures whose stable orbit, spin, thermal routing, or acceleration schedule depended on curvature clearing. Some could localize within months. Some needed years of tug work and mass transfer. A few possessed no physical margin at all.

Seventeen billion. He had said the number so often during the last thirty hours that it no longer fit in his mouth. On the model it fit inside a line of text.

A secure chime sounded at the door.

“Enter.”

Iona Rhyll came in carrying a black evidence wafer. She had slept recently enough to change her clothes but not long enough to soften the bruised shadows beneath her eyes. Her deputy waited in the outer analysis room. Bex had asked for Iona alone, then disliked himself for the calculation behind the request.

“You wanted the shutdown model,” he said.

“I wanted the records you sealed six years ago.”

“They are being transferred to your review vault.”

“After your office removes continuity identifiers.”

“After we remove access paths that could let anyone issue receiver commands.”

Iona put the wafer on his desk. “You have had six years to choose the description that sounds least like concealment.”

Bex let the accusation remain where she placed it. “Watch the model.”

“I know what sudden shutdown does.”

“You know the categories. I need you to see the order.”

He restarted Cylinder Twelve. This time he slowed the index, forcing each failure to occupy the room. A maintenance worker reached for a rail when the industrial spine's load shifted. A cargo handler vanished behind a tumbling container. Pumps lost head pressure. Emergency systems came alive, consumed their reserves, then began disagreeing about which ring deserved the remaining power.

Iona watched without turning away. Bex had expected anger to make her impatient. Instead she leaned toward the rendering and read each timestamp.

“At forty-three seconds,” he said, “the nursery loses one ventilator.”

“At fifty-one, mechanical backup engages.”

“For three units. There are four patients.”

“Why only three?”

“Because the hospital filed its capacity plan before the nursery expanded.”

Her gaze shifted to him. “That has nothing to do with Eidos.”

“It has everything to do with an abrupt order. Systems fail through old choices, not clean diagrams.”

The cylinder reached three minutes. Tugs attempted to arrest the ring drift with conventional thrust. One plume crossed a radiator field because traffic control had lost a compensated reference frame. The display added thousands of injuries and revised projected deaths upward.

Iona touched the pause control herself. “You selected a habitat with neglected backup.”

“I selected one with average reserve compliance.”

“Show me the best case.”

Bex loaded it. A wealthy spindle in the Nacre system appeared, equipped with triple mechanical redundancy, excess fusion capacity, and two resident tug squadrons. Sudden receiver loss killed no one in the first hour. It did break an agricultural bearing, force the evacuation of an outer district, and consume eighteen percent of local maneuvering fuel before the first day ended.

“Now the worst,” Iona said.

“No.”

“Why?”

“Because it teaches nothing the middle case did not. It only makes refusal harder.”

Her expression changed by a degree. “You still think this meeting concerns persuasion.”

Bex closed the model. The amber city folded into his desk. “Every meeting inside this building concerns persuasion. That does not make the numbers false.”

He crossed to a cabinet set into the wall. The lock accepted his palm but rejected the current ministry credential. Bex reached under his shirt and drew out a small steel tool on a faded cord. Its teeth had been cut for physical contacts, not data. He inserted the engineering key beside the electronic plate and turned both together.

The cabinet opened.

Inside lay a grease-darkened field case, two paper notebooks, a cracked pressure gauge, and the orange helmet he had worn during the Aster Ring famine. The helmet still carried a white handprint where a coolant technician had steadied herself against him during a false-gravity surge. Bex took out the nearer notebook.

“I kept this key after the ministry converted the old receiver housings,” he said. “A violation of three security rules.”

“I can note it in the investigation.”

“You should.”

He gave her the notebook. Its pages held his handwriting from twenty-seven years earlier: bearing temperatures, ballast positions, names of dead relays, names of living mechanics. During the Aster failure, a clearing scheduler had trapped three habitats in the same sink queue. Bex had spent eleven days moving water, slag, and two obsolete flywheels to keep the rings within tolerance.

Iona turned a page. “You localized part of the debt.”

“We did not call it that. We called it keeping the walls behind the air.”

“Then you know there are alternatives.”

“I know alternatives require mass, fuel, machines, routes, and time. A moral instruction cannot tow a flywheel.”

“Neither can a dead civilization.”

Bex felt the sentence enter the space between them. He looked past her at the muted star. Six years earlier, the first structured returns from Eidos had crossed his desk as a classified sink-instability report. The pressure intervals showed regularity. Not proof of life, the analysts had written. Not noise either.

He had ordered a deeper survey. He had also ordered the channel left open while the survey proceeded.

The survey never received its carrier allocation. There had been a food failure at Ravel, then a spindle strike, then an election in which three continuity delegates campaigned on abolishing costly sink review. Bex had renewed the seal every ninety days until renewal became habit. Each signature converted uncertainty into permission for another season.

“I did not know what they were,” he said.

“You knew they replied.”

“Yes.”

“You knew our clearings hurt whatever made the reply.”

“I knew amplitude correlated with stronger returns.”

“That sentence has sheltered you for six years.”

Bex took the notebook back. “No sentence shelters anyone from this.”

His desk lit with an incoming priority. The network controller had completed the reduced-export simulation he requested. Bex opened it between them. Forty-three systems formed a pale web. Receiver hubs pulsed according to debt volume. Beyond the inhabited routes, the certified sinks showed as dark destinations with no population layer.

He selected a ninety-percent reduction in exports to Eidos. The model redistributed allowable loads to six other sinks while local systems began mechanical correction. Kestrel Deep reached its tidal cap first. Varda followed. Three low-mass habitats entered unsafe drift within nine hours.

“Reduce demand before redistribution,” Iona said.

“Choose sectors.”

“All nonessential comfort fields.”

Bex removed retail stabilization, private transport compensation, variable floor loads, sports drums, and decorative fountain gravity. The reduction looked large on a public list. It changed total debt by less than four percent.

“Cargo assistance,” she said.

He cut it. Injury projections rose at once, followed by food delays two days later.

“Military load fields.”

“Already queued.”

The Republic defense facilities vanished from the receiver list. Enough capacity appeared to protect one medium cylinder or six hospital rings.

Iona examined the web. “You built the order to prove your preference.”

“I built no order. Choose again.”

For the next hour she did. They suspended ore lifts, delayed shade maintenance, canceled three ship burns, and moved every system with available conventional capacity onto local payment. Each choice spared pressure at Eidos and moved risk toward a human structure. Bex did not object when she accepted those risks. He named them.

At thirty-seven percent reduction, the model held for a day. By the third day, maneuvering fuel shortages forced receiver use back up in the outer systems. At the sixth, agricultural losses began. At the ninth, the first habitat crossed a structural drift limit unless a tug convoy arrived from eleven light-hours away.

Iona enlarged the failing habitat. New Carth had grown around a captured carbonaceous moon, with pressure hulls tunneled into rock and three residential wheels mounted along its equator. Ordinary mass gave the settlement a useful center, but its wheels relied on receiver trim whenever mines moved ore from one hemisphere to the other. The current stockpile sat north of the equator. Without clearing, that imbalance would force the wheel bearings sideways until one housing cracked.

“Move the ore south,” she said.

“Conveyors need fourteen days.”

“Use surface crawlers.”

“Their traction field draws from the receiver.”

“Mechanical cleats.”

Bex applied the change. Crawlers fitted with cleats could move enough ore in nine days, if they stopped delivering carbon feedstock to the air plants. The atmosphere reserve then fell below statutory margin.

“Send feedstock from the next moon,” Iona said.

“No cargo window for six days.”

“Advance it.”

“The freighter burns reserve fuel.”

“Let it.”

He entered the cost. New Carth survived. The freighter lost the reserve assigned to a medical evacuation contract shared by four mining camps.

Iona rubbed the side of her thumb against the desk, then selected the camps. One had a conventional shuttle. Another could support twelve hours of emergency life care. The remaining two depended on the freighter for any surgery their small clinics could not perform.

“Move a surgical unit out now,” she said.

“With whose staff?”

She searched the personnel layer. Bex waited. He had done this during Aster, though with fewer screens and more voices shouting through suit radios. Every movable machine already served a purpose. Every qualified body slept, ate, grieved, and made mistakes. Models permitted redeployment without showing the moment when a surgeon told her child that the next three months belonged to strangers.

Iona found a naval medical unit two transfers away. “They can divide.”

“They can, if Defense releases them.”

“You are the minister.”

“Not of Defense.”

“You sit on continuity cabinet.”

“So I can ask an admiral to accept risk under her own name.”

“Ask.”

Bex opened a real priority request. The model could assume compliance; the Republic required a person to agree. He sent the medical transfer to the Defense duty office with his own liability code. No answer came before the requested window expired on the simulation.

They ran New Carth again with the request pending. At Day Four, a miner suffered a crushed pelvis. Local care stabilized him. The freighter remained with the ore convoy and no rescue ship came. His survival probability declined each hour.

“This is your argument,” Iona said. “One man on a table, multiplied until no one can act.”

“It is not an argument. His name would exist if the event occurred.”

“The pressure lineage you strike also has a name.”

“Yes.”

“Yet the model gives it no bed, no age, no one waiting outside a clinic.”

“I cannot invent what we have not learned.”

“You can stop treating the blank as zero.”

Bex added a second casualty field beside the human projection. He labeled it unmeasured sink harm and made its value block every optimization that assumed no external cost. The program rejected the field as nonnumeric. He overrode it.

The model now returned no preferred plan. Every branch carried an unresolved red mark at Eidos. No algorithm could compare a ventilator, a crushed pelvis, and a damaged pressure lineage without choosing a unit that erased two of them.

“That is more accurate,” Iona said.

“It is less useful.”

“Only if usefulness requires permission.”

Bex left the red mark in place. He returned to the network view and found twelve other habitats with solvable imbalances. Their solutions required ninety-one cargo transfers, six military releases, forty thousand spinwright shifts, and fuel reserves assembled over decades. None made the sink harm smaller by itself. Together, performed before an uncontrolled shutdown, they changed the shape of possible survival.

At last the Defense duty office accepted the medical transfer. A real surgeon somewhere on Nacre had just received an order generated by two people arguing over a simulated miner. Bex entered the acceptance into the model rather than letting it vanish into institutional background.

“Ten,” Iona said. “Give every system ten standard days under mandatory reduction. Use the interval to move ballast and stage conventional burns.”

“That requires authority I do not possess.”

“The Assembly can grant it.”

“The Assembly can debate it.”

Bex opened the emergency statute. Its language had been written after a receiver cascade killed eighty thousand people at Cresset. A Continuity minister could assume network allocation, transport priority, and compulsory mass-control powers for ten standard days. The order would begin immediately upon certification of systemic risk. Renewal required two-thirds of the Assembly.

Iona read the first page. “You prepared this before I arrived.”

“I began preparing it when you froze Perihelion.”

“You intend to invoke emergency authority.”

“I intend to ask whether preparation can buy the choice you claim we have.”

“Authority does not create tugs.”

“It can move them.”

“It does not create fuel.”

“It can stop people spending what remains.”

Her hand closed over the edge of his desk. “And during those ten days?”

There it stood. The decision he had broken into percentages, timestamps, and procedural drafts. Outside the office, thousands of receivers continued clearing. Each low-amplitude export entered a world whose inhabitants humans had failed to recognize. Stopping now produced deaths the models could name. Continuing produced harms no human account yet measured.

Bex brought up the Eidos channel schedule. “We reduce amplitude to the lowest level that prevents network correction. No discretionary clearing. No reserve accumulation. Every system begins local load shedding.”

“You keep sending pulses.”

“Yes.”

“After Aru-in-Pressure identified them as wounds.”

“Yes.”

Iona stepped back from the desk. The distance carried more judgment than her voice had. “Then say what you authorize.”

Bex had spent his career teaching officials to use precise words during failures. A bearing did not become tired. A ring did not want to fall. A casualty estimate did not equal a body. Precision kept engineers from lying to machinery, but it could help ministers lie to themselves.

“I authorize continued harm to Eidos,” he said, “at reduced amplitude, while we prepare to stop without killing the people who depend on us.”

“Do the people at Eidos consent to that schedule?”

“No.”

“Then your interval belongs to the stronger side.”

“It belongs to the side that can still change its machines.”

The reply surprised him with its honesty. He did not know whether it improved anything.

Iona picked up her evidence wafer. “Nemi thinks the pressure grammar may distinguish separate speakers. If she is right, you are not bargaining with an injured system. You are imposing another ten days on populations.”

“I read her analysis.”

“And?”

“And I increased the projected moral cost because I had no unit for it.”

For the first time, anger disturbed Iona's control. “You think adding a column makes this visible.”

“No. I think refusing a column makes it easy to omit.”

A network warning opened over the desk. Three receiver hubs had detected phase resistance from Eidos. The amplitude remained below correction threshold, but the intervals tightened with each pulse. Nemi's preliminary translation labeled the pattern as disagreement among pressure-names. One response favored continued exchange. Another carried a ratio the cradle associated with return.

Bex read it twice.

Iona saw the second classification. “They are deciding.”

“So are we.”

“No. You already did.”

She left the office without taking his notebook or asking for the key. The door closed softly. Bex remained beside the old field case while the network schedule counted down to its next clearing.

He called the duty controller.

“Cancel every discretionary receiver load,” he said. “Issue mechanical preparation orders to all forty-three systems. Place tug fuel, ballast reserves, and spinwright crews under provisional continuity hold.”

The controller hesitated. “Under what authority?”

“Ministerial risk certification now. Draft emergency authority for Assembly notice after the Helix review.”

“And the active sink schedule?”

Bex looked at Cylinder Twelve, dormant in the desk. He could still see the infant ventilator lose flow at forty-three seconds. Behind that child stood seventeen billion others, each attached to a machine with a different hidden weakness. Behind his screen, beyond any human image, pressure lineages received whatever force he sent.

“Eidos only,” he said. “Minimum stabilizing amplitude. No surplus clearing.”

The controller read the order back. Procedure demanded confirmation by a second channel. Bex drew the engineering key from its cord and fitted it into the old physical socket beneath his desk.

Metal met metal. The contact closed.

Far across the Republic, receivers lowered their fields. Hospital spindles accepted mechanical schedules. Cargo lifts slowed. Tugs received orders that would consume years of reserve fuel. The next reduced pulse entered the dark lattice carrying less force than the one before it, and still more than nothing.

Bex turned the key and authorized it.

Chapter 7 — The First Repossession

The cargo deck rose under Iona's feet before the alarm found a name for it.

She had been standing beside a tether winch on Helix Quay, watching Nemi compare pressure intervals, when the apparent down shifted six degrees toward the outer hull. A stack of sealed freight cases leaned in unison. Their restraint webbing snapped tight with a sound like a giant drawing breath.

Iona grabbed the winch frame. Nemi caught the open analysis case with both hands. Around them, loaders stopped because the deck still pressed against their boots and their bodies had not yet accepted that down had moved.

“Receiver phase excursion,” the quay announced. “Hold current stations.”

A loose torque wrench rolled past Iona. It curved sideways across the deck, struck a cargo rail, and climbed it.

“That is not an excursion,” Nemi said.

Her screen showed the Eidos channel at minimum stabilizing amplitude. Bex's reduction order had reached Helix Quay three hours earlier, forcing cargo operations onto a slower mechanical schedule. The receiver continued carrying tether trim, emergency crane loads, and the balance corrections that kept the quay's twelve cargo decks aligned around its spine.

Another interval entered the pressure trace. Not prime. Not part of Aru's careful translation sequence. The modulation arrived broad and exact, carrying the same integrated stress Helix Quay had exported over the previous eleven days.

Nemi stared at the total. “They're sending it back.”

Iona read the value twice. “All of it?”

“Within instrument tolerance.”

The restraint webbing began to sing.

A warning moved through the quay in layered voices. Receiver fault. Spine imbalance. Uncommanded curvature increase. Each system had recognized one consequence and failed to describe the whole event.

Iona opened a line to control. “This is Rhyll, Curvature Office. Disconnect the Eidos channel.”

“Command rejected,” said the receiver. “Outstanding correction in progress.”

“It is not our correction.”

The machine did not have a field for that distinction.

Gravity rotated another nine degrees and doubled. Iona's knees struck the deck. Nemi fell against the winch, shoulder first, but kept one hand around the analysis case. Freight cases tore sideways against their webs. One restraint anchor pulled out of the floor and crossed the aisle at head height.

A loader shoved Iona down. The metal fitting passed over them and punched into a pressure partition. Air screamed through the ragged opening until an automatic seal dropped.

“Clear the stacks!” someone shouted.

People crawled toward fixed rails. On a normal cargo deck, painted arrows marked the nearest pressure refuge. Now those arrows pointed across a sloping surface toward walls that had acquired weight.

Iona found Nemi on hands and knees. Blood ran from her temple into one eyebrow.

“Can you stand?”

“Direction remains under negotiation.”

The attempt at humor came too quickly. Nemi's pupils tracked at different speeds.

Iona clipped her deputy's harness to the winch frame. “Do not move.”

“Excellent plan. Tell the quay.”

The public channel filled with overlapping calls from all twelve decks. A crane carriage had broken loose on Deck Four. Two lifts had stopped between the spine and the outer ring. Cargo workers in a pressurized loading cradle reported their floor had disappeared.

Then a voice cut through the noise with practiced force.

“Helix control, this is Morrowline. We are still hard-docked at Cargo Two. Give me spine rotation and receiver status.”

Iona recognized Tavin Serr before the channel labeled him. She had met the captain the previous day while matching his freighter's reversal event to the Eidos trace. He had listened to the explanation of structured pressure replies with the hostility of a man learning that his damaged ship had participated in someone else's injury.

Control answered, “Captain, release clamps and clear the quay.”

“I asked for rotation.”

“Quay load vector is unstable.”

“So is my dock. Numbers.”

Iona keyed in. “Tavin, full repossession through the Eidos pairing. Integrated return matches eleven days of local clearing.”

“How long?”

“I don't know.”

“Can you shut it down?”

“The receiver treats the return as mandatory correction.”

“Then tell me what stays physical.”

The question steadied her. Curvature could change apparent load, but momentum remained. The quay still rotated. The spine still held mass. Thrusters still pushed against expelled propellant. Every force had a destination, even when the receiver confused the route.

“Spin gives us point three standard toward the hull,” she said. “Returned curvature currently adds one point eight toward starboard and rising. Conventional thrust can alter the resultant.”

“Control, unlock my port clusters. I can push the dock axis back toward the cargo floors.”

“You will load the clamps past certification.”

“If I release, my stern crosses Deck Two. Unlock them.”

A crash traveled through the structure. The deck jumped. Iona's harness fitting drove into her ribs, and the freight stack nearest the partition moved half a meter despite its webbing.

Control granted Tavin access.

The Morrowline fired one port thruster cluster. Vibration entered Helix Quay through the docking collar, deep enough to feel in teeth. The painted refuge arrows no longer pointed downhill, but the deck became usable again. Workers began moving.

Tavin spoke over the rescue channel. “This buys a vector, not stability. Get everyone out from under suspended mass.”

Iona unhooked herself and checked Nemi. “We need the spine control room.”

Nemi attempted to rise. Her body leaned left while her hands reached right. She caught the rail and vomited between her boots.

“No,” Iona said.

“The pressure trace is on my case.”

“I can carry it.”

“You read omissions. I read intervals.”

“You have a head injury.”

“My head has always been an administrative concern.”

A medic arrived on a tether line. He took one look at Nemi's eye movement and clipped a support frame around her shoulders. “Vestibular trauma. Possible concussion.”

“She needs the case,” Iona said.

“She needs a fixed visual reference.”

Helix Quay offered none. Walls had become slopes. Loose objects continued taking paths that curved as the receiver load changed. The medic unfolded an illuminated bar in front of Nemi and locked it to the frame.

“Keep your eyes here,” he said.

Nemi obeyed for two seconds. Then her gaze dragged toward Iona. “The return had internal modulation. Not just load.”

“Can you isolate it?”

“Not while the room keeps turning through me.”

Iona took the analysis case. “I will bring it back.”

“That promise lacks statistical support.”

“So revise it when I do.”

She left Nemi with the medic and crawled toward the cargo-spine access. Three workers moved with her. One wore a dockmaster's white helmet, cracked above the ear. Another had a burn across one glove. The third carried a manual cutter.

At the access hatch, the deck load increased until Iona could barely lift her head. Tavin adjusted thrust. Pressure eased by a fraction.

“Rhyll,” he said privately, “my port clusters have nine minutes before thermal limit.”

“The receiver room lies two decks inward.”

“Then I need you there in eight.”

“Can you hold the direction?”

“No. I can keep the heavy things choosing approximately the same wall.”

The dockmaster opened the hatch. Beyond it, a ladder descended toward the spine according to paint and extended sideways according to Iona's body. They clipped onto a track and pulled themselves along. Every movement cost more than it should. The returned field pressed blood into unfamiliar places and made her old unease with unmodulated gravity feel childish.

Halfway to Deck One, the repossession changed sign.

Load vanished.

Iona's hands remained clamped on the track while her legs floated away. The dockmaster struck the ceiling. The cutter spun from the third worker's belt, tethered but live, its blade flashing past Iona's face. Farther down the passage, a person without a tether drifted out of an open service bay.

“Tavin, zero load!” Iona called.

“I see it.”

The Morrowline's thrusters cut. For one breath Helix Quay contained no reliable down beyond its slow spin. People, tools, and broken cargo moved along whatever momentum they already possessed.

The untethered worker reached toward the track and missed.

Iona pushed off. She caught his sleeve with her left hand. Their combined motion pulled her tether to full extension. Pain closed around her shoulder. The man struck the passage wall, found a grip, and held her wrist until she could reel them both in.

“Name?” she asked.

“Ors.” His face had gone gray. “Deck Five refrigeration.”

“Clip in, Ors.”

“My kit.”

“It can orbit without you.”

A low surge returned, this time toward the outer hull. They settled against the passage floor in a tangle.

The analysis case had remained attached to Iona's waist. Its screen displayed the field change as a descending curve interrupted by fine pressure pulses. Nemi had been right. Modulation rode inside the returned debt.

Iona opened the crude grammar they had built with Aru-in-Pressure. The cradle could not translate full meaning through a portable unit, but it marked repeated physical relations. Received load. Matched load. Boundary closure. A ratio associated with restoration appeared seven times.

Repossession, Iona thought. Not attack. Not accident. A creditor enforcing equivalence through the only mechanism both sides shared.

She hated the word as soon as it formed. It could make the event sound orderly. Nothing in the screaming public channels felt orderly.

“Receiver access ahead,” the dockmaster said.

They reached Deck One in six minutes. The control room door had warped in its frame. The worker with the cutter anchored herself and opened a narrow seam. Hot air escaped carrying insulation dust.

Inside, two technicians lay strapped beneath the main console. One had a broken forearm. The other kept issuing commands with blood filling one nostril. The receiver core occupied the center of the compartment, nested in superconducting loops and mechanical restraints. Status lights showed every protective interlock engaged.

Iona connected Nemi's case to the diagnostic port.

“Do not write to it,” the conscious technician said.

“I need to alter channel interpretation.”

“That qualifies.”

“The receiver believes the returned pulse belongs to local correction.”

“It has full authentication.”

“From whom?”

“No origin code. The lattice pair itself confirms the debt.”

A machine built to ensure that no force vanished had recognized its own exported quantity. The Olun did not need Republic credentials. They possessed the other side of the account.

Iona opened the settlement rules. Every clearing carried a completion condition: sink acceptance reduced local debt to zero. The returned pulse had reopened eleven days of entries and bound them to Helix Quay's receiver.

“Can we separate the reopened batches?” she asked.

“Not during correction.”

“What if local mass takes part of the load?”

The technician wiped blood from his lip. “You want to settle against the quay.”

“I want the receiver to recognize payment here instead of forcing every g-second through occupied decks.”

“Moving what mass?”

Helix Quay's cargo manifest covered the display. Ore, water, machine housings, habitat shielding, two dismantled reactor blankets, and the replacement flywheel that the Morrowline had delivered after sacrificing its original cargo. Each item already had a destination and owner.

Iona selected the water reserve in the spine tanks. “Pump Tank Three outward while the quay maintains rotation. The mass shift can absorb part of the curvature change.”

“We need that water.”

“We keep the water. We spend pump power and accept a lower spin margin.”

“Tank mounts could fail.”

“They fail if the field keeps cycling.”

Tavin joined the technical channel. “I can take the opposite load through my docking yoke. Pump toward my berth. I'll counter with conventional thrust.”

The dockmaster looked at Iona. “That transfers quay stress into his ship.”

“Yes.”

“How much can it hold?”

Tavin answered. “More than your clamps. Less than optimism.”

They built the sequence in ninety seconds. Helix pumps would move eight thousand tonnes of water outward. The Morrowline would fire along the dock axis and carry the transient through its reinforced cargo yoke. Helix control would release two cargo rings to free spin, sacrificing alignment with their transfer bridges. The receiver would see local curvature work and reduce the unsettled return.

Iona sent the plan to Bex's office for emergency approval.

His system demanded a four-minute structural review.

She canceled the request and signed under her forensic authority.

“Execution on my mark,” she said.

The pumps began. Deep impacts ran through the spine as valves opened under distorted load. Tank Three's water shifted through pipes wider than corridors. On the cargo display, two rings released their drive locks and drifted out of alignment. Transfer bridges tore free at designed breakpoints. Atmosphere plumed from one failed seal, glittering as ice in the external cameras.

“Morrowline burning,” Tavin said.

Conventional thrust pushed against the quay. Iona felt the floor choose a direction and keep it. The receiver's correction curve faltered. Returned debt declined by six percent, then eleven.

A mounting bolt sheared from the core restraint. The cutter worker trapped it beneath her boot before it reached the console.

“Tank Three at fifty,” Helix control reported.

“Port cluster temperature critical,” said Tavin's engineer.

“Keep it lit,” Tavin replied.

The correction fell through thirty percent. Load surged to twice standard and held. Iona's chest compressed against her harness. The technician with the broken arm screamed once, then bit down on his sleeve.

Inside Nemi's pressure trace, the modulation changed. Restoration ratios shortened. Another pattern interrupted them, sharper and repeated. Iona could not translate it, but the shape resembled refusal.

The Olun were not acting with one mind. One pressure-name, or one faction, had returned the debt. Another might be arguing through the same wound.

“Seventy percent water transfer,” control said.

The Morrowline's port thrust flickered.

Tavin swore. “Cluster Three lost an injector. Pella is moving the burn.”

The vector swung. Everyone in the receiver room dropped sideways. Iona struck the console but kept one hand on the settlement command. The debt value rose again.

A second thruster cluster ignited. The yoke groaned through the whole quay.

“Burn restored,” Tavin said. His breathing came hard over the channel. “Finish it.”

Iona committed the last local mass entry.

Tank Three reached its outer limit. The free cargo rings took the changed spin. Helix Quay's receiver registered physical work, fuel expenditure, and permanent loss of two transfer alignments. The reopened debt dropped below correction threshold.

Gravity settled at four-tenths standard toward the hull.

No one moved at first.

Then alarms separated into intelligible problems. Fire on Deck Four. Pressure loss at Bridge Nine. Medical priority in six locations. The receiver channel to Eidos remained open at diagnostic amplitude, but the full return had stopped.

Iona released her harness. Her knees shook when they accepted the lighter load.

“Nemi,” she said over medical channel.

The medic answered. “Conscious. Vestibular injury confirmed. She cannot stand without support.”

“Put her through.”

Nemi's voice arrived thin and irritated. “Did you bring back my case?”

“It is connected to a damaged receiver in Deck One.”

“That sounds like no.”

“You were right about internal modulation.”

“Of course.” A pause followed. “I cannot tell which way my own tongue is moving.”

Iona closed her eyes once. “Stay with the medic.”

“I had already developed a competing plan to wander into vacuum.”

The channel cut as treatment began.

Iona climbed back toward the cargo decks. Emergency crews passed her carrying braces, sealant, and folded stretchers. On Deck Four, a crane carriage had crossed the work zone and crushed three cargo bays. Workers cut survivors out beneath floodlights. The free rings hung visibly out of alignment beyond the hull windows. Helix Quay had survived, but its geometry no longer matched the city it had been that morning.

At Cargo Two, the Morrowline remained docked. Frost covered the overloaded collar. Tavin stood inside the quay-side lock with one hand against the frame while Pella Jor argued with a port engineer about coolant access.

“You spent a thruster cluster,” Iona said.

“An injector.”

“Your engineer said critical temperature.”

“Pella enjoys emotional language.”

Pella looked over. “The captain enjoys owning no repair tools.”

The port engineer scrolled a shortage list across her sleeve: surgical bearings, water membranes, orchard inoculant. Tavin stopped at the names. Thirty-two pallets carrying those same classes of cargo had left his deck during the Day One inversion, jettisoned so the Morrowline would not drive its spine through Helix Quay. The loss had been correct. It had also arrived here as delayed surgery, restricted water repair, and another growing season placed at risk. He signed the salvage trace beside each item before Pella could ask him to move.

Behind them, medics moved Nemi on a stabilized couch. Her eyes stayed fixed on the illuminated bar above her face. Iona walked beside her until the couch entered the hospital lift.

The public observation gallery overlooked Cargo Two. Its safety glass had polarized during the failure, but someone had overridden it. Hundreds of passengers, clerks, vendors, and off-shift workers stood behind the transparent wall. Many held personal recorders. They had watched freight cross the deck, watched people fall toward walls, and watched the Morrowline fire against the station.

A child near the front pressed both palms to the glass. His father held a serving cart by its brake while cups drifted above one bent shelf in Helix Quay's weak remaining load. Farther back, two cargo clerks compared the public field command against a pocket accelerometer. No official explanation had taught them to distrust the deck. Their bodies had done that. Iona saw the change pass through the gallery as people tested handrails, tied loose bags to their wrists, and moved elderly passengers away from the outer windows. They were already building a private safety procedure around an institution that still called the event a fault.

A quay reporter called through the barrier speaker. “Auditor Rhyll, did the receiver fail?”

Iona looked toward Helix control. A ministry notice waited on her screen: Describe as multi-system load fault pending review. Do not discuss sink interaction. Bex's authorization code appeared beneath it.

Another reporter raised her voice. “Why did the public gravity reverse?”

“It did not fail by itself,” Iona said.

Tavin turned toward her but did not interrupt.

“What does that mean?” the first reporter asked.

Iona opened the pressure trace on the gallery display. The descending return curve appeared above the crowd, labeled with eleven days of exported curvature debt.

“Helix Quay sent force through its receiver to a system our government certified empty,” she said. “Today, someone there returned it.”

The gallery became quiet. Personal recorders remained raised.

On Iona's private channel, Bex called. She declined the connection.

Across the public screens of Helix Quay, the word repossession left the forensic record and entered the Republic.

Chapter 8 — The Captain of Stillness

Stillness refused the Morrowline's docking request because the ship approached too gently.

Tavin Serr read the refusal twice, checked Haru Den's vector, and found no error. Their closing speed sat three centimeters per second below the refuge's minimum. Helix Quay had cooked one port injector and warped two docking sensors, so Haru had brought them in with the delicacy of a needle entering damaged cloth.

The refuge did not reward delicacy.

“Add five centimeters,” Tavin said.

Haru looked back from navigation. “Their collar is older than our forward yoke.”

“Their collar expects to feel us arrive.”

“Charming place.”

“Stillness likes commitment.”

Haru gave the bow cluster a breath of conventional thrust. The heavy freighter crossed the minimum and received immediate clearance.

Ahead, Stillness turned against the stars. It had no graceful cylinder, no bright arc of windows, and no receiver field to smooth its awkward mass. The refuge consisted of five inhabited drums mounted around a mined asteroid, joined by trusses thick enough to look excessive until a person understood what they carried. Water tanks and blocks of nickel-iron moved along rails outside the drums. As cargo shifted or a transport docked, the blocks changed position to preserve spin.

Every adjustment remained visible. No force disappeared into a remote system.

Tavin had learned tug work among those rails at seventeen. His first instructor used to tap the external mass diagram before every maneuver and ask where the push would finish. Any student who answered with a coordinate failed. A push finished in a bearing, a ballast block, a plume of exhaust, or somebody's spine.

The Morrowline met the collar hard enough to compress its mechanical buffers. A tremor ran aft through the cargo yoke. Docking clamps closed one by one.

“Contact earned,” Haru said.

“Put it in the captain's praise file.”

“That file rejected the entry for insufficient storage.”

On the engineering display, Pella Jor marked the failed injector for removal. She had slept four hours since Helix Quay and objected to all questions as if they consumed propellant.

Tavin opened the passenger link. “Rhyll, we're docked.”

Iona answered from the converted chart room. “The cradle felt it.”

“It feels load change?”

“Aru perceives acceleration history. Docking may have qualified as an introduction.”

Tavin left Haru with the bridge and crossed the ship. The passage load differed from deck to deck because the Morrowline's compensator remained shut down. Near the bow, Stillness's spin pulled at an angle through the docking collar. Tavin had spent most of his adult life walking through tuned fields that made every floor agree. Now his shoulder kept warning him that the corridor had been installed wrong.

The chart room contained Iona, two portable receiver modules, and the translation cradle Nemi had built before her injury. Nemi herself remained under treatment at Helix Quay. Her absence showed in a row of labels written too neatly and an exposed connector no one had insulted into obedience.

Aru-in-Pressure had no body inside the room. The speaker existed across layered ice at Eidos, assembled from pressure histories and temporary agreement among lineages. The cradle gave that distant coherence a narrow way to modulate local acceleration. Three suspended beads moved inside a sealed glass tube. A low speaker rendered pressure ratios into human language after delays that varied with complexity.

One bead shifted when Tavin entered.

“Is that recognition?” he asked.

Iona watched the trace. “It could be your mass changing the room.”

The speaker clicked twice. “Difference retained.”

Tavin stopped beside the table. “I'll take recognition.”

“Translation remains approximate,” Iona said.

“So does docking.”

She closed a data pane. Helix casualty reports remained visible beneath it. Nine dead, more than two hundred injured, three still missing inside a crushed freight bay. Nemi's vestibular damage carried a guarded prognosis. The quay had lost two cargo-ring alignments and enough water-pump life to require replacement within a year.

Stillness control called them to wait for ballast transfer before opening the collar. The refuge had accepted the Morrowline's mass, but the nearest inhabited drum now ran beyond its preferred bearing load. External cranes began moving nickel-iron blocks outward and opposite the dock.

On a camera feed, three suited workers guided a block as large as a house along a truss. Their rail motors crawled. Frost smoked from mechanical brakes. The operation would take twenty-three minutes.

“At Helix, a receiver would have trimmed this in a second,” Iona said.

“And sent the difference somewhere else.”

“Here they spend labor.”

“Labor, brake surface, and time.” Tavin pointed to the heat graph. “Also radiator margin. They cannot move another major block until those brakes cool.”

Aru's middle bead descended. “Local body records push.”

Tavin looked at the glass tube. “That's the whole town.”

“Town?” the cradle asked.

“A human pressure lineage with invoices,” Iona said.

The speaker attempted her phrase, failed on lineage, and produced a low sequence that made Tavin's molars ache.

Once the ballast locked, the docking collar opened. Dry air entered carrying machine oil and cooked grain. Stillness had never possessed enough imported volatiles to waste water on humidity. Its corridors showed patched pressure panels, exposed cables, and handrails polished by generations of gloves. Nothing floated loose. Every cup had a lid. Every tool had two clips.

The dockmaster met them without ceremony. Her left leg ended below the knee in a work prosthesis built from tug components. She looked past Tavin at the ship status displayed over the collar.

“You arrived with one dead port injector.”

“Temporarily dead.”

“Pella still working for you?”

“Against her judgment.”

“Then temporarily.” The dockmaster glanced at Iona's ministry credentials. “We have no receiver for your auditors to certify.”

“That's why we came,” Iona said.

“People with receivers say that whenever their machines frighten them.”

She led them inward. Tavin remembered the route as wider. At seventeen he had carried tool cases through these corridors and believed any place containing a dock led to the whole Republic. Now pipes crowded his shoulders. Repairs lay over repairs in visible strata. A pressure panel carried five dates in different paints.

Stillness's market occupied the hubward end of Drum Two, where spin produced a light but reliable down. Stalls sold algae bread, recast machine parts, sealed fruit paste, and cloth made from fungal fiber. Fresh produce occupied one table under a locking cover. Six bruised pears cost more than a thruster technician earned in a shift.

Children ran along a curved wall until their feet climbed above Tavin's head. They understood the drum's changing vector without looking. An old man moved water containers on a handcart balanced to the kilogram. When Tavin stepped aside for him, the cart's counterweight shifted along its axle.

Iona watched the mechanism. “How often do bearings fail?”

“Main drum bearings? One every three years,” the dockmaster said. “We manufacture replacements here.”

“With that metal stock?”

“With whatever arrives. The tolerances make engineers religious.”

Tavin shook his head. “They make engineers thieves. Religion comes after inventory.”

The dockmaster's mouth moved toward a smile but stopped. “You still owe us two carbide cutters.”

“I was nineteen.”

“Debt matures.”

Aru's portable cradle traveled between Iona and Tavin on a rigid frame. The suspended beads responded to each change in spin. At the market hub, all three settled close to the tube's center.

“Down made by rotation,” the speaker said.

“Yes,” Iona replied. “No dark-lattice export.”

“Pressure remains near source.”

“Every correction here has a local destination,” Tavin said.

The dockmaster heard the translated voice for the first time. She did not step back. “Is that someone from the sink?”

“One temporary speaker,” Iona said. “Aru-in-Pressure.”

“Temporary as in shift work?”

The beads moved in three different directions. Translation took eleven seconds.

“Temporary as agreement under changing load.”

The dockmaster considered this. “That also describes shift work.”

They continued to the refuge's balance room, but the dockmaster took a service bridge around Drum Six first. The corridor ended at a pressure door with no handle on the inhabited side. Beyond its inspection glass, an unfinished cylinder curved into darkness. Bare ribs divided the interior. Capped air pipes projected from the hub. A painted line marked where soil had once been planned.

“We built the shell fifty-three years ago,” the dockmaster said. “Then Drum Three needed new fly masses. The metal came from here.”

Tavin remembered hauling one of those masses as an apprentice. For six days, his tug crew had worried a section of Drum Six's outer wall free, shifted it through the truss, and machined its edges into a rail block. He had thought the work clever. It had never occurred to him that the dark cylinder had been promised to families.

“How many berths?” Iona asked.

“Eighty thousand.”

“Could you complete it now?”

“With imported structure, four reactor units, and enough water to make the farms useful. We have requested all three for thirty-one years.”

Tavin put one hand against the pressure door. The metal carried a faint periodic tremor from the inhabited drums. Stillness had not escaped cost by refusing a receiver. It had converted cost into rooms never filled, children who left, and machinery kept alive past sane retirement.

Aru's cradle responded with a long pressure descent.

“Absent human pattern?” the speaker asked.

“People planned to live there,” Iona said. “They never did.”

“Ended before forming?”

“Not ended,” the dockmaster replied. “Deferred. Some went elsewhere.”

“Some were never born,” Tavin said.

The dockmaster faced him. “Do not turn our empty drum into a funeral. We chose bearings over expansion because inhabited rooms came first.”

“I know.”

“No, you remember leaving.”
