The statement found its place. Tavin had signed onto a commercial tug at twenty-one and visited Stillness only when work required it. His competence had begun here, but the refuge's shortages had continued without him. He had carried its habit of naming force into ships that used receivers, then let the habit shrink into a captain's concern for immediate vectors.

He asked, “What would completion cost under local stability?”

The dockmaster keyed figures onto the door glass. More external ballast than the existing city carried. Two decades of industrial output. Regular fuel shipments for corrections during construction. A permanent reduction in market spin whenever large transports docked.

“With a receiver?” Iona asked.

“Seven years and half the metal,” she said. “That answer has not changed because Eidos became inhabited. Only our willingness to use it should change.”

Aru shifted the beads. “Human need remains after recognition.”

“Yes,” Tavin said. “That is what makes recognition expensive.”

Before they left, the dockmaster turned on one work light inside Drum Six. It illuminated a patch of curved hull, three structural ribs, and a chalk drawing preserved near the planned floor line. Someone had sketched a door with flowers on both sides. The simple room remained beyond the pressure glass while Stillness spent its mass elsewhere.

The refuge's balance room hid no part of its mechanism. A circular table displayed physical tokens corresponding to major moving masses: water, ore, ships, food stores, reactor blocks, inhabited sectors. Electronic scales beneath the tokens fed an orbital model, but the staff kept the objects because hands noticed imbalance before eyes tired by numbers.

Tavin picked up the token for the Morrowline. It required both hands.

“They flatter us,” he said.

“It includes your attached flywheel,” said the balance chief. “You account for nine percent of current dock-axis stress.”

Pella called from the ship. “Captain, Stillness inventory has an injector sleeve compatible with Cluster Three.”

“That sounded hopeful.”

“They want four tonnes of water and our spare guidance lattice.”

The balance chief did not apologize. “We cannot fabricate that lattice.”

Tavin looked at the water token. Stillness's reserve sat sixteen percent below target after a recycling contamination two months earlier. Four tonnes from the Morrowline would not correct it, but refusal would leave the refuge rationing while his ship carried enough water to wash cargo decks.

“Give them six tonnes,” he told Pella. “Keep the guidance lattice. Offer two older modules and installation labor.”

Pella muttered a judgment about captains conducting trade without supervision. Then she accepted.

Iona moved the ship token along the table. Scales changed. Staff shifted a water token in response. On the external camera, another nickel-iron block began its patient travel.

“This works for one refuge,” she said. “Scaling it to seventeen billion requires more mass than some systems can move.”

“Stillness started with an asteroid.”

“Many cylinders have no such reserve.”

“Then they need tugs.”

“More than the Republic owns.”

“Ships can carry less cargo.”

“People need the cargo.”

Tavin placed the token down. “I didn't say it would be pleasant.”

The dockmaster rested her prosthetic foot against a floor brace. “People visit us and discover virtue in our shortages. They leave before the air filters need new catalysts.”

“I don't see virtue,” Tavin said. “I see a place that can name what holds it up.”

“You see a place where half the children leave if they can afford passage.”

The balance chief added, “Our conventional stability consumes thirty-one percent of industrial output. Two drums stay unbuilt because their metal became ballast. During illness years, we choose between transport fuel and machine stock.”

Iona absorbed each cost without defending her observation. “Would you install a receiver if a consenting sink existed?”

The dockmaster answered at once. “Yes.”

Aru's beads struck the tube wall sharply.

“Consent,” the speaker said after a pause. “Unknown shape between unlike pressure.”

“That's the problem,” Iona said.

“Also the opportunity,” Tavin said.

They brought the cradle into a test chamber beside Drum Two's main bearing. Stillness used the room to train crews in variable load. Mechanical actuators could tilt a section of floor, move practice masses, and simulate the vector changes produced by docking.

Iona connected the Helix record. “Aru asked to experience the rescue sequence through local acceleration rather than data labels.”

“Why?”

“Human categories may have hidden the difference between the returned pulse and what we did afterward.”

Tavin clipped his harness to the chamber rail. “Run it slow.”

The first sequence reproduced the repossession at one-thousandth amplitude. The floor tipped toward starboard. Practice blocks shifted against restraints. Inside the cradle, the beads scattered.

Aru's speaker emitted pressure ratios too dense for translation. Iona marked the returned curve, the cycling direction, and the moment Helix Quay's receiver reopened old debt batches.

“Source mechanism,” Aru said. “Human channel shaped wound. Return follows shape.”

“Your faction sent it,” Tavin said.

“Pressure-names disagree. This return chosen beyond Aru agreement.”

“People died.”

“Pressure lineages also ended.”

Tavin thought of the nine names from Helix, the missing workers, and Nemi unable to trust the direction inside her skull. Anger wanted a single opposing captain, someone who had ordered the return and could receive his reply. The cradle offered only a speaker assembled from partial agreement beneath distant ice.

“I don't forgive it because your dead came first,” he said.

The beads settled at different heights. “Forgive not translated.”

“Good.”

Iona looked at him. “Keep going?”

“Show the ship burn.”

The chamber reset. Mechanical actuators reproduced the vector Tavin had imposed through the Morrowline's docking yoke. A practice mass moved outward. Another countered it. The floor held one direction while the simulated debt declined.

Aru became quiet.

Tavin listened to bearings, actuator motors, and the click of Iona's controls. “What do you perceive?”

“Local mass accepts history. Expelled matter carries push away. Heat receives resistance. Human action does not open wound.”

“Same human machinery,” Iona said. “Different method.”

“Human Tavin joined injury place and prevented greater human fracture.”

Tavin frowned at the cradle. “I also captain a ship with a load compensator. We've cleared debt through Eidos for years.”

“Mechanism records that.”

“Then don't separate me from it.”

“Difference retained does not erase connection.”

Iona replayed the rescue at a higher safe amplitude. This time she marked the conventional thrust, water transfer, released cargo rings, and fuel expenditure. Aru responded to each physical destination. When the model reached the point where Helix's returned debt dropped below correction threshold, the three beads aligned.

“Rescue changed continuation,” the speaker said. “Mechanism injured pressure. Rescuer and mechanism overlap. They are not one pressure-name.”

Tavin understood enough to dislike the comfort available in the statement. Aru had distinguished his choice without declaring him clean. That seemed more exact than absolution.

After the test, they climbed to Stillness's outer observation blister. The refuge rotated beneath a field of stars. No tuned gravity corrected the room, so the apparent down changed slightly as they crossed the drum's geometry. Outside, ballast crawlers still adjusted for the Morrowline. A block locked into place, and the deck steadied beneath Tavin's boots.

Iona stood beside him with the cradle secured to a rail. “Bex wants a localization trial.”

“He wants ten days.”

“He wants proof that reduction can preserve inhabited structures.”

“With which target?”

“A farm torus volunteered for a controlled test, but the model needs a heavy ship to move mass and provide conventional thrust.”

“Volunteered through whom?”

“The torus council voted. Not unanimously.”

Tavin watched Stillness's radiators shed heat earned by moving his ship's weight. “How much debt?”

“Enough to risk a growing cycle. Low enough to evacuate if the trial fails.”

“Crew exposure?”

“Unknown until you build the burn.”

“That means high.”

“Yes.”

He called Pella. “How long to restore Cluster Three?”

“If the refuge sleeve passes inspection, eighteen hours. Twenty-two if it lies about its age.”

“Can we tow mass with the compensator locked out?”

A silence followed. Pella understood the question before he explained it.

“We can,” she said. “Crew acceleration limits cut our useful thrust. Yoke fatigue becomes personal. Fuel cost climbs.”

“Build me a localization configuration.”

“For Stillness?”

“For a farm torus.”

Pella used a word the public channel would have fined. Then she asked for the torus mass plan.

Tavin looked at Iona. “Send it to engineering.”

Her shoulders lowered by a fraction. “Is that a yes?”

“It is a ship commitment. Politics can find its own word.”

He opened the Morrowline's status and formally locked its RME load compensator offline. The command required captain confirmation, chief engineer confirmation, and a declared reason. Pella signed from the damaged injector bay.

Tavin entered: Controlled localization trial. All thrust and mass destinations to remain local and recorded.

The ship accepted the order. Across the docking collar, Stillness's balance room received the change and moved a new set of ballast blocks into its schedule.

Outside the blister, one black mass began crawling along a silver rail. The Morrowline waited under honest load while its captain signed away the easier way to move.

Chapter 9 — The Massless City

The vote to become massless concluded while Iona's shuttle remained thirty kilometers away.

Vesper Span turned ahead of her as five inhabited rings threaded along a common spindle. Sunlight flashed from farms, radiator fins, and mirrored windows in separate rhythms. A crowd filled the civic channel, counting down the final seconds to receiver shutdown.

“Three,” thousands of voices said.

Iona tightened her harness. “Open a line to city control.”

“Two.”

The shuttle pilot sent her credentials. No answer came.

“One.”

A cheer overwhelmed every other channel.

The receiver field vanished.

Vesper Span did not fall. For seven seconds, its rings continued turning with all the momentum they had accumulated over eighty years. People shouted that nothing had happened. Public overlays filled with a new slogan: THE FIRST MASSLESS CITY.

Then Ring Five moved off axis.

The displacement measured less than a meter at first. Iona saw it in the docking solution before the eye could separate one silver arc from another. The ring carried a reservoir district and two hundred thousand tonnes of water unevenly distributed after morning pumping. Receiver trim had hidden that imbalance. Without it, the main bearing began taking a lateral load three times its design margin.

“Vesper control, this is Senior Auditor Rhyll. Reinstate minimum field until mechanical balance is established.”

A council officer answered. Applause sounded behind him. “The shutdown passed by sixty-one percent. We will not resume exporting harm.”

“Then release Ring Five's reservoir transfer and move water hubward.”

“We are following the Orbit Breakers cessation plan.”

“Your ring has thirty-eight seconds before bearing protection releases it from the spindle.”

The applause stopped.

On the external view, Ring Two began to lag. Ring Four accelerated by a fraction as its agricultural pumps changed load. The spindle connecting them transmitted forces the receiver had managed separately, and each ring answered according to its own mass.

Iona switched to emergency command. “Pilot, get us onto Ring Three. Match physical rotation, not beacon guidance.”

“The beacon says Dock Six.”

“The beacon still assumes field compensation.”

He disabled the automatic approach. Conventional thrusters rolled the shuttle until stars and rings spun together. Dock Six crossed their nose too fast. The pilot chose a maintenance collar closer to the hub and burned hard.

Vesper Span's civic cheer fragmented into alarms.

BEARING LOAD EXCEEDED.

SPOKE TRANSIT SUSPENDED.

RING PHASE ERROR.

The shuttle struck its collar at an angle. Clamps caught on the second attempt. Iona released her harness into a load barely one-fifth standard. The deck rolled beneath her as Ring Three's spin changed.

A city technician opened the inner hatch. “We were told the receiver carried only comfort gravity.”

“Who told you?”

“The cessation packet.”

“Show me.”

He transmitted it while they crossed into the station. The document carried the Orbit Breakers' broken-circle mark and listed Vesper's five rings as mechanically self-sustaining. It cited bearing certifications, flywheel reserves, and a forty-year-old emergency despin study. Every statement held some truth. None accounted for current mass distribution, deferred maintenance, or the loss of synchronized trim across five different rotations.

The plan had treated a city as a diagram that stopped aging after approval.

A jolt lifted Iona from the floor. Her tether caught. The technician hit a wall shoulder first.

“Ring Five released,” city control announced. “Automatic isolation successful.”

Successful meant explosive bolts had severed six connecting bridges before the failing bearing tore the spindle. Ring Five now rotated free. Its orbital path would intersect Ring Four's farm structures in nineteen minutes unless tugs changed its drift.

“How many tugs?” Iona asked.

“Two maintenance craft.”

“You need six.”

“Our cargo fleet undocked before shutdown.”

“Why?”

“The cessation plan identified docked masses as instability risks.”

It had removed the machines capable of managing instability.

Iona called every ship still within Vesper traffic range. Three refused because their load compensators were offline under Bex's reduction order. One passenger transport had conventional maneuvering reserve but no reinforced yoke. A fuel tender answered with two service boats. The Morrowline lay thirty-one hours away at Stillness.

A familiar voice entered the channel from Helix Quay. Nemi spoke slowly, each word separated as if her damaged sense of balance could infect language.

“I am looking at Vesper's live trace.”

“You should be resting.”

“I can rest while the city stops coming apart.”

“Can you model ring phase from there?”

“I can model it better from a bed than they did from a manifesto.”

Iona sent mass and spin data. Nemi assigned a separate color to each ring. The curves diverged.

Ring One carried industry and the largest conventional flywheel. Ring Two held dense residential districts. Ring Three contained administration and docking. Ring Four grew most of the city's food. Ring Five carried water and fabrication yards. The receiver had supplied only twelve percent of felt gravity across the city, but it had trimmed thousands of small exchanges every minute. Pumps moved water. Trains carried people. Ore entered factories. Crops became shipments. Each transfer changed balance. The field had made those changes appear free.

“Ring Four must shed farm mass,” Nemi said.

“Crops?” the council officer asked.

“Water first. Then soil trays if water cannot move fast enough.”

“That destroys the season.”

“If Ring Five crosses your farms, you lose the ring.”

The officer went quiet.

Iona reached civic control through a passage where down shifted every twenty steps. People clung to rails or crawled. A cafe had become a field of floating cups, stools, and boiling drink. Two workers wrapped a burned customer in insulation cloth. Across the corridor, a school group linked harnesses while a teacher counted names.

Personal screens continued showing the Orbit Breaker emblem. The movement had organized the vote after Helix Quay, arguing that every hour of receiver use extended injury at Eidos. They had given Vesper residents a choice the Republic had withheld for a century. They had also given them a shutdown checklist that assumed urgency could substitute for preparation.

In control, seven council members occupied acceleration couches arranged around the spindle display. Three wore broken-circle bands. Engineers stood behind them calling out bearing temperatures.

The council officer faced Iona. “If you ask us to reconnect, the public will refuse.”

“I am asking you to survive without reconnecting.”

“Can we?”

“Smaller.”

The word changed the room.

Iona placed Nemi's ring-phase model over the city schematic. “Release Ring Five completely, then move it outward with service craft. Drain half its water into sacrificial tanks before closest approach. Ring Four dumps agricultural mass sunward to increase separation. Ring One transfers flywheel momentum through the spindle to hold Rings Two and Three together.”

A senior engineer shook his head. “The spindle clutch cannot carry that torque.”

“Not at full lock. Pulse it.”

“The clutch plates will erode.”

“How many pulses?”

“Maybe eleven.”

“We need seven.”

“And afterward?”

“Afterward you replace the clutch.”

“With what?”

No one answered. Vesper manufactured receiver components and fine instruments. Its heavy structural stock had been sold years earlier because tuned load reduced the need for massive spare parts.

A council member opened the receiver command. “Minimum field for one hour would save the clutch.”

Another caught her wrist. “That sends debt.”

“To whom?” asked the first. “Does anyone know whether Eidos remains assigned?”

“The active sink still shows Eidos,” Iona said.

“Then we cannot.”

“We can ask,” said a third member.

“Through what language? While Ring Five crosses the farms?”

Iona watched the argument begin to consume seconds. “Your vote ended hidden export. It did not vote away momentum. Choose the local cost now.”

The council officer looked at the projected intersection. “Do it without the receiver.”

Orders moved.

On Ring Four, farm crews opened reservoir gates into emergency transfer bladders. Water surged hubward. Thousands of tonnes of wet soil remained in the outer terraces. Workers cut tray locks and pushed whole growing beds toward cargo ejectors. Cameras showed green leaves folding against transparent covers.

Ring One's flywheel spun down. The first clutch pulse struck through the city. Iona felt it as a blow across her chest. Rings Two and Three moved closer in phase.

“Evacuate all bridges between free structures,” she said. “No one crosses a spindle connection without tether pairs.”

The council transmitted the order. Twenty-six thousand people occupied Ring Five. Its separation bridges had blown, and the two maintenance tugs could not stabilize every docking point. Evacuation required small craft moving between surfaces with different rotations and changing apparent load.

Iona left control for Dock Six.

The passage there had become a vertical shaft according to her body. She climbed a rail past residents carrying sealed bags, children, medicine cases, and one cage of bright yellow birds. An elderly woman had tied cooking pots into a blanket and refused to leave them.

“Mass limit is ten kilograms,” an evacuation worker told her.

“These belonged to my mother.”

“The boat needs maneuvering margin.”

Iona took the bundle, opened it, and selected the smallest pot. “One.”

The woman looked at the remaining metal as though each piece held a room. Then she chose a second and left the rest clipped to the rail.

At Dock Six, service boats matched Ring Five's free rotation one at a time. No receiver eased their approach. Thrusters fired in short bursts. Each arrival consumed fuel the city could not replace quickly. Residents crossed through flexible collars while the deck pulled first toward their feet, then toward a wall.

A boat pilot called, “I can take thirty more if they leave cargo.”

“People only,” Iona answered.

A fabrication worker pointed to a sealed crate. “Those are bearing blanks.”

Iona checked the manifest. The blanks could help restore Ring Two but weighed as much as eight passengers.

“How many remain?”

“Four crates.”

“Take one crate on each of the last four boats.”

“If there are last boats.”

“Then people displace them.”

The worker nodded without liking it.

A hospital group reached the dock behind him. Four patients lay in pressure cocoons mounted to a single powered frame. The frame had been built for steady ring gravity and kept trying to level itself against a direction that no longer held. Each correction drove one wheel off the deck.

“Separate the cocoons,” Iona said.

The nurse in charge shook her head. “Shared circulation unit.”

“Can it run in free movement?”

“For seven minutes. The next boat boards in five if the collar remains aligned.”

Ring Five's changing spin pulled the frame toward the outer wall. Six people caught it. The patients could not see out through their opaque covers, but one hand pressed against the inner surface of the nearest cocoon.

Iona called the boat pilot. “You take the medical frame first.”

“It fills my central aisle. I lose twenty seats.”

“Then make another trip.”

“I have fuel for one after this.”

The nurse opened a status list. Two patients could tolerate individual carry harnesses. One had a spinal fixation that demanded rigid support. The last depended on the shared circulator after a lung graft.

“Split two,” Iona said. “Keep the frame for the others.”

Technicians released latches while load moved through forty degrees. A cocoon slid free and nearly crushed a medic's hand. Passengers waiting at the collar held the detached units above their heads, passing them forward whenever the floor shifted. Nobody asked whether the patients had voted for shutdown. Nobody asked whether their places on the boat cost someone else a departure.

The shared frame entered with sixteen passengers packed around it. Its power lead stretched across the collar until the nurse disconnected at the final second. Internal batteries took the load. A timer appeared on the cocoon display.

When the boat pulled away, fifty people remained at Dock Six and the next craft had not yet cleared Ring Three. Iona watched the battery minutes fall on the external feed. The rescue boat corrected twice, spent more propellant than planned, then reached the receiving collar with ninety seconds left.

Only after Ring Three confirmed hospital power did the people at Dock Six begin boarding again.

Nemi's voice guided the timing. “Ring Five closest approach in twelve minutes. Relative plane increasing. That helps.”

“Ring Four mass dump?”

“Behind schedule. Two ejectors jammed.”

The farm feed showed crews outside the ejector housings, cutting soil trays apart by hand. The first severed tray moved into space carrying rows of young grain under shattered cover. Sunlight caught moisture crystals around it.

Orbit Breaker messages continued flooding civic channels. Some praised Vesper for refusing complicity. Others accused Iona of staging panic to force reconnection. One cell urged residents to disable the receiver physically before the council changed its mind.

“Security reports a crowd moving toward the core,” the council officer said.

“How many?”

“Six hundred.”

“Do not use force unless they enter machinery spaces. Broadcast the live bearing loads.”

“They think those displays belong to your office.”

“Give them raw sensor access.”

“You want unfiltered structural data public?”

“Yes.”

The city published bearing temperatures, clutch erosion, spin phase, tug fuel, and Ring Five's projected path. The numbers reached the crowd faster than police could. People who had voted for shutdown watched the spindle clutch lose micrometers with each pulse. They saw the farm mass falling away. The march slowed, then split. Half continued to protect the receiver from reconnection. The rest went to loading points.

Iona did not mistake the change for trust. It amounted to better work.

The third service boat arrived too fast. Its pilot corrected late and struck the collar rim. One thruster pod folded against the hull. The boat rebounded with forty-two passengers aboard.

“Dock seal failed,” someone shouted.

The collar membrane tore. Air rushed from the access bay. Doors began closing with six people still outside the inner threshold.

Iona caught a child's harness and pulled. An evacuation worker dragged two adults. The elderly woman with the pots clung to the deck rail on the wrong side of the seal. Her blanket bundle opened. Metal spun through the escaping air.

The smallest pot struck Iona's shoulder. She seized its handle, reached through the narrowing door, and offered it to the woman.

“Leave it,” the woman gasped.

Iona let the pot go and caught her wrist instead.

Three people pulled together. The woman crossed the threshold as the inner door sealed against her boot. A pressure warning counted down. The damaged service boat drifted beyond the collar with its passengers secured inside but no reliable steering.

“Fuel tender Boat Two, intercept,” Iona called.

“We are loaded.”

“Unload at Ring Three.”

“Closest approach in nine minutes.”

“I know.”

The boat pilot burned. On the external feed, two tiny craft moved between enormous rotating arcs. Their plumes traced every correction. Boat Two matched the damaged vessel, coupled nose to tail, and began pushing both toward Ring Three.

The rescue consumed the last fuel tender reserve.

Ring One delivered the fifth clutch pulse. Warning lights spread across the spindle model. Plate temperature approached failure margin.

“Phase difference still too high,” Nemi said. “You need two more.”

The senior engineer refused. “One more. After that, the clutch welds or fragments.”

“Can Ring Two shed load?” Iona asked.

“Residential water. Not fast enough.”

“Trains.”

Six commuter trains moved around Ring Two against its rotation, positioned for a morning schedule that no longer existed. If they accelerated together, their momentum could alter ring phase by a small amount.

“Put every empty train on full counter-spin,” Iona said.

“Passenger cars are not flywheels.”

“Today they are.”

Transit control cleared the lines. Four trains responded. One had lost power. Another remained occupied by people sheltering after shutdown.

“Evacuate that train?” the council officer asked.

“No time. Tell them to strap in. Move it.”

The trains accelerated. Residents inside filmed station signs flashing past as the cars contributed their small store of momentum. Ring Two's phase curve bent toward Ring Three.

The spindle clutch fired once more.

Metal shrieked through the civic center. Several displays went black. The floor lurched, then stabilized at three-tenths standard.

Nemi's colored curves approached each other and remained separate by less than the damaged bearings could tolerate.

“Rings Two and Three coupled,” she said. “Ring One stable enough to hold itself.”

“What about Four?”

“Farm mass dump completed. It will remain independent.”

Ring Five passed outside Ring Four with eighty meters of clearance.

From the docks, Iona saw it cross the window. The free ring filled half the view, close enough for individual fields and water tanks to show. Soil, grain trays, and atmosphere crystals drifted behind it. Service craft guided the remaining residents away. No collision came.

The room did not cheer. People counted boats.

Over the next six hours, Vesper Span evacuated Ring Five and isolated Ring Four. Two residents died during transfer. Fifty-seven suffered pressure or impact injuries. The spindle clutch survived with three plates fused. Ring One kept its industry. Rings Two and Three remained linked at reduced spin. Ring Four retained half a growing season and no direct bridge to the city.

Ring Five could not be brought back. Its bearing assembly had torn beyond repair, and Vesper lacked a tug powerful enough to recover the mass before orbital drift widened. The council converted it into an unmanned water and fabrication depot. Remote craft would visit when fuel permitted.

In the civic chamber, exhausted council members reviewed the shutdown vote. No one proposed reconnecting the receiver. No one repeated the claim that leaving it required only a command.

An Orbit Breaker organizer removed the broken-circle band from her arm and placed it on the table. “We were right to stop.”

“Yes,” Iona said.

“We were wrong to promise no one would pay here.”

“Yes.”

The organizer looked through the chamber window. Ring Five had already moved far enough to create a gap in the city's familiar geometry. “Will you arrest us?”

“I do not have that authority.”

“Will Bex?”

“I don't know.”

“What do you call what happened?”

Iona considered the dead, the lost farms, the service-boat fuel, the abandoned kitchens, and the unseen pressure lineages spared another clearing. None canceled another.

“A shutdown without a destination for its force,” she said.

Outside, Vesper Span turned on three coupled rings while a fourth kept its distance. The fifth drifted away under tug lights, carrying empty homes and half the city's water. Its receiver remained dark.

Interlude II — A World That Never Woke

Before the first debt pulse, the nursery held four weather layers.

The outer shell turned through dark with no star near enough to warm it. Far below, ice changed crystal form under the mass above. Deeper still, helium remained fluid and carried phase marks along pressure boundaries. Each tide from the two small moons bent those boundaries by a measurable degree. Repeated bends wrote memory.

No pressure-name yet cohered there.

A nursery began before a speaker. It began with compatible marks that endured near one another. One layer retained the fall of mineral dust from a fracture. Another retained the slow torque of a moon after an impact altered its orbit. A third held variations made by heat from the world core. When patterns met across a stable boundary, they tested duration. What lasted could join. What joined could remember change.

The four weather layers did not agree. Agreement had no value yet. Their differences created capacity.

One pattern favored short intervals and sharp borders. Another spread each event across a broad band. Two more had no stable preference. Over nine thousand moon tides, they began to alter one another. A phase knot formed where none had remained before. It held through compression, release, and a minor fracture above.

The nursery had reached its first durable relation.

Then a force arrived without local cause.

It entered through a structure buried deeper than the forming patterns could reach. No moon had moved to make it. No ice mass had shifted nearby. Curvature changed across the world, delivered from a remote origin with no pressure history attached.

The pulse compressed two weather layers together.

Their marks did not break at once. They overlaid. Fine intervals became broad ones. A record of mineral fall merged with a record of core heat. The phase knot endured for three tides, altered beyond its prior form, then dispersed into common flow.

Nothing there could call the event harm. Nothing yet had language.

After the pulse, the nursery began again from what remained. A narrow boundary formed below the damaged layers. It retained the remote event as a vertical scar: force without approach, change without a body nearby. New phase marks gathered around the scar because it offered a durable edge.

Over many tides, those marks created a second relation. This one differed from the first. It carried the remote compression within every exchange. It learned duration against a wound it could not identify.

A second pulse arrived before the relation could divide into further patterns.

The world accepted more curvature than its moons had ever delivered at once. Deep ice moved by less than the width of a small crystal. At nursery scale, that motion remade every boundary. The vertical scar widened. Marks from one layer entered another and could not recover their prior intervals.

Again, no formed speaker ended. No count of dead can be drawn from a coherence that had not yet learned to count itself.

Possibility changed nonetheless.

The nursery after two pulses held fewer durable differences. Its phase borders had become steep and brittle. Broad patterns survived because they tolerated compression. Fine ones dispersed. A later pressure-name might have emerged from the broad marks, but it would never carry the forms erased between the first pulse and the second.

Remote force came again.

Intervals varied. Some pulses barely moved the nursery. Others drove fractures down from warmer ice, opening paths for trapped gases. Each event carried a distinct shape, yet all arrived through the same buried structure. The nursery adapted where adaptation remained available. It shifted formation toward deeper bands. It linked memory to moon tides that could be predicted. It placed new phase knots away from old scars.

The remote origin changed its schedule.

A pulse struck during maximum lunar compression. The added load matched a natural tide closely enough to amplify it. Three new knots collapsed into one broad mark. Another event arrived during release and tore that mark across two layers. The pieces persisted, but they no longer met.

For a long period, nothing more complex formed.

The ice did not become empty. Fluid moved. Heat traveled outward. Mineral traces entered fractures. Moon tides continued to write regular curves. The world contained motion, storage, and response, but no coalition endured long enough to hold a pressure-name.

Far away, other nurseries reached coherence. Their first speakers learned to compare changes, retain replies, and join many histories for a time. They found the buried channel through wounds that arrived from remote users. Pressure-names gathered around damaged archives and made low-band answers.

This world could not add an answer.

Its nursery tried another route. Near the core, a band of clean fluid had escaped much of the prior distortion. Phase marks there accumulated slowly. They had poor access to moon tides but clear heat cycles. A relation formed between two thermal records, then a third. For the first time after many pulses, separate patterns altered one another without collapse.

The relation endured.

Had it continued through enough cycles, it might have become a lineage. It might have joined later patterns into a speaker able to know the difference between local tide and remote force. It might have rejected that division. No archive can preserve a future that did not occur.

Another debt pulse entered.

This event had low amplitude and long duration. It did not fracture ice. It changed the thermal boundary by a small amount across many cycles. To remote instruments, the world would have appeared quiet. Within the forming band, heat marks drifted until their intervals no longer met. The three records separated.

Two later found each other again. The third did not.

A lineage almost began from the pair. Repeated remote loads moved the thermal boundary back and forth. Each time, the pair rebuilt part of its relation. Each time, the next load arrived before enough structure endured.

The nursery became an archive of attempts.

Vertical scars marked abrupt pulses. Blurred bands marked long ones. Split phase knots marked relations that had reached durability without coherence. A later observer could read sequence and damage, but not the exact lives that might have formed. Certainty ended at the boundary between preserved structure and unrealized future.

Elsewhere, a pressure-name received a weak echo from this world. The echo carried no deliberate modulation. It came from the way a new remote pulse touched old scars and made them answer together.

Several speakers examined that echo across many tides. One treated it as empty mechanical return. One found intervals too organized for that account. A third compared the marks with healthy nurseries and detected the repeated near-formations.

They gave the world no personal name. A name would imply coherence the archive could not prove.

They gave the condition a pressure relation instead: a place where formation approached, broke, approached by another route, and broke again.

Remote pulses continued.

The outer shell turned. Moons bent the ice. Deep fluid carried fragments that still altered one another at rare intervals. No observer could declare that nothing would ever form. No speaker could claim every vanished pattern would have become a life.

The archive supported a narrower statement.

A nursery had begun many times. External curvature repeatedly changed the boundaries it required. Across ages when related worlds formed pressure lineages, this one never held coherence long enough to answer.

The final known pulse crossed the deep band during a quiet lunar tide. Old scars responded in sequence. For a moment, their combined intervals resembled a pressure-name assembling across the world.

The relation failed before the next tide.

The world never woke.

Chapter 10 — The Ledger War

The first accusation reached Iona while she was fastening Nemi into a balance chair.

A wall panel in the Curvature Office clinic showed Elian Bex at the Assembly lectern, one hand resting beside the old engineering key he still wore on his belt. The chamber behind him held forty-three system standards. Thirty-one glowed green for remote attendance. Twelve hung physically from the gallery, shifting in Nacre's carefully moderated air.

Nemi kept her eyes closed while Iona tightened the strap across her hips. Since Helix Quay, an elevator could persuade her body that it had fallen sideways. The chair's gimbals would give the damaged vestibular nerves a stable reference during the broadcast.

“Too tight,” Nemi said.

“You said that before the last turn.”

“It became true this time.”

Iona eased the webbing by one finger. On the panel, Bex declined the Assembly clerk's offer of water.

“At zero six forty Nacre standard,” he said, “I invoked emergency authority under the Continuity Preservation Act. The authority will remain in force for ten standard days unless revoked by this chamber. Receiver operations will continue at restricted amplitude while the Ministry establishes a cessation schedule.”

The clinic display divided. One half kept Bex. The other filled with a document carrying the Curvature Office seal and Iona's audit number.

Nemi opened her eyes. “That isn't our release.”

It contained the sink-response summary they had prepared under Bex's containment order. Someone had removed the uncertainty ranges, the channel traces, and every date earlier than Day 3. The remaining page admitted contact with nonhuman intelligence in Eidos. It described the Olun reprisals as an unprecedented external attack.

Below it, a public annotation counted the dead at Helix Quay.

Iona touched the panel and opened source history. The summary had left the Continuity Ministry twelve minutes earlier. Bex's signature authorized publication.

“Six years,” Nemi said. “He cut out six years.”

The Assembly heard the same omission seconds later. A representative from Lumen interrupted before Bex finished his next sentence.

“Minister, did your office possess structured sink responses before Auditor Rhyll's finding?”

Bex looked toward the public recorders. He had calculated this moment, Iona thought. He had likely rehearsed a dozen versions and rejected each one because none could make concealed time harmless.

“Yes.”

“How long?”

“Six years.”

The chamber microphones opened all at once.

Sound struck the clinic in a compressed burst before the broadcast system separated speakers. Questions climbed over demands for resignation. Someone called the continued clearings an undeclared war. Another voice asked whether turning off the network would kill seventeen billion people. The clerk muted the chamber. Public channels supplied their own noise.

Nemi's chair rotated two degrees as she tensed. Iona steadied it with both hands.

Bex waited until the clerk restored order. “The signals did not meet the contact standard then in force. They did establish structured modulation. I sealed them. I also authorized continued transfers.”

“You knew they answered,” the Lumen representative said.

“I knew something opposed the pulses.”

“You knew enough to hide it.”

“I knew an abrupt cessation would place seventeen billion people in structures unable to maintain safe load, orbit, or spin. That fact remains true this morning.”

The panel's public side multiplied into ledger windows. Helix mutual-aid networks posted thirty-four confirmed dead, eight missing, and treatment counts for more than two hundred injured across the damaged cargo decks. A Continuity Ministry model appeared beside it: 2.6 billion deaths in the first thirty days after immediate network shutdown, followed by a broad range that climbed above nine billion within a year. Neither account shared assumptions. Each spread faster than its source data.

A third ledger entered from an Orbit Breaker channel. It labeled every resident of an RME-dependent habitat a hostage maintained by murder and assigned zero moral value to continuity losses after the first warning.

Nemi read it twice. “They turned people into a rounding rule.”

“Bex did the same in the other direction.”

“He at least counted them.”

“He counted one side with names and the other with a threshold.”

The chair's gimbals corrected a small motion in the clinic floor. Nemi gripped the rests until the false tilt passed. Iona watched the live ledgers breed copies. Numbers acquired colors, slogans, and portraits. Every system chose a format that made its fear appear measured.

A red notice crossed her terminal: REPORT TO CONTINUITY REVIEW, IMMEDIATE.

Nemi saw it. “He wants you inside the authority before you can contradict him.”

“He may want the traces explained.”

“He has had six years to ask.”

Iona released the chair's lock. “Can you work?”

“I can work without walking.”

“That wasn't the question.”

Nemi tested the room by turning her head. Her face tightened, then settled. “Give me a fixed screen and no helpful people pulling the chair.”

Iona rolled the instrument console within reach. “Open every source packet from the sealed sink archive. Make a checksum map. Do not transmit.”

“You are going to the review?”

“I am going to learn which account they intend to call complete.”

As Iona left, the clinic panel showed Bex under forty-three standards and a thousand incompatible totals.

Emergency authority changed the building before it changed the law.

Two Continuity officers occupied the lift vestibule. Curvature Office staff surrendered personal wafers at each floor boundary. Public windows had gone opaque, but crowds beyond the glass made their presence audible through the frame: boot strikes, shouted names, a steady hammering against the outer barrier.

Iona took the mechanical stair. Unmodulated Nacre gravity pressed on the old fear behind her knees. Each landing carried a Ministry notice explaining that restricted clearings would preserve essential services. Someone had written HOW MANY REEFS beneath one notice. Another hand answered HOW MANY CHILDREN.

The review room had once housed sink-certification appeals. Its long table displayed orbital maps from all forty-three systems. Bex stood at the far end with three continuity modelers and a legal clerk. No security officer entered with Iona, though two remained outside.

“You published an incomplete packet,” she said.

Bex dismissed the modelers with a glance. The clerk stayed to preserve a witness.

“I published what I could verify for public use.”

“You removed the older responses.”

“I admitted them under oath.”

“You called the repossessions an external attack.”

“They are coordinated returns of force that kill civilians. Ordinary language permits the word.”

“Ordinary language also permits cause.”

Bex touched the table. Forty-three systems contracted into a web of receiver hubs. Seventeen billion dependent residents appeared as white points, too many for the display to separate.

“This is cause,” he said. “Mass, spin, and trajectory. If your preferred account omits those points, it lies as completely as my sealed archive did.”

“My account omits no one.”

“You do not have an account. You have evidence.”

“Then stop dressing your forecast as a tally.”

He enlarged the continuity model. “Every figure carries a range.”

“The public copies don't. Your lower bound became a guaranteed body count before I left the clinic.”

“And the Olun-loss ledger assigns certainty to possible lineages that never formed.”

“It did not come from my office.”

“It came from people using your finding.”

Iona looked at the model assumptions. The first thirty-day estimate treated every receiver shutdown as simultaneous. It allowed no conventional tug mobilization, no habitat consolidation, no mass relocation, and no Olun restraint. The resulting curve had mathematical integrity inside a world where nobody adapted.

She opened the model's neglected branch. “You buried the staged case below six access layers.”

“The staged case lacks fuel allocations.”

“So fund them.”

“It lacks transfer craft.”

“Command them.”

“It still loses habitats.”

“Name those habitats, then. Don't convert them into a line thick enough to hide behind.”

For the first time, anger altered Bex's voice. “You think naming a place gives me engines.”

“No. It gives the people there a chance to know what you have assigned them.”

The legal clerk stopped writing by hand and let the room record for her.

Bex closed the model. “Emergency authority exists because public deliberation cannot move reaction mass in time.”

“Then use it to move reaction mass.”

“I am.”

“While continuing exports to Eidos.”

“At restricted amplitude.”

Iona heard Sella's metal nut strike an old station deck. Restricted amplitude described the sender's hand, not the destination's injury.

“Has Aru accepted that restriction?” she asked.

“Aru does not speak for every pressure-name.”

“Has any Olun speaker consented?”

“No.”

“Then the transfers remain involuntary.”

Bex walked to the wall where the original sink certificates rested in their ceremonial frames. Empty had been printed beneath Eidos in eight languages. “If I stop every transfer today, rings desynchronize before we can place tugs. Hospitals lose controlled load. Ships under burn lose compensation. The deaths begin among people who never saw a sink ledger.”

“And if you continue?”

“I cannot count the Olun losses.”

“That limitation does not make them zero.”

“No. It makes command unbearable.”

The admission landed without defense. Iona saw the former habitat engineer beneath the ministerial coat, a man who could picture bearing failures in inhabited rings and had chosen a distant wound because it never appeared on his maintenance floor.

Bex removed a storage wafer from the table. “Here are the emergency orders. Tug requisitions. Receiver caps. Fuel release from the naval reserve. A mandatory localization design due in forty-eight hours. I am not asking you to praise concealment.”

“What are you asking?”

“Run the sink account. Give the Assembly something more honest than imaginary certainty.”

“You want my name on your partial release.”

“I want your methods on the public record.”

The crowd struck the outer barrier in a new rhythm. Three blows, then two. Iona wondered whether someone had assigned meaning to the pattern or whether people simply needed their hands to agree.

“Publish the old archive,” she said.

“No.”

“Then you want an auditor as decoration.”

“I want ten days in which the Republic remains capable of changing.”

“You already spent six years.”

Bex's gaze moved to the sealed wafer in her hand. “And you think releasing unprocessed channel records will give those years back?”

“No.”

“It may trigger receiver attacks. It may cause systems to sever before localization plans exist.”

“Yes.”

“Then why?”

“Because every model in this room begins after the fact you hid. People cannot judge your authority while you control the beginning of the account.”

Bex did not order her detained. That restraint might have reflected law, calculation, or shame. Iona could not audit motive.

“You remain bound by containment,” he said.

“Your public admission destroyed the factual basis for containment.”

“The order remains valid under emergency authority.”

“Put that in writing.”

The clerk resumed her manual notes.

Outside the review room, an alarm had emptied the receiver-control bay into the corridor. A young operator knelt beside an open service panel while two Continuity officers read an emergency order over his shoulder. His console showed a hospital load request from the inner spindle and an automatic Eidos assignment beneath it.

“Authorize the clearing,” one officer said.

“The cap allows half this load,” the operator replied.

“Medical priority overrides the cap.”

“Then give me another sink.”

“No alternate channel can open inside the treatment window.”

Iona stopped. The operator recognized her and shifted his body enough to expose the screen. The hospital needed controlled load for a spinal repair. Mechanical support would delay the procedure by nine hours. The outgoing pulse fell below every public restriction Bex had just described and still lacked consent at its destination.

“Your audit access has been suspended,” the officer told Iona.

“My eyesight has not.” She addressed the operator. “Can the theatre brace locally?”

“For four hours. After that, thermal limits.”

A conventional service tug could stabilize the spindle by moving two ballast casks along its outer rail. The maneuver would consume reserve propellant and cancel a cargo window. Before emergency authority, three offices would have argued over the expense for a day.

Iona pointed to Bex's new fuel release. “Charge the tug to continuity reserve.”

The officer studied his order. “The Ministry can direct receiver use.”

“It can also direct fuel. Choose the order that doesn't export an unconsented pulse.”

The operator routed the request before anyone stopped him. A tug accepted within twenty seconds. Cargo control protested immediately, which meant the solution had become real enough to inconvenience someone.

No officer arrested Iona. One recorded her name against the intervention. She added his name to her own notes, not as an enemy, but as another person standing where an abstract command acquired a physical destination.

Nemi had built the release packet when Iona returned to the clinic.

The balance chair faced six fixed screens. Each showed a different layer of the sealed archive: raw timing traces, receiver assignments, analyst comments, suppression orders, sink survey limits, and Bex's authorizations. Nemi had added no title page.

“I told you not to transmit,” Iona said.

“You told me to make a checksum map.” Nemi pointed toward a seventh screen. “The map showed three missing blocks. I recovered two from instrument caches. Bex's office deleted the third six years ago.”

“What did it contain?”

“Amplitude correlations. We can infer the shape from references, not restore the values.”

“Mark the absence.”

“I did.”

Iona lowered herself into the chair beside Nemi. The clinic now admitted distant crowd noise through its ventilation ducts. Staff had stopped pretending to work elsewhere. Two nurses stood in the doorway. A receiver technician watched from the floor, his back against a cabinet.

On the public channels, the ledger war had acquired victims chosen for rhetorical use. A child from the Massless City appeared in five continuity feeds, each copy ending before she said her family supported localization. An image translated from an Olun memory reef circulated beneath an invented death count. Someone had placed Sella's face beside a demand for immediate severance, using the official portrait that still named operator error.

Iona closed that window.

Nemi noticed. “They found her quickly.”

“Public archives are efficient when they need a dead woman.”

“The raw data will not stop that.”

“No.”

“It won't tell people what to choose.”

“No.”

Nemi turned carefully within the chair. “Good. I wanted to know whether you had become foolish on the stairs.”

Iona reviewed the release structure. Every file retained its original timestamp. Uncertainties remained attached. The archive separated observed modulation from inferred damage. It also showed the six-year sequence plainly: response, analyst escalation, ministerial seal, continued clearing, stronger response, revised threshold, further clearing.

One comment from a junior analyst asked whether the sink might contain a pressure-sensitive computational medium. The following line reassigned her to navigation audits.

“This name stays private,” Iona said.

“Her analysis is part of provenance.”

“Her current location isn't.”

Nemi removed the personnel routing while preserving the signed comment.

Iona opened a declaration field and began to type. She stopped after one sentence. A preface would become another account competing for authority. The traces required context, but context could name limits without prescribing innocence.

She wrote four notes.

The archive did not provide a defensible count of Olun dead.

The continuity models did not provide a defensible count of deaths under every cessation path.

The raw records proved that structured opposition had been detected six years before public disclosure.

No observation in the archive established Olun consent to continued use.

Nemi read over her shoulder. “Bex will call the second line irresponsible.”

“It says his model has boundaries.”

“He regards boundaries as things other people place around him.”

A call request appeared from the Assembly evidence clerk. Another followed from the Continuity Ministry. Then the Curvature Office director suspended Iona's publication credentials.

Nemi looked at the suspension and smiled without humor. “Seven seconds slow.”

“You transmitted?”

“I prepared routes. You still have to sign.”

The packet could not pass through the public ledger as one block. Ministry filters would stop it. Nemi had divided it across forty-three local audit repositories, each protected by rules designed to prevent Nacre from rewriting system accounts after a dispute. Once signed, the pieces would replicate and verify one another.

Iona studied the route list. “This fractures custody.”

“Custody already fractured when they removed a block.”

“It also exposes every system auditor who accepts a piece.”

“They chose acceptance keys during the last hour.”

“How many?”

“Thirty-four.”

Nine systems had declined. Their empty columns looked more accusatory than a red refusal.

Iona called one accepting auditor at random, a woman in a mining spindle around Kestrel. “Do you know what this packet contains?”

“Evidence my government says will cause panic.”

“That is not an answer.”

The auditor drew breath. “It contains original sink responses, concealment orders, and uncertainties. I accept custody, not your conclusions.”

“Correct.”

“Do you still want my key?”

“Yes.”

The Kestrel signature joined thirty-three others.

Iona pressed her thumb to the release.

For three seconds, nothing happened. Then the six screens began losing local ownership marks as pieces crossed into system repositories. The raw data did not appear as a single revelation. It arrived in fragments that demanded assembly: a trace in Lumen, a seal order at Nacre, receiver histories in Meridian, analyst notes in eight minor systems. Public networks seized the checksums and built mirrors faster than Ministry filters could name them.

The clinic panel changed first. Bex's continuity curve remained on the left. An Olun-loss estimate held the right. Between them, the archive opened a blank-backed record with no approved interpretation.

People filled that space at once.

Some read the traces and demanded immediate severance. Others called every file forged. Habitat councils began publishing local dependency accounts. Receiver crews posted maintenance limits the Ministry had classified. Olun advocates marked each concealed response by date without adding invented bodies. Three systems shut their public ledgers rather than mirror the packet. Two announced independent continuity commands.

The Assembly clerk asked Iona to authenticate the release under oath. The Continuity Ministry issued a detention instruction, then withdrew it before security reached the clinic. Bex did neither on camera.

Nemi watched the system map divide into incompatible colors. “We wanted a shared account.”

“We wanted an honest one.”

“Those may not be the same thing.”

A new public ledger opened from Stillness. It counted local ballast, fusion fuel, available tugs, and how many residents the receiver-free city could shelter. No dead appeared in its first column. Capacity did.

Another ledger from an RME-dependent nursery listed hours until unsafe spin under immediate shutdown. Beside it, someone linked the oldest concealed Eidos response.

Neither cancelled the other.

Across the Republic, the single green network diagram broke into forty-three system accounts, then hundreds of habitat accounts beneath them. The lines no longer agreed on what counted as survival, injury, evidence, or debt. For the first time, they could not conceal their disagreement behind one total.

On Iona's screen, the official shared ledger failed checksum and went gray.

Chapter 11 — The Orbit Breakers

The unauthorized clamp flashed once beneath the nursery cylinder's receiver spine.

Tavin Serr caught the reflection through the Morrowline's forward blister as his ship eased a ballast cask toward the cylinder's outer rail. A legal maintenance clamp would have carried a yellow locator pulse. This one swallowed light except when the blue-white primary struck its cutting edge.

“Hold translation,” he said.

Haru Den killed the tug program. Six thruster clusters dropped to idle in sequence, leaving the heavy freighter and its suspended cask moving on the momentum they already possessed. The cargo yoke groaned as the cask tried to continue alone.

Pella's voice came from engineering. “That hold just spent twelve kilograms of propellant we won't get back.”

“Charge it to my eyesight.”

“Your eyesight has no budget code.”

“Then make one.”

Tavin enlarged the receiver spine. The nursery cylinder turned below them, three kilometers long and narrow enough to complete a rotation every forty-two seconds. Window bands passed through sunlight, then under the radiation shade. Inside, twelve thousand children lived with parents, teachers, clinic staff, and the workers who maintained the cylinder's farms. Its receiver smoothed bearing loads and handled orbital trim that the original fusion tugs no longer supplied.

Four black clamps now showed against the spine.

Haru leaned toward his display. “Maintenance plan lists none.”

“Ask cylinder control whether they have a crew outside.”

The answer came before his query left the bridge. A woman on the public emergency channel spoke too close to her microphone.

“Receiver crew, clear the spine. We are going to sever the export system. You have nine minutes.”

A compressed file followed. It contained Bex's concealment orders, images from Helix Quay, and a translated pressure reef damaged by a human debt pulse. The last page carried the Orbit Breaker mark: an open ring crossed by a cut line.

Pella came up from engineering with a tool harness over her pressure suit. “They have better timing than the Ministry. Four clamps in under six minutes.”

“Can they cut the spine?” Tavin asked.

“Anything cuts if you put enough heat into it.”

“Not what I asked.”

She studied the magnified hardware. “Those aren't heaters. Induction collars, probably with explosive interrupts under the shell. Four visible means twelve around the ring. If they fire together, every field segment loses its reference in the same millisecond.”

“Synchronized,” Haru said.

Pella nodded. “No rolling shutdown. No bleed path. They want the receiver unable to hand debt to an adjacent segment.”

The cylinder controller reached them at last. “Morrowline, we have intruders in the service annulus. Local security lost access. Receiver output remains at emergency cap.”

“How much local support can you carry?” Tavin asked.

“Bearing margins allow thirty-two minutes without smoothing. Orbital trim diverges after three hours. The nursery fluid beds exceed lateral tolerance sooner if we change spin.”

“Conventional tugs?”

“Two docked. One lacks a flight crew.”

Tavin looked at the ballast cask hanging from his yoke. They had brought it for Iona's localization trial, five thousand tonnes of tungsten and water meant to move along the outer rail while fusion thrust paid part of the curvature debt locally. With preparation, the mass could help. Suspended between ship and cylinder during a violent receiver collapse, it could punch through a window band.

“Put the cask on the emergency cradle,” he said. “No receiver compensation during the set.”

Pella gave him a sharp glance. “That costs us the alignment window.”

“We don't have the window anymore.”

Haru bent to navigation. The Morrowline's thrusters woke, not together, but in pairs chosen to keep exhaust clear of the cylinder. Conventional thrust pressed Tavin into his couch. The cargo yoke swung by half a degree. Pella cursed the movement, then fed corrective torque through two independent winches.

Below, the intruder's voice repeated its warning. “This mechanism sends force into living worlds. Leave it intact and you choose the injury.”

Tavin opened a direct channel. “Cut it now and you drop a nursery cylinder into an uncontrolled load transition.”

A pause answered him. Then the woman said, “Captain Serr. Stillness made your position public.”

“My position includes people inside the machinery.”

“Every delay sends another pulse.”

“Then help me shut it down without killing them.”

“You had a century.”

“I had nine minutes from you.”

No reply came. The black clamps remained dark.

The emergency cradle caught the ballast cask on its third attempt.

Its latches had been designed for empty construction drums, not five thousand tonnes arriving with residual motion. The first pair struck and released. On the bridge, the impact sounded like a hammer against a pressure hull. The second pair took load unevenly. Pella shifted the yoke by centimeters while Haru spent propellant to hold the ship away from the cylinder.

When the final indicators turned green, Tavin released the cargo.

The Morrowline rose two meters relative to the cylinder as the mass left her suspension. Haru corrected before the hull crossed a service antenna.

“Cask secure,” Pella said. “Our yoke has three warped pins.”

“Can it lift later?”

“It can disappoint us later.”

Tavin unstrapped. “Sive, bridge.”

The medic arrived carrying a restraint bag and a portable load monitor. “I assume diplomacy failed.”

“It has nine minutes in it.”

“Eight now.”

Tavin pulled his pressure hood over his head. Pella was already sealing her suit.

Haru blocked the hatch with one hand. “Captain, boarding an active receiver spine does not improve command continuity.”

“You have the ship.”

“I have a freighter attached to a cylinder under sabotage.”

“Which qualifies you.”

Pella handed Tavin a tether reel. “Save the argument for when there is down.”

They crossed through the forward utility lock in a two-person pod. The pod carried no receiver compensator. Its cold-gas jets pushed against stored nitrogen and produced honest, limited acceleration. Each correction reduced the pressure gauge beside Tavin's wrist.

The nursery cylinder filled half the view. Rotation made its service lights draw circles against the star field. A maintenance spoke extended from the nonrotating hub to the moving habitat shell, with slip bearings at the joint. The receiver spine wrapped the hub in twelve armored segments.

Tavin matched the hub rather than the cylinder. His body lost the Morrowline's deck reference and floated against the pod straps. The old naval training returned without ceremony: choose a frame, name every relative motion, never trust the eye when a rotating horizon occupies most of the window.

Pella pointed. “There.”

A maintenance barge clung to the far side of the hub with its beacon disabled. Three suited figures worked along separate receiver segments. Tether lines linked them to the barge. Tavin counted the visible clamps as the rotation carried each through sunlight.

Twelve.

The cell had placed one collar on every field segment. Fiber ran between them beneath the spine's thermal blanket. A synchronized command could sever all paths before the receiver controller shifted load.

“Can you jam the trigger?” Tavin asked.

“Maybe. If it fails closed, we trigger it.”

“Can you cut their fiber?”

“Same risk. The collars may fire on lost signal.”

“Then we take the command unit.”

The barge lay forty meters across open space. Tavin fired the pod's lateral jets. Nitrogen dropped six percent. The cell saw them move and one figure turned a tool toward the pod.

Pella recognized it first. “Grapple driver.”

The bolt struck the pod's outer rail and dragged their nose toward the barge. It did not pierce pressure skin. Tavin killed his jet command before the guidance system fought the tether and wasted gas.

“Helpful of them,” he said.

Pella stared through her visor. “I will mention your gratitude while they detonate us.”

Tavin reeled the hostile grapple in. The cell had anchored its far end to the maintenance barge. Instead of pulling the pod off course, the line now gave them a physical route. He vented one short burst to keep their closing speed below a meter per second.

At ten meters, the cell leader opened a suit channel. “Do not board.”

“I need your trigger.”

“You need permission to continue killing the Olun.”

“No. I need time to put local support under twelve thousand children.”

“Continuity language.”

“Bearing language. Come inside and read the loads yourself.”

“We read Helix.”

“So did I.”

Tavin caught the barge rail. Rotation placed the bright primary behind the cylinder, and shadow erased depth. He clipped his tether by touch. Pella landed beside him with enough force to flex both knees against her suit joints.

A cell member launched from the receiver spine toward them. She carried a manual cutter, not a gun. Tavin let her close, caught her tool wrist, and turned with the impact rather than opposing it. Their combined bodies rotated around the barge rail. Pella clipped a restraint line through the intruder's harness.

The leader moved toward a gray case fixed under the barge console.

Tavin pushed from the rail. He could not cross five meters before her hand arrived. Pella could not reach either of them.

“Haru,” he said. “Mark now.”

The Morrowline fired one attitude jet.

Its exhaust did not touch the barge. The pulse changed the ship's relative position enough to tighten the cargo yoke's recovery line, which Pella had left clipped to the pod. The pod shifted. Its grapple line snapped taut and jerked the barge through twenty centimeters.

The leader missed the case latch.

Tavin struck her shoulder and carried both of them into the console. His helmet hit metal. White static crossed his vision, then cleared. The leader drove an elbow into his suit chest, found hard plating, and reached again.

Pella arrived with the restraint bag. She wrapped it around the leader's forearm and inflated it. Foam stiffened around the joint without crushing it.

“Engineering solution,” Pella said.

The third cell member remained on the spine. “I can fire locally.”

His glove rested on one collar.

Tavin froze against the console. “One collar doesn't sever the receiver.”

“It opens an imbalance.”

“It opens a correction through the other eleven segments. You won't know where the load goes.”

“It stays here.”

“No. The controller will seek a paired path before it accepts collapse. You built the synchronized rig because you know that.”

The man's hand did not move. Behind his visor, his face looked younger than Keir's.

Pella kept her voice flat. “Your local switch also burns the collar battery through your glove. If you want to lose the hand for a partial fault, press hard.”

Tavin glanced at her.

“Probably,” she added on their private channel.

The cell leader strained against the foam. “Saren, hold.”

Saren lifted his hand.

Pella opened the gray case. A timing bus filled most of its interior. Twelve optical channels ran from a temperature-stabilized clock to individual collar keys. The workmanship belonged in a shipyard. Someone had balanced path length so every interrupt would fire within twenty microseconds.

“They didn't improvise this,” she said.

“Can you disarm it?”

“Not safely from here. I can break synchronization without breaking signal.”

“How?”

“Give each collar a different delay.”

“That creates a rolling shutdown.”

“If the receiver controller accepts the sequence.”

The cylinder controller joined their channel. “We need twenty-four minutes to transfer smoothing onto bearings and local flywheels. More for orbital trim.”

Saren spoke from the spine. “Twenty-four minutes means another clearing.”

“No clearing,” Tavin said. “Set the receiver to local hold. Accrue the debt here.”

The controller hesitated. “That exceeds our authorized local balance.”

“Emergency authority can arrest me after the children stop floating into walls.”

“It can correct automatically if the balance crosses threshold.”

Pella examined the case. “Then we keep the segments alive until local support takes load. Afterward we can bleed them in sequence faster than the correction threshold.”

The cell leader gave a short, disbelieving laugh. “You are using our device.”

“I am keeping it from using you,” Tavin said.

Twenty-four minutes grew into thirty-seven.

One of the cylinder's conventional tugs lacked a pilot, so Haru flew it remotely from the Morrowline. Signal delay remained small, but camera angles lied whenever the cylinder's window bands crossed behind the tug. He placed the craft against the hub and began a continuous low-thrust burn that replaced part of the receiver's orbital trim.

Inside, maintenance crews moved water from axial tanks toward the outer shell. The redistribution altered bearing loads a fraction at a time. Each pump consumed power. Each tonne changed the cylinder's spin response. Families left the two innermost residential decks and strapped into school shelters closer to the stable rim.

Tavin watched their movements through load diagrams patched into his visor. He could not see the children. He saw twelve thousand body-mass allocations travel through corridors in uneven clusters, each marker delayed by someone carrying an infant or waiting for a slower neighbor.

The cell members watched too.

Saren had crossed from the spine under restraint and now sat against the barge rail. His collar switch floated in Pella's evidence bag. “You could have begun this years ago,” he said.

“Yes,” Tavin answered.

“Then why stop us?”

“Because being late doesn't make every fast act right.”

“Your ship used clearing.”

“It still does at capped load.”

“You are defending it.”

Tavin pointed to the local debt trace. It climbed while the receiver held without export. “No pulse has left this cylinder since we boarded. I am defending the time needed to replace what the system does.”

“And afterward?”

“Afterward the cylinder will have less capacity, shorter bearing life, and a tug burning fuel every second. People may have to leave.”

Saren looked toward the rotating habitat shell. “At least the cost stays visible.”

“Visible costs still crush people.”

“Hidden ones already did.”

Tavin had no answer that reduced either fact. He checked the cylinder load instead.

At minute thirty-one, one water pump overheated. The redistribution stalled with too much mass near the axis. Pella recommended restoring a receiver segment for six minutes. Tavin refused until she showed him the bearing temperature curve. Then he authorized one segment at diagnostic amplitude, with local debt still accumulating and no sink path open.

The narrow field eased the bearing load enough for technicians to swap the pump. It produced no free support. Stored electrical power fell, and the receiver account rose against the cylinder itself.

At minute thirty-five, Haru reported the remote tug's fuel margin. “We can maintain this burn for four hours, twelve minutes. After that, the return reserve disappears.”

“Schedule a relief tug.”

“Nearest qualified craft arrives in six hours.”

“Then the first tug won't return.”

Haru understood. “I'll tell its owner.”

The owner objected for forty seconds, learned that twelve thousand children occupied the cylinder, and donated the return reserve under protest. Tavin respected the protest. Fuel did not become less finite because a good cause consumed it.

Pella rewrote the timing bus. Instead of one firing command, the device now held twelve delays separated by ninety seconds. Each delay required confirmation from the cylinder controller. A skipped confirmation would keep the remaining collars closed.

“You designed a safe shutdown tool inside their sabotage case,” Tavin said.

“They designed most of it. I added patience.”

The cell leader listened from her restraint. “Patience under armed control.”

Tavin released the foam from her arm but kept her tether short. “You can verify each delay.”

Pella objected. “Captain.”

“She knows the hardware.”

“She tried to fire it.”

“And now she can tell us whether you made a mistake.”

The leader flexed her hand as blood returned. “If your controller opens a sink path, I stop the sequence.”

“If the controller opens a sink path, I help you stop it.”

That agreement satisfied no law. It gave each side a way to prevent the other's worst choice.

At minute thirty-seven, the cylinder reported local support ready. Schools had sealed. Clinics had locked their articulated beds. Water mass reached the commanded radius. The remote tug held a steady conventional burn against orbital drift.

Pella enabled the first delay.

Collar one opened its field segment. The receiver controller pushed load toward the other eleven, then found the rising local support and accepted a lower command. Bearing temperature climbed by two degrees.

Ninety seconds later, collar two opened.

By the fifth segment, the cylinder's apparent down shifted three degrees from the floor normal. Children in the shelters would feel the tilt. Loose objects rolled toward spinward walls. No cargo deck inverted. No hidden destination accepted the force.

At segment eight, an automatic continuity routine attempted to restore the sink link. Tavin saw the path request form on his visor.

“Block it,” he said.

The cylinder controller answered, “Ministry override.”

The cell leader reached the case before Pella. She pulled the optical authorization lead without disturbing the collar signals. The sink request failed. Every remaining segment stayed alive.

Pella stared at her. “That lead also carried the clock reference.”

“I built the bus,” the leader said.

“You could have mentioned it.”

“You could have asked who built the bus.”

They restored timing from the case's internal oscillator. The sequence continued.

When the twelfth collar opened, the receiver spine went dark without a surge. The nursery cylinder relied on moved water, bearing friction, and the continuous push of a fusion tug. Its local debt stopped growing because no field remained active. Its fuel account fell instead.

A cheer rose over the cylinder channel. Tavin muted it after two seconds. The shutdown had worked, but four residential decks would remain closed, the tug could not recover, and every bearing inspection interval had shortened. Celebration could wait until someone assigned those costs without hiding them.

Security craft arrived forty minutes late.

Their commander demanded custody of the cell, the barge, and all sabotage components. Tavin transferred the three people after recording their suit condition and the agreement that had completed the shutdown. He released Saren last.

“You'll call this a rescue,” Saren said at the lock.

“I'll call it an interrupted killing followed by useful work.”

“Whose killing?”

“Everyone's, if we keep choosing machinery over people or people over physics.”

Saren shook his head. “That sounds like balance.”

“No. Balance would imply the harms cancel.”

The security lock closed between them.

Pella held up the gray timing case. “Commander wants this too.”

Tavin examined the twelve-channel bus. One corner had bent during the fight. Its clock still held synchronization within twenty microseconds. With different control logic, the device could coordinate receiver shutdown across an entire habitat after mass and thrust were ready.

“Log the collars as transferred,” he said.

“And the command unit?”

“Damaged beyond field custody.”

Pella's eyebrows rose. “That would be inaccurate.”

“Then make it accurate.”

She removed the scorched outer shell, stamped it as scrap, and installed the timing bus inside a spare thruster-control case. The act violated evidence procedure and preserved no weapon by itself. It kept a way to make twelve dangerous changes occur in an order people could survive.

Back aboard the Morrowline, Tavin secured the rebuilt unit in the conventional-drive locker. Outside, the nursery cylinder turned under its own spin while a fusion tug burned away its return fuel.

The receiver spine stayed dark. The synchronizer kept time in Tavin's hand.

Chapter 12 — The Price of Correction

Soil struck the inner pipe in heavy, irregular blows.

Iona stood knee-deep in a drained tomato bed while farm workers shoveled the torus's growing medium into transfer hoppers. Fine black grains clung to her audit suit. The irrigation tubes under her boots had carried water that morning. Now they opened into a temporary manifold leading toward the axial tanks.

Above her, rows of tomato vines curved with the farm torus until they disappeared behind trellises and light panels. Red fruit hung among leaves already beginning to soften. No harvest crew moved between the rows. The workers had three hours to strip sensor wire, salvage seed stock, and convert forty hectares of cultivated mass into ballast.

A hopper jammed. One worker drove a bar into the throat and called for lower feed. The shovel line slowed but did not stop.

Iona checked the mass ticket on her wrist. “This load contains root mat.”

“Everything contains root mat,” said the farm superintendent. “We built soil instead of ordering it from a factory.”

“The test plan specifies clean medium.”

“The test plan came from people who have never removed a field.”

Another impact rang through the pipe. The superintendent watched the nearest vines tremble. She was a compact woman with gray hair shaved above both ears, dressed in a patched humidity jacket. Her badge identified her only by office. She had refused to let the public networks use her name.

“You can still cancel,” Iona said.

“Can you?”

“Yes.”

“Will Eidos receive tonight's clearing if we do?”

“The emergency schedule assigns a restricted pulse.”

“Then I cannot cancel. I can only choose which people see what I lose.”

The torus rotated around a nonspinning service axis. Mechanical spin supplied most of its apparent gravity.
