Iona copied all three. A clean number would have required choosing which imperfect record to discard. She had spent too many years watching institutions improve reports by excluding the people who made them difficult.

A chime passed through the ring. Every loose shoe shifted toward the gas giant, then settled aft as the spin caught up.

“Stage One begins in six minutes,” said Meridian Control over the public circuit. “All personnel secure against variable load.”

The lockmaster laughed without humor. “Secure where?”

Iona opened the correction plan. Meridian’s eleven inhabited cylinders had accrued debt unevenly through decades of orbital station-keeping, spin smoothing, and load compensation. The Accord would reduce receiver support in three stages. Fusion tugs would absorb the orbital correction. Internal ballast would move outward to preserve spin. The Olun speakers in contact with Aru had agreed not to repossess while the debt fell through local expenditure.

Agreed did not mean unanimous. Aru had repeated that distinction until human negotiators accused the translation cradle of imprecision.

A pressure pulse crossed Iona’s wrist display. The cradle rendered it as a rough sentence beneath the raw ratios.

Several histories hold. Several prepare return. The interval narrows.

She called Bex. The Minister appeared from Meridian Central, strapped into a command couch. Staff moved behind him with the restrained speed of people trying not to jostle a failing floor.

“Aru reports the interval narrowing,” Iona said. “Delay Stage One until the nursery clears.”

“Delay increases the accumulated return.”

“Proceeding puts the load change through occupied transfer corridors.”

“We have four tugs committed and a fifth approaching. The nursery ring retains full structural margin.”

“The same phrase appears in the report from sixteen years ago.”

Bex looked directly into the camera. “I know.”

For a moment she saw the habitat engineer beneath his office, the man who understood bolts and bodies before continuity models taught him to aggregate them. Then another alert drew his eyes aside.

“We cannot preserve every cylinder,” he said. “Stage One protects the hub and opens ten thousand more berths.”

“Only if Eidos accepts the declining load.”

“No new clearing forms part of the Accord.”

The wording came too quickly. Iona marked the call for forensic retention.

“Tavin has asked for full abandonment order,” she said.

“Tavin sees docking geometry. I see seventeen billion dependent people watching whether Meridian can localize without collapse.”

“You see them through a model.”

“I see their air inventories by name.” Bex’s voice remained low. “Begin the stage.”

The channel closed.

Iona sent the lockmaster the three census lists and boarded Morrowline’s gantry. It flexed between hulls as the nursery ring turned beneath the ship. Forty children came across in paired restraint lines, each clipped to the rail by a waist tether. Some cried. Most concentrated on their hands. A teacher moved backward ahead of them, counting faces while an engineer called the changing distance between hulls.

Tavin met Iona at the freighter lock. “Stage plan?”

“Proceeding.”

“Evacuation order?”

“Still partial.”

His jaw moved once. “Then I am issuing one to every ring captain who will take it.”

“You lack civil authority.”

“I have empty berths.”

“That may prove more persuasive.”

They reached the bridge as Stage One began.

Receiver output dropped four percent. The change entered Iona through her knees before the display recorded it. Across Meridian, cylinder spin assumed more of the local load. Rings widened their orbits by fractions. Fusion tugs fired in timed sequence, exhaust turning white against the gas giant’s shadow.

For thirty-two seconds, the Accord worked.

Debt fell on Iona’s trace. No corresponding pulse entered the dark lattice. The nursery ring’s ballast trains moved outward. Its rotation steadied. Evacuation collars gained enough load to cycle without binding, and six locks opened together.

Then an unlisted channel took part of the reduction.

Iona enlarged the path. It carried a Continuity seal and no public destination. The signature reached through Meridian Central, split the local debt, and sent one portion into a sink assignment removed from the visible plan.

“Bex,” she said.

Tavin glanced across. “What did he do?”

“He diverted the remainder.”

“Where?”

The hidden path resolved after its authorization packet closed.

“Eidos.”

Iona opened a priority channel to Central. Bex did not answer. His deputy appeared, mouth moving before the audio synchronized.

“Ministerial command is occupied.”

“Terminate emergency sink route M-Delta-Seven.”

“No such route appears on my board.”

“Then your board has been blinded. Cut the Continuity trunk physically.”

The deputy looked offscreen. “Doing so would interrupt the staged correction.”

“It has already been interrupted.”

Aru’s raw pressure signal struck the cradle so hard the translated text dissolved into blocks. Through the receiver network came a pattern Iona had learned to recognize without language: histories aligning around impact.

The Olun returned the diverted load.

Gravity surged through Morrowline. Iona’s restraint drove into her hips. On the forward display, three evacuation gantries folded against their ships. One tore free and spun between the ring planes, shedding white restraint webbing.

“Full release,” Tavin ordered. “All rings. Haru, assign craft by nearest viable lock. Pella, break us from the nursery before its collar takes our truss.”

Pella’s acknowledgment arrived beneath metal alarms. “Collar has us by six teeth. I can retract four.”

“Cut the remaining two.”

“People in the gantry.”

Through the docking camera, Iona saw thirty-one children and four adults clipped along the passage. Sudden load had dropped them against one wall. The teacher at the front tried to stand, failed, and began passing the children hand to hand toward Morrowline.

Tavin opened the gantry circuit. “You have ninety seconds before we lose alignment. Keep every tether clipped until the inner hatch.”

The teacher did not waste breath answering.

Iona left the bridge. Sive met her at the cargo lock with two load crew. They pulled children across the sill in groups of three. A boy had lost one shoe. The remaining one shone red.

“Outer door will not close,” Sive said. “Frame twisted.”

“We do not need it closed if the gantry separates cleanly.”

“It will not.”

Pella climbed through the service hatch beside them carrying a powered cutter. “I can release the teeth from the ring side. When I shout, close this pressure panel.”

“You cannot get back through after it closes,” Iona said.

“I have a suit.” Pella slapped the collar of her work wrap, where a compact hood and pressure film waited. “Also, I dislike arguments conducted while my ship is being used as structural reinforcement.”

She crawled into the service channel before Tavin could countermand her. Iona watched through the inspection glass. Pella reached the first jammed tooth, braced both boots, and applied the cutter. Sparks streaked sideways as the ring’s changing gravity pulled them.

The final children crossed. Their teacher came last, still counting.

“Thirty-one,” she said. “All thirty-one.”

The nursery ring lurched outward.

Pella severed the first tooth. The gantry dropped half a meter and pinned her left glove against the housing. She cut the second with her right hand. Its release drove the collar plates together.

Pella screamed once.

Sive struck the emergency override. Tavin fired Morrowline’s lateral clusters, taking weight off the joint for less than a second. Iona reached into the service gap and caught Pella’s harness. Together they dragged her through as the collar separated.

Two fingers remained inside the glove where the plates had closed.

Sive sealed the bleeding hand before Pella could look at it. “Index and middle. Clean compression. Keep your wrist above your heart.”

“My heart has moved around a lot today,” Pella said through clenched teeth.

A vibration ran through the hull. Iona felt each structural member pass the load onward.

“Morrowline clear,” Tavin said over the circuit. “Cargo lock, secure. We are going back for the next collar.”

Pella tried to rise.

Sive pushed her down with one hand. “You have done your collar.”

“My engines are out there.”

“Your assistant knows which end gets hot.”

“That remains disputed.”

Iona left them because choosing to watch did not improve the outcome.

On the bridge, Meridian had become a set of independent emergencies. Cylinder Four’s evacuation spine had jammed. Ring Eight lost spin synchronization and ordered residents to lie against the outer decks. A water carrier took six hundred people through a cargo hatch built for tanks. Tavin assigned ships by minutes to structural failure, ignoring Ministry priority codes.

Cylinder Four called again while its assigned passenger packet held ten meters from the jammed spine. The lock’s inner wheel had stopped between positions, leaving a gap too narrow for an adult in a pressure suit. Through the packet camera, Iona could see residents gathering behind the glass. They had brought suitcases despite the load order, each person still behaving as if evacuation meant travel.

“Cut the wheel into quarters,” Tavin told the cylinder engineer.

“We lose the lock after that.”

“You have one use left for it.”

The engineer asked for a cutting crew. None could cross the gap safely. Tavin turned Morrowline until a side cluster faced away from the packet, then fired a sequence of short pulses. Exhaust pushed the smaller ship against its collar just hard enough to hold contact without receiver assistance. Two packet deckhands climbed through the partial opening with saws clipped to their chests.

Iona listened as the wheel came apart. The first quarter jammed against the frame. The second broke free and struck a deckhand’s shoulder. Residents on the cylinder side pulled him through, splinted the arm, and sent him back to finish. When the opening widened, the crowd tried to move at once.

“Bags stay,” the cylinder engineer announced. “Hands stay on the person ahead. If you have a child, say the child’s name at the threshold.”

Names crossed the circuit with each body. Ansa. Pell. Ro. Din and Din’s mother. Ansa again, corrected to Asa. The engineer repeated every one until the packet loadmaster answered. No registry held the exchange. Iona opened a local record and let the voices populate it without cleaning their errors.

After four hundred people crossed, Cylinder Four’s spine tore farther along its mount. The packet captain released before the fracture could peel open her hull. Seventy-three residents remained behind the glass. A cargo tug took the packet’s place from the opposite side, using a maintenance port that required people to crawl through one at a time.

Tavin marked Cylinder Four yellow rather than complete. “Next problem.”

Bex finally answered.

His command room had gone dark except for emergency strips. Blood marked one side of his forehead.

“You sent debt to Eidos,” Iona said.

“I sent nine percent of Stage One. The local tugs could not absorb it and preserve the nursery orbit.”

“You concealed the route from every participant.”

“If I had listed it, the Accord would have failed before execution.”

“It failed during execution.”

Bex closed his eyes for one breath. “The nursery had eighty-one thousand people attached to its support registry.”

“It also had Olun lineages at the destination you selected.”

“I chose a temporary export over a cylinder break.”

“And received both.”

Behind him, an aide called that Central’s outer tether had parted. Bex tightened his restraint.

“Can the receiver hub be isolated?” Iona asked.

“Not remotely.”

“Then abandon Central.”

“Someone must hold the stage valves open for the tugs.”

“Manual locks?”

“Yes.”

He had returned to the scale of tools. It did not forgive him. Iona transmitted Tavin’s nearest shuttle assignment to his console.

“Hold them until the last tug clears,” she said. “Then take that shuttle.”

Bex read the assignment. “You still believe I should live.”

“I believe your death would close an account before testimony.”

His expression accepted the wound without pretending it amounted to mercy. “Understood.”

A second returned pulse entered Meridian.

The receiver hub shed its outer field coils. Metal loops hundreds of meters wide lifted from their mounts and crossed the cylinder chain in slow, lethal arcs. One struck an empty industrial ring. Another cut through a cargo dock after its last packet pulled away. The third passed beneath the nursery ring close enough to drag its orbit inward.

Haru called the change. “Nursery pericenter falling. Twelve kilometers on this pass.”

“Tow options?” Tavin asked.

“None with people aboard. The spin axis is precessing.”

Iona looked at the evacuation count. Thousands remained in inner shelters. Most available craft had already docked or waited in the ring’s path. If Tavin attached Morrowline, the freighter could alter the cylinder’s tumble or move people, not both.

“Use the ship for lift,” she said.

Tavin studied the same geometry. “Agreed.”

He ordered every tug to stop trying to save the ring.

The statement took three repetitions before local control accepted it. For sixteen years, Meridian had maintained the nursery as proof that the first incident had been repaired. New hull wrapped old damage. Gardens grew across sections where Sella and nineteen other engineers had died. Families chose births there because the Republic certified the ring safer than any other cylinder.

Now its controller asked, “If we release orbital stabilization, what happens to the structure?”

Haru answered because Tavin had no softer physics available. “It crosses the inner debris plane in forty-seven minutes. The next gas-giant tide exceeds frame tolerance.”

“Can we come back after?”

“No.”

The controller left the circuit open. In the background, someone began announcing shelter groups.

Evacuation changed from organized lift to stripping a place of bodies. School records stayed in cabinets. Incubators crossed without their diagnostic bases. Teachers wrote children’s names on suit film when wrist tags failed. A maintenance crew welded a cargo ramp directly to an ore tug, then abandoned the welder while its electrode still glowed.

Morrowline took twelve hundred into bays rated for seven hundred. Tavin vented reserve water to free tank volume and used the empty compartments as pressure shelters. Sive assigned every conscious adult a child to restrain during thrust. Pella, medicated and furious, directed engine work from a cargo couch with her injured hand sealed against her chest.

Iona returned to the nursery ring on the last service shuttle.

She did not go for a record. One inner shelter had reported forty-six infants and no functioning transfer cradle. The shuttle crew carried them six at a time in hard cases, each case clipped to two people. Iona took the final pair. Their labels read Mera Olt, six weeks, and Tavi Nn, nine weeks. One slept through the alarms. The other watched Iona with unfocused eyes while the deck’s direction moved beneath them.

At the lock, the red-booted boy waited beside the teacher. Someone had found his missing shoe in the wall net.

“Is this all?” Iona asked.

The teacher looked down the corridor. Its lights failed section by section toward the shelter.

“All we could reach.”

That answer differed from all.

They crossed to the shuttle. Behind Iona, lock workers shut the ring-side hatch though nobody required pressure integrity now. Habit finished the task that survival no longer needed.

The shuttle pulled away with eight minutes to spare. Through its small window, Iona watched the nursery ring turn. A painted band of animals circled the children’s decks: birds, long-bodied dogs, a blue creature invented by some class. Sixteen years earlier, Sella had died somewhere behind that painted hull. Iona had imagined the place preserved, a fixed point against which the official account could someday be tested.

The gas giant’s tide reached the ring.

Its axis tipped. Windows flashed across sunlight, vanished, and flashed again. The central spine bent near the old repaired section. For one second the cylinder resembled two rigid bodies joined by a hinge. Then the nursery ring broke.

Air spread into a white cloud carrying insulation, paper, soil, and the bright panels from a children’s corridor. The two halves turned away from each other. No engine could gather them. No later hearing could restore the rooms.

Iona held the infant cases against the shuttle deck while full conventional thrust carried them clear. Around Meridian, surviving cylinders abandoned their orbits one by one. Evacuation craft streamed outward, overloaded and low on fuel. Behind them, the receiver hub burned through its remaining coils and went dark.

Meridian Control removed the nursery ring from the active navigation list. The identifier did not change to lost or destroyed. It simply disappeared, leaving a blank interval between Cylinder Five and the industrial pair.

Iona kept her eyes on that absence until the shuttle docked.

Chapter 17 — The Unpaid Dead

Iona found Sella’s handwriting inside a box that had been struck hard enough to fold its hinges inward.

The salvage team had pulled the box from a section of nursery-ring maintenance deck caught in Meridian Cylinder Six’s emergency net. Its outer casing carried fire scoring, a strip of faded green paint, and a service number from sixteen years ago. Half the number had been hidden beneath newer inspection labels. A technician warmed the latches with a hand torch, cut the bent hinge pins, and asked Iona to stand behind the pressure screen.

When the lid came loose, gray water rose from the seal and formed beads in the salvage bay’s weak spin. Three data wafers rested in individual slots. Two had cracked. The third carried a handwritten tag under transparent film.

S. Rhyll / nursery transfer / unresolved.

Iona read the letters twice before touching the screen.

Around her, Meridian’s wreckage crossed the bay windows in slow procession. A torn garden deck showed black soil packed against one wall. Two pressure doors remained joined to a frame with no corridor on either side. Farther out, one half of the nursery ring turned against the gas giant’s cloud bands while salvage tugs marked a perimeter nobody expected to enter alive.

The technician said, “We can image the wafer without mounting it.”

“How long?”

“Twenty minutes for surface mapping. Longer if the inner layers delaminated.”

“Do it here.”

“You have a cleaner lab aboard Morrowline.”

“I want the custody record to begin before it leaves this deck.”

He looked at the tag and stopped arguing. His name patch read Eren Vass, Meridian Municipal Maintenance. The word municipal had lost its physical referent overnight. He still wore it.

Eren placed the wafer in an optical cradle. Iona started a forensic log with Cylinder Six’s remaining clock, Morrowline’s clock, and a free-running salvage chronometer that had never touched the receiver network. She recorded the box dimensions, hinge deformation, water temperature, service number, and every hand that had moved it since recovery. Procedure steadied nothing. It only made later denial more expensive.

The first image appeared as a field of broken characters.

“Outer layer took heat,” Eren said. “I can read the maintenance header.”

Iona enlarged it. The date matched Meridian’s weight-loss incident sixteen years earlier. Sella had written the entry forty-three minutes before the official report placed her at the nursery ballast controls. The report described her as fatigued, out of sequence, and responsible for opening a manual clutch before receiver output stabilized.

Iona knew its wording without retrieving it. She had cited the same language during training to prove that error categories should remain distinct from intent. A good engineer could still make a fatal mistake. Grief did not improve evidence.

Eren shifted the imaging angle. More text resolved.

Load reduction exceeds local spin change. No authorized sink line in maintenance view. Continuity trunk active under emergency mask.

Iona stopped breathing until her body objected.

“Keep imaging,” she said.

The next lines arrived slowly. Sella had taken readings from three independent strain lattices. Nursery rotation continued to fall despite a receiver command that should have preserved it. An unlisted route had exported the difference through Meridian Central. Eleven seconds later, a smaller disturbance came back. Sella called it a returned precursor pulse.

No phrase in the official inquiry mentioned a precursor. No table listed an emergency mask.

Eren lowered his voice. “Could this refer to yesterday?”

“The date does not.”

“I mean the mechanism.”

“Yes.”

Sixteen years apart, Bex had chosen the same act at the same place: preserve an occupied ring by moving part of its physical burden into a destination concealed from the public plan. Yesterday he had called it nine percent of Stage One. The older record named no percentage, only a line that should not have existed.

Iona opened the historical system image from the official inquiry. Her clearance still worked. The maintenance packet listed every public receiver route, ballast command, and operator action for Sella’s last shift. It showed no Continuity trunk. Someone had prepared the evidence by removing the path rather than falsifying its numbers.

“Can you recover her signature?” Iona asked.

Eren adjusted the scan. “The wafer authenticated when she began the note. Final lines may carry the same session key.”

“May?”

“Heat altered the timing layer. I won’t promise what the material has not given us.”

The answer sounded like Sella.

Iona sent a copy of the raw image to Nemi under protected audit protocol. Her deputy answered from Nacre within three minutes, vestibular brace visible behind one ear. Nemi had learned to keep her head still while her eyes moved across data.

“Source?” she asked.

“Nursery maintenance box recovered from debris net Six.”

“Custody?”

“Continuous from retrieval.”

“Has Bex seen it?”

“No.”

Nemi read the visible lines. Her face changed by degrees. “I have the old inquiry archive. Give me the trunk timestamp.”

They aligned Sella’s note with records the Curvature Office had retained but never connected. A power dip appeared at Meridian Central four seconds before the unauthorized route opened. The sink scheduler logged a destination request, then overwrote it under continuity privilege. Receiver diagnostics recorded a phase return eleven seconds later and classified it as mechanical noise.

Eidos had served as Meridian’s assigned sink during that quarter.

“Can we prove destination?” Iona asked.

“Not from the public packet.” Nemi moved to a second archive. “The network retained aggregate sink loads for cap enforcement. They were never part of the casualty inquiry.”

“Because sink activity carried no relevance to operator error.”

“Because someone defined relevance after selecting the finding.”

Nemi found an unexplained Eidos load increase at the same timestamp. Its quantity matched Sella’s missing load within the historical instrument margin. The returned phase matched a smaller fraction, a warning pulse too weak to damage the ring and too structured to fit the noise model available then.

The Olun had warned them sixteen years ago. Sella had noticed. The Republic had placed her body over the evidence and called the shape an operator’s mistake.

Iona gripped the edge of the imaging table. Anger arrived as a demand for movement. There was nowhere useful to put it. Striking the bent box would add damage to an object that had survived enough. Calling Bex before the recovery finished would convert evidence into an argument. She made herself release the table.

“Continue the chronology,” she said.

Nemi’s eyes flicked up. “Iona.”

“Continue.”

They traced the next forty-three minutes.

Sella closed the hidden trunk locally, but the export had already reduced nursery spin. Automatic ballast refused manual commands because Central still reported nominal receiver support. Sella crossed to the mechanical clutch room with two technicians. She bypassed the receiver interlock, released six outer ballast cars, and used their movement to restore enough rotation for the evacuation locks to cycle.

One technician left at Sella’s order to open the children’s shelters. The other died when the first returned pulse shifted a ballast car off its rail. Sella remained at the clutch because releasing it early would let the cars roll inward and strip away the load they had gained.

The official report described that decision as sequence confusion.

A later line emerged from the wafer, scored through by heat but legible.

Unauthorized export caused the loss. Returned load confirms an occupied or active destination. This is not operator error. Keep nursery locks moving.

Iona read the exact words aloud for the custody log.

Eren turned away. Through the window, the broken nursery section completed another slow half rotation.

Surface mapping reached the final intact band. Sella had added a maintenance note rather than a personal message. She listed clutch temperature, ballast positions, remaining seal time, and the names of the technicians sent ahead. The last line retained the same engineering register.

Outbound load entered Eidos before clutch fault. Preserve destination field in inquiry copy.

The signature layer authenticated.

No one spoke. The salvage chronometer advanced. A tug outside fired to nudge wreckage away from Cylinder Six, and the weak deck load changed beneath Iona’s boots.

She saved three copies. One went to Nemi’s isolated store. One entered Meridian’s municipal archive under Eren’s authority. The third remained with the wafer. She did not send it to the Continuity Ministry.

Tavin called from Morrowline. His image showed fatigue and a fresh scrape along his temple.

“We found another shelter list,” he said. “Cylinder Four, maintenance port. Seventy-three marked behind glass, sixty-nine confirmed aboard the cargo tug. Four names unresolved.”

“Send them to me.”

“Why?”

“I’m building a casualty comparison.”

He studied her face. “What did you find?”

“Sella’s last note.”

Tavin waited.

“She did not cause the first Meridian incident. Continuity opened an unauthorized export. She closed it and restored the nursery ring long enough to evacuate.”

“Who hid the note?”

“I do not know yet.”

“Does Bex?”

“I do not know what Bex knew sixteen years ago.” She kept the statement narrower than her anger. “I know his ministry used the same emergency mask yesterday.”

Tavin looked toward someone outside the image. “Pella is awake. She says two fingers constitute a parts shortage, not a disability.”

“Tell her the salvage team found her cutter.”

“Did they find the fingers?”

“No.”

“Then omit that part.”

The channel ended on work, which helped.

Aru’s translation cradle occupied the far end of the salvage bay. Its pressure vessel had been bolted to a cargo pallet after Meridian Central lost power. Thin acceleration actuators moved a dense fluid through branching tubes. Usually the apparatus communicated through patterns too slight to see. Today its fluid shivered continuously.

Iona carried Sella’s recovered chronology to it.

“Aru, can you identify the pressure lineages affected by the export sixteen years ago?”

The actuators paused. When they resumed, the pattern came in separated intervals.

Identification survives in wounds. Completeness does not.

“I am not asking for completeness.”

Humans often ask for a list and later call outside-list nothing.

The answer found its mark. Iona placed the human casualty register on one side of her display and opened a blank field on the other.

“Then give me every surviving identification and mark the limit.”

Pressure ratios accumulated. The cradle translated names poorly because an Olun pressure-name contained location, phase history, and temporary coalition. Human script reduced each to a pronounceable fragment. Aru supplied the fragments with annotations: surviving witness, interrupted memory reef, lineage unformed after repeated disturbance, identity uncertain, several possible overlaps.

Iona did not collapse the uncertainty.

The first human register held Sella Rhyll, Venn Or, Mahir Tal, and seventeen others killed during the old weight-loss incident. The new Meridian register had not closed. Names entered while she watched: lock workers from Cylinder Two, residents from the nursery shelters, a tug deckhand struck by a loose gantry, people who had reached evacuation packets but died when medical load exceeded capacity.

Beside them she placed the Olun entries.

Aru-in-Dark-Cleft, interrupted.

Pressure-Before-Melt, no later continuity found.

The coalition rendered as Holds-Five-Cold-Strata, partly surviving in two witnesses.

A nursery pressure lineage without a stable name, whose formation ceased after the first emergency pulse.

Sella’s register contained occupations and ages. The Olun archive contained gradients, intervals, and absences. Neither fit the other. Iona resisted every field that promised to make them comparable by deleting what differed.

“Read them,” Aru said through the cradle.

The command startled her because Aru rarely used an imperative without a physical warning attached.

“Which archive?”

Both. Alternation prevents one pressure from becoming background.

Iona opened the salvage bay circuit. Eren looked up from the damaged box. Two tug crew stopped securing a net. Beyond the door, evacuees waited for transport out of Meridian with their remaining property tied into blankets.

She began with Sella.

Then she read Aru-in-Dark-Cleft.

Venn Or.

Pressure-Before-Melt.

Mahir Tal.

Holds-Five-Cold-Strata.

The names did not balance. One human did not equal one Olun coalition. A confirmed body did not equal a lost possible lineage. Alternation made no moral arithmetic. It only prevented the human dead from occupying the whole room.

When the old register ended, the new one continued. Iona read until her voice roughened. Eren took the next human name without asking. The translation cradle supplied another pressure-name. A tug mechanic read after him. Nobody called the sequence a memorial. Work continued around it: welders sealed a cracked compartment, a medic checked evacuees for decompression, and salvage radar warned of another fragment crossing the perimeter.

Bex entered the bay on a shuttle from Meridian Central while the reading continued.

He had changed out of his bloodied jacket. A transparent dressing covered his forehead. Two security officers followed at a distance, uncertain whether emergency authority required them to protect him or witness him.

Iona finished the line in front of her before turning.

Bex saw the opened box. “Whose?”

“Sella’s.”

His face lost color.

“You knew there was a suppressed maintenance note,” Iona said.

“I knew the first inquiry had sealed appendices.”

“Did you read them?”

“Not then. I served in Nacre habitat works sixteen years ago.”

“And six years ago?”

“When I took Continuity, I read the sink-response files. Sella’s note did not appear among them.”

“Yesterday you used the route that killed her.”

“I used an emergency mask built into Meridian before my ministry.”

“You concealed Eidos.”

“Yes.”

The admission landed without defense. Iona gave him the raw wafer image. Bex read the visible lines standing beside the bent box. His lips pressed together at This is not operator error. When he reached the last sentence, he set the display down carefully.

“I am sorry,” he said.

“Your sorrow has no authority here.”

“No.”

“Who can open the sealed inquiry appendices?”

“My engineering key and the Curvature Office director’s credential.”

“Order access.”

Bex looked toward the security officers. “Under emergency authority?”

“Under evidence law.”

“That moves more slowly.”

“It also survives your office.”

He entered the order.

The sealed appendices opened in layers. First came photographs of the clutch room taken after recovery: a ballast lever fused in the released position, Sella’s tool tether cut by rescue crews, scoring where the derailed car had crossed the wall. Next came witness statements from nursery staff who said gravity improved after Sella entered the mechanical bay. Their accounts had been classified as contradictory because none of the witnesses could see the receiver console.

A review memorandum followed. Its author, a deputy continuity engineer dead nine years, compared Sella’s note to the emergency trunk record and wrote that an external route had preceded every manual action in the clutch room. Someone above him returned the memorandum with a single instruction: Remove sink speculation from operator finding.

No signature appeared beneath the instruction. The archive did preserve the authorization tier, a predecessor office to Bex’s ministry with the same power to blind maintenance views.

Iona read the witness statements again. A nursery nurse described Sella arriving at the shelter lock with blood on one sleeve and asking whether the children could move. When told the lock still lacked load, Sella went back toward the clutch. A spin technician reported that she refused relief because anyone new would need three minutes to learn which damaged rail still carried force. The official account compressed both statements into one phrase: operator remained at controls despite evacuation order.

“Those witnesses spent sixteen years believing the report had considered them,” Iona said.

Bex stood beside her. “The report did consider them. It treated their evidence as an obstacle.”

“Can you identify who issued the removal instruction?”

“Not from the tier alone. Five officials held it.”

“Then preserve all five names and their access logs.”

He added them to the evidence order. One official still served on the Assembly’s infrastructure committee. Another had retired to a receiver-supported medical spindle. Two had died. The fifth had left no current address after the six-year sink responses were sealed.

The archive had not lost Sella’s truth. It had distributed each piece into a different category until no ordinary inquiry could assemble them. Recovery required a dead ring, a bent box, two credentials, and another catastrophe built from the same concealed route.

Iona removed her Curvature Office credential from her wrist. The strip had granted access to every sink ledger she had trusted, every inquiry category she had defended, and every sealed field someone above her decided she did not need. She placed it beside Sella’s wafer.

Bex watched. “If you resign now, they can exclude you from the inquiry.”

“They already excluded me for sixteen years.”

“You retain authority to compel records.”

“I retain evidence copied under valid custody. Nemi retains hers. Meridian Municipal Maintenance holds another.” She looked at Eren. He nodded. “The Office can decide whether to cooperate with facts after I leave it.”

“What will you do without standing?”

“Continue the work.”

Iona drafted her resignation in six lines. She cited concealment of material evidence, misuse of sink certification, and the Office’s failure to distinguish continuity from truth. She did not cite grief. The record did not need to know why accuracy hurt.

Before sending it, she copied the recovery authorization attached to her credential. Salvage operations could continue under Meridian municipal authority and convoy necessity. Eren signed her onto his team as a forensic contractor with no pay code and no institutional rank.

“Welcome to municipal maintenance,” he said.

“Does the municipality still exist?”

“Enough to owe me three months’ wages.”

She sent the resignation.

Her credential went dark. Doors, archives, and command channels vanished from its permissions one after another. The loss felt physical, a reduction in reach measured by each gray icon. Nemi’s isolated store remained connected. So did the translation cradle. Tavin’s convoy channel stayed open because he had granted it personally.

Outside, a salvage tug caught the maintenance deck fragment in a net and drew it toward Cylinder Six. The box would go with it. Sella’s wafer would go to three archives. Nothing would bring back the clutch room, the nursery ring, or the pressure histories struck below Eidos.

Iona picked up the next unresolved human name and placed it beside the next Olun pressure-name. Then she read both into the record under no office but her own.

Interlude III — The Archive of Impacts

Activation transcript, Nacre Receiver One. Network Year 0.

“Proof mass steady.”

“Local curvature increased by commanded interval.”

“Destination response?”

“Sink Eidos Four accepts full disturbance. No reflected energy above threshold.”

“Record successful exchange.”

A technician laughed after the formal line. Someone asked whether a coin would fall faster. The proof mass crossed its optical cage, the receiver warmed by two degrees, and a new entry appeared in the Republic’s engineering register: Weight relocated without propellant expenditure.

Far beneath Eidos Four’s ice, no register opened. A pressure history formed instead. The first pulse compressed a superfluid boundary that had held stable across nine seasonal tides. Three local memory structures sheared. A temporary speaker assembled long enough to compare the impact with moon strain, found no matching orbit, and dissolved before it could preserve the question.

Twenty-four years later, sink certification acquired legal language.

The Republic survey vessel Aster Vale crossed Eidos at speed. Its report included atmospheric spectroscopy, radio silence, thermal imaging, and two gravimetric passes optimized for liquid oceans. The second pass detected a persistent discontinuity below the pressure floor. Survey software labeled it mineral phase separation.

The certification board copied one sentence into its public finding: No biosignature consistent with inhabited use.

An Olun archive from the same passage preserved no vessel. Light and radio did not reach its depth with useful shape. The gravimetric scan did. It passed through the ice as a gentle ordered pressure, too small to injure and too brief to answer. Four pressure-names aligned to it. One proposed intentional origin. Two classified it as distant fracture. The fourth kept both interpretations and marked a watch interval in the upper strata.

At Network Year 31, a maintenance child on Leto Cylinder wrote a school answer in green stylus:

Receivers let habitats adjust gravity without wasting fuel. The extra force goes to empty systems where it cannot hurt anything. A sink works because nobody lives there.

Her teacher marked the answer correct and circled without wasting fuel for spelling.

During that school hour, three routine clearings reached Eidos. The first shifted a moonlet’s pericenter by less than a finger’s width in human measure. The second crossed a memory reef already weakened by earlier pulses. The third completed the fracture. Histories from six pressure lineages survived in neighboring ice, but their order did not. Later Olun speakers could recover a child-pressure, a migration toward warmth, and a warning about the next tide. They could not determine which memory had preceded which.

The human ledger retained all three clearings to nine decimal places.

Network Year 58 produced the first industrial emergency export. A refinery spindle in the Corve system lost a flywheel bearing while eleven thousand workers occupied its outer decks. Operators used Reciprocal Mass Exchange to hold local load for four minutes and forty seconds. The incident report credited the receiver with preventing mass casualties. Its appendix listed damaged machinery, medical outcomes, and deferred fuel cost.

No field accepted a remote casualty.

In Eidos, the emergency pulse crossed two ice bodies. One pressure archive recorded sudden compression followed by a partial rebound. Another preserved an absence: a mature coalition had begun assembling for seasonal exchange, then failed to answer any later call. The Olun could not know whether every component ended. They marked the site with a pressure interval meaning do not build continuity across this break.

Human sink accounting called the same event a successful full acceptance.

Sixteen years before the present audit, Meridian opened an unauthorized emergency route.

Sella Rhyll’s maintenance note captured the event from inside the nursery ring:

Load reduction exceeds local spin change. No authorized sink line in maintenance view. Continuity trunk active under emergency mask. Returned load confirms an occupied or active destination. This is not operator error. Keep nursery locks moving.

The official inquiry omitted the note. Its public conclusion assigned premature clutch release to operator error and counted twenty human dead. Meridian repaired the nursery section, renewed its safety certificate, and painted animals around the children’s decks.

Eidos retained the returned precursor in several forms. A shallow archive represented it as a probe toward the source. Deeper histories described a reflex after unexpected compression. One pressure-name claimed the return had carried structure. Others heard only fracture. The disagreement persisted because the next human pulses arrived before the disputed interval could settle.

Six years before Meridian fell, Republic instruments detected organized replies.

A Curvature Office classifier saved the sequences under structured sink residuals. Prime ratios, controlled pauses, and amplitude mirroring appeared across fourteen sessions. Analysts proposed unknown dark-lattice behavior, concealed human machinery, or deliberate source action. The Continuity Ministry sealed the packet. Clearing schedules continued.

Olun pressure archives preserved those sessions as attempts rather than replies. One faction repeated the impact spacing at reduced force. Another inserted long intervals copied from undamaged ice. A third returned small portions of each pulse, testing whether the source changed behavior. When clearings continued, several lineages concluded that the sender could not perceive. Several concluded that it perceived and refused. Neither conclusion achieved unanimity.

On Day 0, a Perihelion audit froze one clearing.

The human file began with an apple, 0.0037 g of unsettled curvature, and a blank destination column. It continued through hospital delay, propellant cost, and a sequence of prime intervals received while the local device remained in standby. Iona Rhyll entered unassigned reciprocal load into a codebook that lacked the phrase.

An Eidos archive marked the same pause as the first interruption in 112 years of repeated impacts. Pressure-names assembled around the coupled channel. They sent a sequence chosen for divisibility by no smaller interval. The source altered its behavior. For the first time, both archives preserved not only an event but a change attributed to the other side.

The records multiplied after contact. Human files acquired translations, casualty estimates, and arguments over seventeen billion dependent lives. Olun strata acquired human dissent patterns, localization trials, and proof that some senders spent their own momentum rather than exporting it. Neither archive became singular. Republic ministries concealed data that maintenance workers copied. Olun factions returned wounds that other pressure-names asked them to suspend.

Then Meridian opened the emergency route again.

Day 19, Morrowline cargo record: Twelve hundred evacuees embarked against rated capacity of seven hundred. Reserve water vented to create shelter volume. Chief engineer Pella Jor injured during collar release. Nursery-ring transfer incomplete.

Day 19, Meridian Municipal Maintenance: Receiver hub lost. Eleven inhabited cylinders abandoned or irretrievably displaced. Nursery ring separated across its repaired section after tidal load exceeded frame tolerance. Final confirmed shelter census unavailable. Record remains open.

Within Eidos, the returned diversion struck a coalition already aligned for restraint. Some histories broke. Others joined the hard return and increased it. Aru-in-Pressure lost coherence across two local strata while carrying evidence of human evacuation. A surviving pressure-name recorded children moving through unstable load, not as absolution, but as proof that the source contained opposed actions.

On Day 20, Iona placed Sella Rhyll beside Aru-in-Dark-Cleft. A Meridian technician added Venn Or. The translation cradle supplied Pressure-Before-Melt. Human names continued beyond the old casualty inquiry. Olun names continued beyond defensible translation. Blank fields remained marked as unknown rather than zero.

The two archives received their final entry from different instruments.

Meridian salvage radar tracked the broken nursery ring until its fragments crossed below recoverable orbit. Eidos pressure histories tracked the returned pulse until its last gradient dispersed through damaged ice. Human recorders entered the time. Olun witnesses preserved the interval.

Both left the event open.

Chapter 18 — The Third Account

Iona began by cutting Meridian’s evacuation board into three pieces.

The board had hung outside Morrowline’s cargo lock during the rescue. One side still carried chalk capacities for children, critical patients, and ambulatory evacuees. Coolant had blurred the lower rows. Tavin’s crew no longer needed it for loading because the survivors had transferred to Nacre packets, but nobody had erased the names.

Iona laid the pieces on three cargo tables. The ship held one fifth standard gravity from spin, enough to keep the boards down and weak enough that a careless sleeve could move them.

Nemi watched through a wall display from Nacre. “You resigned yesterday.”

“I remember.”

“You are using a Republic cargo registry, Ministry survival models, Curvature Office sink records, Meridian municipal credentials, and an Olun translation cradle attached to a freighter.”

“Yes.”

“Good. I wanted the jurisdiction clear.”

Tavin entered carrying a fuel wafer and a cup with a lid. “It is not.”

“That also helps,” Nemi said.

Iona wrote three headings.

REPUBLIC SURVIVAL

OLUN LOSS

LOCAL PHYSICAL COST

“These are the three accounts,” she said. “No conversion between them. No net total.”

Tavin set the fuel wafer beneath the third heading. “What does a negotiation do with numbers that refuse to become one number?”

“It stops pretending one subtraction can answer every claim.”

“Politicians will love that.”

“They have had simpler figures for 112 years.”

Nemi added a fourth participant to the secure channel. Pori Sal had retired from comet mass accounting four years earlier, after Continuity transferred her away from sink analysis. Her hair had gone white. The orange cord around Nemi's stylus carried the knots that had survived containment.

“Seven years ago I asked for a deep survey of Eidos,” Sal said. “Today I am not asking you to treat that request as prophecy. I am asking you to preserve the interval tables that made the request reasonable.”

She deposited her original checksum and twelve clearing traces into the public workspace. The old warning did not solve the present account. It did establish where deliberate uncertainty had begun, and it gave the Olun-loss table a human custody chain no ministry could quietly relabel.

The first table began with the Republic’s fixed population: 31.4 billion across forty-three systems. Seventeen billion lived in structures whose orbit, spin, or long-term load depended on RME. Bex’s continuity model treated that dependency as a single exposed population. Iona opened its assumptions one by one.

Some habitats would lose air within hours after abrupt receiver shutdown because bearings or seals required controlled load. Others could operate for months while spending fuel and reducing capacity. Stillness already survived without a receiver, though its people paid through slow spin, ballast mass, cramped construction, and constant tug work. A farm torus had localized successfully and lost a growing season. The same label, dependent, covered immediate death and expensive adaptation.

“Separate time,” Iona said.

Nemi divided the model into ten-hour, ten-day, one-year, and generational windows. Tavin added mobility: fixed cylinders, towable stations, settlements with detachable population modules, and ships that could move mass but not house everyone attached to it.

They found the first dangerous assumption after eleven minutes. Continuity had counted every person registered to a receiver-supported structure as a likely abrupt-shutdown death.

“Meridian proves that overstates and understates at once,” Tavin said. “We evacuated people the model assigned to a lost ring. We also lost people after they reached craft because the craft lacked medical load.”

Iona created two fields: structural exposure and evacuation exposure. Neither counted as a death forecast. Each carried a range linked to a date and a named physical failure.

Bex joined by secure channel. He had not been invited, but his emergency authority still opened convoy policy circuits.

“You removed the continuity total,” he said.

“I disaggregated it.”

“The Assembly needs a number.”

“It needs several honest ranges.”

“A range can conceal a preferred endpoint as easily as a total.”

“Then every range carries its assumptions.” Iona turned the display toward him. “Your model counts a person in Stillness and an infant in an unsupported nursery lock under the same dependency class.”

“Because both live after the network fails only if local systems perform.”

“That describes everyone aboard this ship.”

Bex leaned closer. Fatigue had deepened the lines beneath his eyes. “What minimum do you defend?”

“Seventeen billion exposed to disruption. Not seventeen billion certain dead. We estimate consequences by habitat, time, evacuation capacity, and available conventional force.”

“If the Olun demand immediate correction, you will have hours to explain those distinctions.”

“Then help us make them legible now.”

He remained on the channel.

Before moving to fleet costs, Iona tested the structure against one inhabited place. She chose Nacre’s north residential band because its operators had already reduced receiver support and because Keir’s public spin crew could answer without Ministry filtration.

Keir appeared from a ballast control room with four people working behind him. Chalk lines covered the wall, each one connecting a water tank to a rail position. The spindle’s slow rotation pulled his harness toward the outer deck.

“Give me the consequence of zero receiver draw now,” Iona said.

“Now means which minute?” he asked.

“Exactly.”

Keir checked the local schedule. “For the next forty minutes, we hold point eight standard in residential decks and point nine in two hospital sectors. North bearing temperature rises. Water pressure falls above Deck Ninety because the pumps were sized with compensation. Nobody loses air.”

“And after forty minutes?”

“We move another eighteen tanks outward or reduce spin load in the hospital sectors. The tanks block two public corridors while traveling. We need every rail crew awake.”

“Ten hours?”

“Bearing inspection, pump replacements, and no cargo rotation. Food packets come through the south spine. We can keep living here.”

“Ten days?”

Keir looked toward a spin supervisor. The woman stepped into view, gave her name as Varo Sen, and pulled up a maintenance forecast.

“Without receiver smoothing, bearing wear triples at present mass,” Varo said. “We either shed population, replace the north race, or slow the spindle. Replacement takes a tug lift we do not currently have. Slowing means less load across every deck, including surgery and childhood development wards.”

Bex asked, “Minimum evacuation?”

“Unknown until we inspect the race after a full day,” Varo replied.

“Give a planning range.”

“I can give conditions. If vibration stays below this line, we relocate no one. If it crosses amber, we move the outer four decks. If it crosses red, we clear the north band. A population number without tomorrow’s bearing trace only rewards whoever chooses the preferred forecast.”

Iona entered all three branches. The Republic account now carried observable triggers instead of a single casualty expectation. Keir added the available shelters, train capacity under weak load, and the number of rail crews who could move ballast without receiver support. Bex supplied hospital demand from the Ministry model. Its patient registry ran two days behind.

“Your air inventory has more recent hospital data,” Iona told Varo.

“It has occupied beds. Not diagnoses.”

“Use occupied beds for lift mass. Let medical staff classify care needs separately.”

A nurse joined from Nacre Hospital Sector Two. She identified six procedures that could shift to mechanical schedule, three that required stable load, and a neonatal unit that could survive reduced gravity but not repeated changes. The account widened again. No single field could represent hospital dependence without losing the distinction between lightness and variation.

Keir moved a chalk line while they spoke. “Local cost includes the water we cannot use while tanks sit on outer rails. It also includes access. Two residential decks have only one open corridor during transfer.”

Tavin asked, “What would another tug change?”

“We could bring replacement bearing segments before the race crosses amber. It would also let us move ballast without closing both corridors.”

“Fuel requirement?”

Keir named a mass and route. Tavin compared it with Morrowline’s reserve. The voyage could be flown, but not alongside the planned Nacre flywheel transfer. One ship could reduce two risks at different times, never both at once.

Iona linked the tradeoff across tables. Republic survival gained a narrower exposure branch if the tug went to the bearing. Local physical cost then gained a delayed flywheel transfer elsewhere on the spindle. Olun loss changed only if either task used hidden receiver support.

Aru sent a brief pressure pattern through the cradle.

This location begins naming destination of force.

Keir heard the translation. “Does that count for anything?”

“It changes what can be negotiated,” Iona said. “It does not cancel prior harm.”

“Good. Cancellation sounds like a Ministry service.”

Bex accepted the line without visible reaction.

When the Nacre test ended, Varo kept the three account fields on her local board. She would update them after the bearing inspection. The exercise had not predicted who died. It had named what crews could observe, which action followed each threshold, and where every action moved cost.

Tavin opened Morrowline’s fuel record. The convoy had burned reserve propellant on the sunward flight, vented water to create shelter volume, damaged three thruster collars, and consumed service parts intended for a year. Those costs seemed small beside Meridian until Tavin connected each one to future work the ship could no longer perform.

“Reserve fuel had destinations before we spent it,” he said. “A hospital spindle lift at Orison. Flywheel transfer for Nacre’s south bearing. Two ice hauls to Stillness. We saved people at Meridian by borrowing capacity from those jobs.”

“Can they be reassigned?” Iona asked.

“Some. The spindle lift has a six-week window. Miss it and they brake the theatre mechanically. The Nacre flywheel cannot wait that long.”

She recorded schedules rather than assigning monetary value. Local physical cost included fuel mass, tug hours, removed ballast, reduced habitat volume, food cycles, medical risk during relocation, and structures deliberately abandoned. It also included who performed the work.

Pella arrived from engineering with her left hand sealed in a regenerative brace. Two empty channels ended below the first knuckles. She placed a tool authorization on the table.

“What category holds fingers?” she asked.

“None yet,” Iona said.

“Then your account is short.”

Iona added injury, rehabilitation, and lost skilled capacity.

“Do not call the last one lost,” Pella said. “I can still supervise people with inferior hands.”

Tavin looked at her brace. “Your generosity restores morale.”

“I require a new cutter grip and six weeks before pressure work. Put that in. Also put the assistant who ran my engine room while Sive had me tied down. He did not become free labor because my name occupies the casualty field.”

Iona entered both.

Pella tapped the Meridian board beneath the headings. “Count replacements by object, not price. A receiver coil can be fabricated. Nursery soil takes years. A pressure door takes weeks. Someone’s left shoe remains a shoe, but they may need it tonight.”

After she left, Bex said, “If you enumerate every consequence, negotiation never begins.”

“It begins when each side can see what an offer moves.”

“That standard exceeds any treaty the Republic has written.”

“The Republic has never negotiated with the destination column.”

Aru’s cradle activated at the second table. Fluid moved through its clear branches in pulses that made the cargo deck seem briefly steeper. The translated voice came from no speaker’s location.

You prepare count of ended Olun.

“I prepare an account of loss,” Iona said. “I cannot count ended individuals under a form of identity that changes by coalition.”

Correct refusal. Incomplete refusal. Ended structure has measures.

The display filled with pressure intervals. Aru distinguished a temporary speaker, a persistent memory reef, a seasonal lineage, and a possible coalition prevented from forming. Human casualty categories fit none. The Olun also lacked a universal unit. Different ice worlds preserved identity through different pressure regimes. A fracture that erased a shallow speaker might leave its component histories elsewhere. A thermal shift could preserve structure while making it unreachable.

Nemi asked, “What does absence mean in your archive?”

Aru returned three patterns before translating.

Expected answer fails across known call. Expected structure fails across mapped stratum. Expected successor fails after formation interval. Each absence differs.

“Can absence prove death?”

No. Presence later can disprove some endings. Continued absence increases boundary, not certainty.

Iona wrote the phrase under OLUN LOSS: boundary of possible survival.

Aru struck the cradle with a sharp pulse.

Do not make our unknown smaller by naming boundary.

She deleted it.

“What would you preserve?”

The response came as a pressure sequence that the translation software first rendered room without return. Aru corrected it twice.

Mapped expectation, failed continuity, search extent, confidence. Keep four.

Iona built four fields. Every estimate would show what pattern had been expected, what continuity failed, how far witnesses had searched, and how much confidence survived. Confirmed interruption occupied one range. No later response after repeated calls occupied another. Destroyed memory reef with fragments preserved elsewhere required a third description. Possible lineages prevented from forming remained possibilities, not retroactive people and not zero.

The work slowed because each translated term carried physical history. Nemi proposed lineage-equivalent as a unit. Aru rejected it; coalitions varied too widely. Bex asked whether integrated archive volume could provide a lower bound. Aru replied that deep histories stored more pressure duration than shallow ones without containing more persons. Tavin suggested event classes instead of totals.

That held.

They arranged Olun losses by event: routine clearing, repeated clearing across one formation interval, emergency export, memory-reef fracture, returned pulse, and habitat-scale disruption. For each event, Aru supplied surviving witnesses and explicit uncertainty. Iona linked the human sink ledger to the pressure archive without claiming either could complete the other.

The first defensible lower statement took an hour: human debt pulses had interrupted named Olun continuities across multiple Eidos bodies. The evidence proved more than damage to inert geology. It did not yield a population count.

Bex read it. “The public will call this evasive.”

“The public has been given exact lies,” Nemi said. “Uncertainty may look evasive because it has never received an official typeface.”

“What can the hard Olun faction negotiate without a total?” Bex asked.

Aru’s fluid accelerated.

Cessation does not require count. Restitution requires shape of wound. Immediate return faction uses completed human load as measure. It does not require our dead to become numbers.

Iona understood. The hard faction treated accumulated exported disturbance as a physical debt to return, independent of casualty accounting. Human negotiators kept asking for a death total because they wanted payment to resemble damages in a court. The Olun demand began with force already delivered.

“Then our accounts need a fourth link,” she said. “Not a fourth account. A trace between exported load and each documented impact class.”

She drew it without conversion. A clearing quantity could connect to a known pressure fracture. It could also connect to failed continuity with wider uncertainty. The same clearing had enabled a local hospital adjustment or cargo orbit. Physical cause linked the sides even when moral units did not.

By the end of the first work period, the cargo tables had filled with records. Nothing balanced. Republic survival showed exposure windows and local failure modes. Olun loss showed documented interruptions, mapped absence, and limits on inference. Local physical cost showed the fuel, capacity, relocation, injury, and abandonment required to stop exporting harm.

Iona ordered no summary total.

A public-policy aide from Bex’s office requested one anyway. “Negotiators need to compare offers,” she said over channel. “If we cannot say whether a staged plan saves more than it costs, the Assembly cannot authorize it.”

“More of what?” Iona asked.

“Lives.”

“Whose lives, measured how?”

“All affected persons.”

“A possible Olun lineage has no direct conversion to a human evacuation death. A lost growing season does not equal Pella’s fingers. Refusing conversion does not refuse comparison. It requires the negotiators to state which harm they accept and why.”

The aide looked toward Bex. He said, “Keep the accounts separate.”

It marked his first public refusal of his own preferred model.

The second work period tested an offer. Immediate total receiver shutdown entered the Republic table. Within hours, certain habitat classes lost seal stability and orbital control. The casualty range widened rapidly because evacuation capacity fell below exposed population. Olun exported load ceased, but abrupt local corrections risked new pulses through uncontrolled channels. The local-cost table showed insufficient tugs, fuel, and shelter.

A continuation offer entered next. Republic disruption narrowed. Olun impact classes remained active. Local expenditure stayed deceptively low because Eidos absorbed it. Aru rejected the offer before translation completed.

Finally they entered staged localization by system. Receivers would reduce on public schedules. Tugs and ballast crews would take local force. Marginal installations would evacuate where physical support could not continue. No secret route could transfer an overrun. Each stage carried a range for human exposure, a linked reduction in Olun impact, and a list of concrete local costs.

The plan did not yet choose which installations to abandon. That decision belonged to negotiation and public authority. The accounts made it impossible to describe abandonment as a rounding error.

Aru reviewed the structure through three long pressure sequences.

Survival account does not erase wound. Wound account does not command impossible motion. Motion account shows whether promise has body.

Nemi saved the translation, then saved the raw sequence beside it.

“Will your chorus receive this?” Iona asked.

Several receive. Several refuse translated form. Hard return faction will test for concealed path.

“They should.”

Testing may damage.

“Then specify the test before we agree.”

The cradle held a low gradient. Iona had learned that such pauses could contain disagreement rather than silence.

This request has negotiation shape, Aru said.

They prepared two packets. The human packet went to every Assembly system, receiver crew council, convoy captain, and public archive that could carry it. The Olun packet used dark-lattice modulation through the cradle. It contained fewer human words and more linked pressure traces. Both included Sella’s note, Meridian’s incomplete evacuation register, and the unaltered uncertainty fields.

Bex asked to sign as Minister of Continuity.

Iona said, “Sign the Republic survival inputs you supplied. Not the whole.”

Nemi signed the sink-record extraction. Tavin signed fuel and vessel capacity. Pella added her tool authorization and rehabilitation estimate. Eren Vass signed Meridian custody. Aru marked the Olun structure with a pressure sequence no human credential could imitate.

Iona signed no office. She identified herself as compiler and listed every source of authority separately.

The packets left Morrowline together. One traveled by Republic channels toward forty-three systems. The other entered the same lattice that had carried hidden force for 112 years, now bearing a proposal with no force attached.

Minutes later, Assembly procedure accepted the human packet for emergency negotiation. The cradle answered with three distinct Olun acknowledgments and two refusals. None accepted payment. None returned a pulse.

On the cargo tables, the three accounts remained physically separate. Between them, Iona laid a blank negotiation sheet and fixed its corners beneath Pella’s damaged cutter grip.

Chapter 19 — The Planetary Strike

The forward yoke missed its capture mark by eleven centimeters.

Tavin watched the stripped flywheel housing drift past the green brackets on his display. Twelve thousand tonnes of alloy moved without hurry, close enough to Nacre Receiver Nine that one bad impulse could buckle a service collar. The Morrowline held the housing on four cables while the station turned beneath it. Three tug pilots waited for his correction. None of them had used curvature compensation since the receiver crews walked out.

“Port cluster, two percent for six seconds,” he said. “Yoke Three, pay out half a meter. Do not chase the mark.”

Pella Jor answered from engineering. “Port cluster is yours. It would like you to know it resents restraint.”

“Tell it resentment saves fuel.”

“It says captains always discover thrift after engineers lose fingers.”

Her left glove had two empty stalls pinned flat against the palm. Pella could still run six thruster clusters by touch, though every hard burn pulled her healing hand against its brace. Tavin had ordered her off the deck twice since Meridian. She had treated both orders as requests for technical review.

The housing slowed. Cable loads crossed, settled, then balanced within the old Navy tolerance Tavin still trusted more than any Republic certification. He brought the mass over the capture brackets and let the station's mechanical clamps close around it.

Receiver Nine shuddered when the clamps took weight. Across its outer hull, a row of work lamps flashed twice. Strike signal. No hidden export. No unlogged sink. No force without a named destination.

Haru Den looked up from navigation. “Security cutter on an intercept from the inner cordon. Eight minutes.”

“Did they answer our berth notice?”

“They answered with a seizure warrant.”

Tavin released the yoke cables. “Same bureaucratic instinct, sharper typography.”

The warrant filled his command pane. Under emergency authority, the Continuity Ministry claimed the Morrowline, its remaining fuel, every detachable yoke, and the housing they had just delivered. The stated purpose involved preservation of critical receiver infrastructure. The attached operations order directed Receiver Nine to resume clearing through Eidos before the next orbital correction window.

Below the legal block sat Bex's engineering signature.

Tavin read it once. He had learned aboard Navy tugs that an order could contain several different things: a desired result, an assigned risk, an unstated casualty list, and a name placed at the bottom to make all three look singular. The warrant wanted the housing because its mass could stabilize the receiver station without exported curvature. It wanted the ship because the Ministry lacked enough conventional lift to move that mass. It wanted the fuel because every honest option consumed something visible.

Sive An secured a medical cabinet behind the bridge. “If they board, do I list our wounded as cargo or obstruction?”

“Crew,” Tavin said.

“That category has lost legal precision.”

Receiver Nine's dockmaster opened a local channel. Lio San appeared with the washed complexion of someone who had slept under work lights. A strike band crossed his coverall in white paint. Behind him, mechanics were bolting braces over a load manifold instead of operating its clearing valves.

“We saw the warrant,” Lio said. “The cutter told us to uncouple your yokes.”

“Can the housing carry you through the correction window?”

“If we spin it up. We need forty minutes and seven tonnes of maneuvering propellant.”

“You have twenty-eight tonnes in the dock reserve.”

“Ministry lockout took the pumps.”

Tavin examined the station schematic. Receiver Nine served six spindle neighborhoods, two hospitals, and a water refinery. With its workers refusing Eidos assignments, local load would drift outside tolerance in three hours. The housing could absorb enough angular momentum to hold the service collar level, but only if the station moved it along a rail and spent fuel bringing it to speed.

“Can you cut a manual feed from our aft tank?” Lio asked.

“Not before the cutter arrives.”

Pella shifted the engineering plot onto Tavin's pane. A maintenance purge line ran from the Morrowline's auxiliary tank to the yoke manifold. Its couplings carried coolant in ordinary service. They could pass propellant if someone replaced two seals and accepted a pressure loss.

“I can give them nine tonnes,” Pella said. “Eight reaches their tank. One becomes a lesson about using the wrong pipe.”

“Time?”

“Sixteen minutes if the dock sends hands.”

The security cutter reached weapons range in six.

Tavin keyed the cutter. Its captain appeared without a uniform jacket, a concession to long emergency shifts. Commander Vey Orra had served two years below Tavin in the Republic Navy and once flown a tether rescue through a dust ring with three dead attitude jets. Her face carried no pleasure at finding him on the other side of an order.

“Captain Serr, stand down your drives,” she said. “Prepare for Ministry seizure.”

“We're attached to a civilian receiver.”

“You have a detachable manifold. Detach it.”

“The receiver will cross load limits before your prize crew can move the housing.”

“I have engineers aboard.”

“Do they know the strike plant?”

A pause answered him.

Orra reduced her voice. “Bex says hidden clearing for one more cycle prevents four habitat evacuations.”

“Workers in twelve systems received the same assurance last cycle.”

“Those systems still have gravity.”

“Meridian had gravity until someone used Eidos as a private answer.”

The cutter's range marker turned amber. Orra could disable the Morrowline's maneuvering jets without breaching the hull. Her gunners knew exactly where to place the shot. Tavin also knew their capacitor recovery time, because he had approved the cutter class while still in service.

He sent her the local mass model. “If you fire, our cables slack. The housing rolls six meters along the collar. Your seizure gets a station fracture.”

“You released the cables.”

“Four magnetic shoes remain engaged.”

That claim was almost true. Two shoes held. A third reported intermittent contact, and the fourth existed only as a warm icon Pella had painted onto the diagnostic layer.

Orra studied the model. “You are using inhabited infrastructure as cover.”

“I am finishing a delivery.”

“To strikers.”

“To people keeping six neighborhoods from drifting while they refuse an unauthorized sink.”

“Your distinction may matter at trial.”

“It matters now.”

Tavin closed the channel and ordered Pella to rig the purge line. Receiver mechanics came across the service bridge carrying replacement seals, a hand pump, and a coil of cable. No one gave a speech. One mechanic locked boots to the yoke, another stripped insulation from a valve actuator, while a third painted a white strike bar on the borrowed propellant line.

The cutter held at five kilometers. Its targeting beam touched the Morrowline's stern and remained there.

For sixteen minutes, Tavin had to keep twelve thousand tonnes apparently dependent on his ship. Haru pulsed the attitude jets in uneven pairs, making the housing's residual movement look less stable than it had become. Pella vented a thread of coolant past the failing shoe to produce a convincing thermal bloom. Sive moved the crew away from the likely boarding locks, then strapped herself beside the casualty rack.

A pressure alarm sounded in engineering.

“Seal Two is biting sideways,” Pella said. “Flow at thirty percent.”

“Can you hold it?”

“With my hand, yes. With optimism, no.”

Tavin checked the cutter. A launch bay opened along its belly.

“Boarding skiff away,” Haru said. “Ninety seconds.”

Lio's mechanics forced the second seal into its collar. Propellant began crossing the improvised line. Eight tonnes would not arrive in time. Four might. Receiver Nine needed seven.

Tavin opened every local work channel the station had given him. “Dock reserve pumps are Ministry locked. Who has an independent transfer cart?”

Answers arrived from places not listed in the operations plan. A water tender carried half a tonne for emergency attitude control. Two garbage scows had another tonne between them. A funeral carrier offered its entire landing reserve, then withdrew the offer when Sive pointed out that it still had to land. A school ferry held three hundred kilograms and a pilot unwilling to surrender any while children remained aboard.

“Keep yours,” Tavin told the ferry. “Everyone else, match Receiver Nine's approach code. Lio will sequence you.”

The first garbage scow detached from the refinery spoke. Its drive plume flickered yellow from poor mixture, but it made the crossing. The water tender followed under manual guidance. On the traffic plot, six unrelated craft altered course toward one transfer point while Republic control ordered them back to assigned lanes.

Orra called again. “Disperse those vessels.”

“They heard the same warrant.”

“You are creating a collision field.”

“I sent you the approach sequence.”

“You sent it after they moved.”

“They moved before command found a reason to let them.”

The boarding skiff stopped outside the yoke's hazard zone. Its pilot saw the mass housing, the crossing utility craft, and a propellant hose never rated for its current use. Boarding would require cutting through working people while the cutter recorded every angle.

By the twentieth minute, Receiver Nine held 6.2 tonnes. The improvised line shook against its cable restraints. Pella reduced flow before Tavin could order it, saving the second seal from tearing free.

“We can give them six-point-eight,” she said. “After that, we keep enough for one hard departure or two polite ones.”

“Give six-point-eight.”

“We may need the hard departure.”

“We already spent the polite one.”

The station spun up the housing. Its rail motors drew power from three neighborhoods at once, darkening every fourth corridor in a scheduled wave. Hospital loads remained protected. The water refinery stopped for nine minutes. Residents shifted into warm rooms and tied cabinets shut as felt gravity changed by hundredths.

Tavin watched cost become motion on the board. Local payment lacked the clean disappearance of a clearing. It arrived as empty tanks, delayed water, cold corridors, and a flywheel housing dragged into service years before its bearings were ready. No distant pressure archive had to absorb any of it.

At 6.6 tonnes transferred, a priority packet broke across the work channels. Not from Ministry control. The sender used a receiver-maintenance key from Borun System, twenty-one light-years beyond any ordinary conversation with Nacre. Its timestamp preceded Meridian's fall by years. The workers there had created a dormant strike protocol after the first structured replies disappeared from public maintenance records. Iona's raw-data release had supplied the trigger they lacked.

The packet contained no current voice. It carried procedures: refuse unconsented sink assignments, preserve hospital and nursery loads, publish local fuel margins, and coordinate mass transfers without Continuity routing. Its checksum had already been countersigned by receiver crews at Kestrel Reach, Lumen, Sere, and seven Nacre habitats.

Haru leaned closer. “This has been waiting in maintenance caches.”

“For a truth no one wanted to name,” Tavin said.

A second packet surfaced from Lumen. Helix Quay workers had adapted the protocol after the repossession. A third came from Stillness, offering ballast tables and conventional spin schedules. Old messages, local decisions, delayed proofs. The strike had not crossed interstellar distance in four days. It had grown from buried suspicion in many places and recognized itself when the concealed records opened.

Another Nacre site broke into the channel before the pattern finished resolving. Refinery Spindle Four had begun a receiver slowdown with twelve thousand workers aboard. Its ore trains ran on a central load rail. Once local field support dropped below seventy percent, loaded cars would press unevenly against the ring and push its center of mass off the bearing line.

“We need nine thousand tonnes sunward within fifty minutes,” said the refinery coordinator. “Continuity has two mass barges locked at our outer berth. Their captains will not release without a routing code.”

Tavin pulled the refinery model beside Receiver Nine's plot. The Morrowline could move the requested mass, but not while attached here. Even after release, its remaining propellant allowed one transfer. A second would leave them unable to brake at a safe port. He marked those limits on the shared table.

“Do not wait for us,” he said. “What can move inside your ring?”

“Three ore trains. Two are loaded.”

“Where?”

“Outer quadrant, coming spinward.”

“Reverse one loaded train. Stop the other at quarter circumference. Empty cars go sunward at maximum safe speed.”

The coordinator hesitated. “That closes all smelter feed.”

“You asked for mass, not production.”

“We lose twelve hours of heat.”

“Put twelve hours in the strike account. Is the centerline responding?”

Numbers replaced the coordinator's face. Haru entered the train movements and watched the projected bearing error shrink. Not enough, but the refinery gained twenty-three minutes.
