The delegate stared at the draft. “The Olun offered seventy-two hours.”

“They suspended repossession for that interval. They did not require complete localization within it. They require no hidden export and visible reduction.”

Vela submitted a twenty-year plan. Engineers exposed its first deception within minutes: early reductions came from transferring residents to towers whose receiver loads were excluded from the baseline. Iona returned the plan without accusation. The public comment pane supplied enough.

A revised version counted every inhabited structure. It closed five upper districts during the first three years, reinforced fourteen others, and ended new high-load construction immediately. Vela's delegate signed provisionally after inserting relocation rights, including equivalent housing volume rather than cash compensation alone.

Case by case, the Compact acquired machinery.

Every receiver needed a public debt ledger tied to strain measurements, not commanded values. Every clearing required a named destination and a consent record available before transmission. Local cessation plans had to list fuel, mass, evacuation capacity, deadlines, and failure conditions. Independent crews could refuse any route that diverged from the published plan. Emergency actions expired after one physical interval and could not create a hidden sink assignment.

Nemi built the audit standard while delegates argued. She used the 0.0037 g discrepancy from Perihelion as the minimum demonstration: a ledger had to expose unsettled load even when no destination field existed. Blank entries would count as failed certification, not harmless absence.

An industrial delegate objected that old receivers could not produce such detail. “You are outlawing installed hardware by documentation.”

“Installed hardware concealed the return phase,” Nemi said. “Upgrade the instruments or reduce operation to what you can measure.”

“Upgrades consume rare strain lattices.”

“List them under transition cost.”

The delegate did. Scarcity appeared across eleven systems. Nacre had enough lattices for its own receivers or for six smaller systems, not both. Bex proposed Ministry allocation. Strike workers objected to restoring his control through parts.

Iona asked receiver crews to publish priority criteria. Hospitals, nursery structures, evacuation routes, and habitats with the least mechanical margin came first. Commercial load relief came last. Nacre surrendered half its reserve stock. Four systems offered manufacturing capacity in return for public tooling designs rather than exclusive licenses.

The Compact gained a shared instrument pool without creating a central power to order hidden transfers.

Its first emergency test came before the clause left revision.

A hospital spindle on Nacre reported that one bearing had exceeded thermal margin during receiver slowdown. Six operating theatres and a dialysis ward depended on its rotation. The local controller requested a four-minute curvature lift, small enough to fit beneath Bex's current emergency threshold. No consenting sink existed.

The request appeared automatically on the hearing wall because the draft ledger treated a missing destination as a blocking defect. Thirty delegates watched the bearing temperature rise.

Bex opened the hospital model. “Four minutes buys time to cool the bearing and complete every procedure underway.”

“Destination?” Iona asked.

“None available under your clause.”

“Then show local options.”

The hospital engineer listed them. Abort three operations safely. Continue two that could not pause. Move dialysis patients to reserve cartridges. Brake the spindle by two percent while external tugs took part of its load. The brake would damage a bearing surface and close half the hospital for months.

A patient advocate joined from the spindle. “Your local option transfers risk from current patients to everyone waiting for care.”

“Yes,” the engineer said. “It keeps the force here.”

“That does not make it fair.”

“No.”

The hearing chair asked whether the Compact required the hospital to refuse the four-minute lift even if deaths followed.

Iona read the clause as machinery rather than rhetoric. “It prohibits sending the cost to a destination that cannot refuse. It does not choose among the local harms.”

“Then who chooses?”

“The affected medical authority under public emergency review, with patients represented and the physical account visible.”

The advocate demanded access to the procedure list. Privacy counsel objected. Nemi split the data: medical conditions remained protected while load requirements, delay tolerance, and transfer risk became public. Two surgical teams confirmed they could abort. One family refused transfer from the dialysis ward until the reserve cartridges were inspected in front of them.

A Debtors' Fleet tug moved from its staging corridor toward the hospital spindle. Its captain published the fuel cost and warned that accepting hospital load would remove the vessel from Nacre's ninth housing transfer. Residents from that transfer entered the hearing. They could preserve their route by lending a smaller utility tug, but its crew lacked experience near hospital docking arms.

Keir supplied two spinwrights to ride the utility tug. The larger vessel returned to fleet position. Hospital mechanics engaged the brake while the small tug took lateral load. Temperature peaked three degrees below bearing failure, then fell.

One operation ended early. Two patients entered extended sedation. Dialysis cartridges passed inspection and moved through the ward by hand. The bearing surface scored along eighteen centimeters, condemning half the spindle to closure after evacuation.

No pressure pulse traveled toward Eidos.

The patient advocate remained on the channel after the crisis. “Put this event in the Compact commentary. People must know zero can leave them with a worse hospital.”

Iona added the full emergency record. “Not commentary. Precedent.”

Hesh's delegate seized on the damage. “You have proved the rule sacrifices human welfare for procedural purity.”

“We chose among human harms while the affected people could answer,” Iona said. “The alternative would have forced an Olun history to join our emergency without a voice.”

“The Olun might have preferred four minutes to the political consequences of a hospital failure.”

“Then they can say so before a future transfer. We cannot manufacture their yes from our need.”

The damaged hospital bearing entered Nacre's transition cost. Replacement alloy had already been assigned to a shade joint. Engineers shifted that repair, shortening the shade's inspection interval and increasing fuel use during its next maneuver. One local decision continued spreading consequences, but every step retained a name and a challenge path.

Delegates revised the emergency clause around the event. A local authority could compel access to mass or fuel during imminent danger, but the seizure required live publication, proportional compensation, and independent review within one operating cycle. It could not touch receiver routing. Medical privacy could hide identities, never aggregate load or physical destination. Failed equipment remained in the ledger until repaired rather than disappearing after the emergency closed.

The hospital spindle signed its own cessation plan from half capacity. Its mark appeared beneath the draft before any system delegate signed above it.

Consent from the Olun required different architecture. Aru could not sign for every pressure-name. Its diminished translation arrived as ratios through the cradle, carrying the supporting factions' condition: each sink coalition identified its own acceptable load, duration, and withdrawal right. No Republic certificate could convert silence into consent.

A delegate asked whether one Olun faction might accept payment while another occupying the same world refused.

Iona sent the question through Nemi. Aru's answer returned in three attempts.

PRESSURE PATHS OVERLAP. CONSENT REQUIRES ALL HISTORIES MEASURABLY AFFECTED BY THE PATH. UNCERTAINTY MEANS NO TRANSFER.

“That standard can make future exchange impossible,” Bex said.

“It can,” Iona replied.

The Compact did not promise preserved convenience. It promised that inability to identify an affected party could no longer become permission.

At hour thirty of the hearing, the chair called for provisional signatures. These would not enact the Compact before the Assembly vote. They would commit each delegation to introduce conforming local plans and would authorize preparation for receiver ramp-down if emergency authority ended.

Thirty desks showed amber. Six remained gray for delay. Seven held red opposition.

Hesh demanded one final amendment: change zero hidden debt to zero undisclosed debt, allowing classified sink assignments reviewed by a security panel.

Iona placed the proposed wording over Bex's sealed records. Six years of structured replies disappeared behind the word classified. Sella's maintenance note became protected technical context. Meridian's returned pulse became a matter for later review.

“No,” she said.

“Then Hesh delays.”

“You have that authority.”

The Hesh desk changed from amber to gray. Another delayed delegation moved into provisional support after its receiver workers filed a local cessation plan. The count stayed thirty.

Bex stood beside the Nacre desk without signing power. “If the Assembly refuses renewal tomorrow, my routing authority ends immediately. Your plans will still require security access, fuel releases, and engineering keys held by my office.”

“Will you block them?” Iona asked.

“That question belongs to tomorrow.”

“So does your office.”

He almost smiled, then looked at the Compact wall. “You have written a republic that cannot promise what its citizens expect from one.”

“We wrote what the machinery allows.”

“No. Machinery allows hidden export. You wrote what some people will refuse to do.”

The correction mattered. Iona entered it in her private notes.

Delegates signed in no ceremonial order. Vela first, then Lumen, Sere, Nacre, Perihelion's system authority, and twenty-five others. Each signature opened its attached cost plan on the public wall. Shrinking habitats appeared in amber. Relocations appeared in blue. Evacuations marked red. Fuel deficits, lost industry, delayed hospitals, and housing claims remained visible beside every pledge.

No column claimed zero sacrifice.

The thirtieth signature came from a system whose delegate had opposed the title. She attached a reservation stating that honesty did not compensate the displaced. The Compact accepted the reservation without weakening the destination rule.

Nemi locked the provisional text against silent amendment. Any later change would generate a new public version and require fresh signatures. The first fleet-stage authorizations went out as preparation only. Tugs could approach Nacre's mass yards. Tankers could unlock fuel. Synchronizers could load the signed timing plan but not execute while Bex's routes remained active.

On the wall, the Republic map changed. It still showed forty-three systems. Thirty now carried provisional Compact marks, six showed delay, and seven displayed refusal. The political boundary had moved before any legal vote.

Iona signed as drafter, not governor. Her name granted no routing power. It certified only that the visible text matched the obligations and costs in the attached plans.

Outside the chamber, mass-yard clamps opened around Nacre. Civilian tugs entered their assigned corridors while emergency authority continued counting down. The Honest Orbit Compact had not saved a single habitat yet, but thirty systems had authorized work that could begin the moment hidden command ended.

Chapter 24 — The Republic Divides

Elian Bex kept his thumb on the renewal plate while the Assembly clerk counted the absent systems.

The plate had warmed under his skin. A security precaution, the engineers called it: living tissue had to remain present until the motion closed. Bex had designed an earlier version for pressure-door overrides in cylinder hospitals. A frightened surgeon could not authorize decompression by tapping once and leaving. He had never imagined that a minister might one day hold an entire polity open by the same method.

“Forty-three delegations certified,” the clerk said. “Thirty-nine present in chamber or secure relay. Four have deposited sealed instructions.”

Above the circular floor, the system emblems burned in three incomplete arcs. Thirty white marks indicated provisional assent to the Honest Orbit Compact. Seven red marks belonged to systems rejecting its cessation schedule. Six amber marks indicated delay. The colors did not predict the renewal vote, though every delegate had spent the morning pretending otherwise.

Bex looked through the ministerial glass toward Nacre’s spindle. Radiation shades passed across the blue-white star in measured silence. Beyond the glass, four-point-eight billion people depended on engineers who had started moving mass before the Assembly granted them permission. The Debtors’ Fleet held station in a ragged shell around the receiver hub. Their ships carried flywheels, ballast, propellant, food, and the improvised synchronizers confiscated from people who had once meant to cut the network apart.

Aru’s seventy-two-hour suspension had seventeen hours left.

The first ten-day emergency term had expired four days earlier. Meridian's loss had persuaded the Assembly to grant a four-day bridge, authority measured not by confidence in Bex but by the hours needed to move evacuees and expose the standing receiver queue. That bridge expired with today's vote.

The clerk read the motion. “Renewal of emergency continuity authority under Article Nine, with custodial power retained by Minister Elian Bex and operational command over all Reciprocal Mass Exchange receivers, fleet assets, and local cessation schedules.”

Someone behind Bex coughed. The chamber acoustics carried the sound cleanly to every system.

Assembly Speaker Rho said, “Minister, you may release the plate after your statement.”

Bex did not release it. “If I remove my authorization before the vote records, receiver command passes to the standing continuity queue. That queue still contains the pre-Compact clearing schedule.”

A movement traveled through the delegates, not panic yet, but its administrative cousin.

“You retained an active schedule?” Rho asked.

“I retained every schedule capable of keeping habitats intact. The old clearings remain locked against execution. They are not erased.”

Iona Rhyll sat at the witness rail rather than with the Curvature Office. She had resigned eight days earlier and had spent those days doing the work without the office. Her hands rested beside a thin display showing Nacre’s correction clock. She did not look surprised.

“Can your lock survive loss of authority?” Rho asked.

“For nine minutes,” Bex said. “After that, the queue seeks the nearest valid command source.”

“Which source?”

“That depends on this vote.”

Seven red system emblems brightened as their delegations requested objection time. Bex recognized the tactic. Make the risk sound like a threat, then let fear do the subtraction. He had used it himself in emergency budgets, dock seizures, fuel allocation, and the concealment order six years ago.

The delegation from Var spoke first. “Minister Bex has placed the Assembly under operational duress. Renew him or the network executes a prohibited schedule.”

“No,” Iona said from the witness rail.

Speaker Rho struck the light once. “Witnesses answer when recognized.”

Bex looked at Iona. “Recognize her.”

“You do not control recognition here, Minister.”

“Not for another twelve minutes, apparently.”

A few delegates laughed. The sound died when Nacre’s correction clock lost another second.

Rho recognized Iona.

She stood. Vestibular braces glinted beneath the table where Nemi Vale sat beside her. “The standing queue cannot execute while the receiver hub remains under physical maintenance hold. Spinwrights placed that hold six hours ago. If ministerial authority lapses, the old schedule will seek command and fail. We then have nine minutes to issue a Compact schedule through a different root.”

Var’s delegate said, “And who possesses that root?”

Bex felt the old engineering key inside his coat.

The object had no light, no active transmitter, and no ornamental seal. It consisted of a black ceramic bar with three contact ridges worn smooth by thirty-two years of use. He had carried it while crawling through Nacre’s unfinished receiver vault, before the Continuity Ministry existed in its current form. Its authority did not come from office. The key belonged to the people who had built the isolation trees and expected that one day a command network might fail.

Iona’s gaze dropped once toward his coat. She knew. He had shown it to her during the first emergency model, when seventeen billion dependent lives had still seemed like an argument capable of ending every other argument.

She said, “A physical engineering key can install a local root after the queue rejects. The change requires presence at the hub and three civilian countersigns.”

“Who holds it?” Var asked.

Bex lifted his thumb from the renewal plate.

The plate cooled. The motion locked for voting.

“I do,” he said.

The chamber entered a sixty-second silence while the delegations cast. Bex had seen forty years of Assembly rituals compress violence into lights. Border closures, ration schedules, evacuation priorities, and debt ceilings had all entered history through the same quiet interval. The polished floor never shook. No blood touched the desks. Somewhere beyond the walls, crews obeyed what appeared on their consoles.

During the first ten days of emergency authority, he had signed one hundred and sixteen receiver continuations. Each order carried a modeled consequence for shutdown. Each also carried an Olun cost that no model could count. He had kept the latter column sealed until Iona broke the account open.

The plate beside Speaker Rho chimed. Outside the glass, a shade vane completed its turn across Nacre’s star.

“Renewal requires twenty-nine affirmative votes,” she said. “Twenty-six affirmative. Eleven opposed. Six abstaining or delayed. Renewal fails.”

No emblem changed color. The thirty provisional Compact systems still held white. Seven still rejected. Six still delayed. The vote had ended Bex’s authority without repairing the Republic’s division.

The Speaker opened a two-minute register for declarations of consequence. It had no legal force, but systems used it to tell one another which failures to expect.

Nacre spoke first. Its civil delegation confirmed that spin redistribution had already begun and would continue under local authority. Three residential bands had accepted load rationing. Two industrial crescents would shut down before the correction peak. The delegation named neither heroism nor unity. It listed water hours, lift restrictions, evacuation berths, and the expected loss of eleven percent of habitable volume.

Stillness followed with an offer of ballast pilots and no receiver support. Its delegate reminded the chamber that a poor city could contribute skill without pretending poverty made it pure.

Lumen committed Helix Quay’s surviving heavy tugs. The quay had rebuilt only half its cargo spine after the first repossession. Its crews would sail anyway.

Then Var, first among the seven rejecting systems, declared every Compact command invalid within its jurisdiction. Six other red emblems filed matching notices with different language: sovereign safety, local necessity, procedural defect, unavailable fuel, unacceptable Olun terms, and unlawful surrender of Republic infrastructure. None authorized involuntary export in public. Bex noticed the omission. Even rejection now required its own concealment or a new honesty.

The six delaying systems spoke last. One awaited a receiver audit. Two lacked evacuation capacity. Another demanded proof that Aru could restrain the hard pressure-names after seventy-two hours. The fifth had lost contact with an outer mining swarm. The sixth simply said its population had not consented and would not be hurried by Nacre’s clock.

Those reasons entered beside the amber marks. Bex could have answered every one with emergency doctrine. He had legal templates for seizure, priority fuel transfer, mandatory silence, and custodial replacement of local councils. Until moments ago, his office could have issued all four within a minute.

A junior clerk approached the dead ministerial console with a portable reader. “Sir, continuity archive requests your final operational note.”

Bex almost dictated the standard line: authority lost under active hazard, successor command advised to preserve network stability by all available means. Such wording would give frightened engineers permission to rediscover the Eidos branch without naming it.

“Record this instead,” he said. “No receiver may interpret loss of central command as consent to export. Local physical safety measures continue. Any debt movement requires a named destination and visible authority.”

The clerk hesitated. “That limits the successor queue.”

“That is its purpose.”

“Do you want the note attributed to the ministry?”

“The ministry no longer has authority. Attribute it to Elian Bex, former custodian, at the minute renewal failed.”

She read the text back. He corrected one comma because habit survived disgrace. When she sealed the note, it propagated to all forty-three system ledgers as testimony rather than command. Thirty accepted it into their Compact records. Seven marked it advisory. Six held it pending. Not one receiver could mistake it for an order.

His ministerial display cleared itself.

First went the fleet map. Then the receiver ledger, habitat reserve summaries, emergency warrants, and direct lines to system governors. The Nacre correction clock remained because Iona had put it on the public channel. Seventeen hours of alien restraint continued to fall away where anyone could see it.

Two security officers approached the ministerial enclosure. Their uniforms belonged to the Assembly, not his ministry. Bex knew both by sight. He had authorized their emergency housing allotments after a shade failure three years earlier.

“Elian Bex,” the senior officer said, “remain where you are.”

“I had no plan to run.”

The officer studied him with the disciplined disbelief of someone trained by Bex’s own risk doctrine.

Speaker Rho said, “The Assembly has not yet issued a detention order.”

“It has,” said the delegate from Meridian’s surviving civil register.

A new motion appeared on the public display. The signatures had accumulated during the renewal debate: concealment of structured sink replies, unlawful diversion during the Meridian correction, command interference resulting in civilian death, and preservation of an unauthorized export schedule after public cessation.

The Meridian emblem had no inhabited ring behind it now. Its delegate sat under the sign of a place that could never send another local vote.

Bex read every charge.

He could still stop the Nacre correction. The thought came without drama because he had built contingencies for worse betrayals than a lawful vote. His old key could open the isolation tree, but it could also burn the local command root and force the hub into conservative continuation. Conservative continuation meant one more export to Eidos while Nacre stabilized. The pulse would injure Olun pressure strata, perhaps badly. It would preserve the spindle, protect the fleet, and give six delaying systems a reason to wait for a cheaper settlement.

Nine minutes offered enough time.

He pictured the command path as if standing inside the receiver vault. Key into maintenance root. Physical interlock left, then center. Decline the new countersigns. Restore continuity queue. The procedure required no ministry credential. It had been designed to survive dead ministers.

Bex had used human bodies as the final unit in every continuity account. Seventeen billion dependent. Four-point-eight billion at Nacre. A nursery ring at Meridian. He could divide the figures by fuel, available thrust, evacuation berths, or expected correction load. Each quotient made command feel like engineering.

Six years earlier, a technician had brought him the first structured response from Eidos. The pattern repeated prime intervals around a returned load. His scientific staff had not claimed life. They had claimed uncertainty. Bex had asked what would happen if the network paused while the Republic investigated.

The projected dead had filled three walls.

He sealed the response before lunch.

Nothing in that choice had felt like cruelty. He had eaten standing over a receiver reliability chart, answering a habitat governor who feared a bearing cascade. His hand had not shaken. The danger in his work had never come from enjoying harm. It came from the ease of loving people inside a boundary.

Iona left the witness rail and crossed the chamber. The officers watched her approach but did not interfere.

“Six minutes until queue search,” she said.

“You planned for a longer speech.”

“I planned for you to keep your thumb down.”

“I considered it.”

“Of course you did.”

Bex studied her face. Fatigue had pared it since Perihelion. Meridian had added a stillness he recognized from engineers after a structure failed despite every correct motion. She had recovered Sella’s note among debris Bex helped create. She could have addressed him as murderer and remained within evidence.

Instead she held out her hand.

Around them, the Assembly began its next argument. The seven rejecting systems demanded local command guarantees. Three of the six delays wanted the correction paused until their own audits concluded. Compact delegates sought a temporary coordinating council, but any central authority sounded too much like the office they had just removed. Thirty systems had agreed on zero hidden debt. They had not agreed on who would carry the first open cost.

“You do not have enough time to negotiate the countersigns,” Bex said.

“Tavin has them at the hub.”

“He has captains, not legal custodians.”

“Keir Serr holds Nacre spin authority. The receiver workers elected Pella Jor as fleet engineering custodian. The public debt panel gave Nemi audit countersign.”

“Pella should be aboard the Morrowline.”

“She is. The key can be carried to her through the service lock.”

Bex almost smiled. “Two fingers short, and you make her sign for a planetary correction.”

“She insisted.”

“Good.”

Iona kept her palm open.

The senior officer said, “Minister, place both hands where we can see them.”

“In a moment.”

“Now.”

Bex turned toward him. “If you prevent this transfer, the standing queue will seek a root in four minutes and thirty seconds. You may arrest me before or after I keep it from finding one. Decide which report you prefer to write.”

The officer glanced toward Speaker Rho. She gave one short nod.

Bex reached inside his coat.

The engineering key weighed less than the identification wafer the Assembly had just voided. Its ceramic surface retained heat from his body. One corner carried a notch made during Nacre’s construction, when a dropped support frame had trapped him and two apprentices against an unfinished vault wall. They had used the key as a lever to release a manual latch. That misuse had caused the first ridge to wear unevenly.

He placed it in Iona’s hand but did not release it.

“The isolation tree has two false accepts,” he said. “The first exists for remote diagnostics. Ignore it. The second arms the export branch even if the display says local. Ignore that too. Wait for the mechanical contact beneath the third ridge.”

“I remember.”

“You saw it once.”

“I remember once.”

“If synchronization slips past eleven milliseconds, the receiver will offer Eidos as automatic relief.”

“We will refuse.”

“Nacre may lose the outer residential band.”

“Yes.”

“The Morrowline may not clear the mass yoke.”

Her fingers closed around the bar. “Tavin knows.”

Bex still held the other end. “Knowing does not create thrust.”

“No. Fuel does. Mass does. Time does. They have counted all three.”

The answer left him nowhere to hide a fourth term.

He released the key.

Iona passed it at once to Nemi, who sealed it in a transparent transit case and attached the public evidence strip. Every person in the chamber could watch the custody record begin. Bex’s private contingency became a common instrument through the act of being seen.

The queue clock showed three minutes, fifty-one seconds.

“Get it to the hub,” he said.

Iona turned to go.

“Rhyll.”

She looked back.

“The local staging table underestimates shade flex by four percent. I corrected it in the ministry model, not the public copy.”

“Why?”

“Because I was still keeping tools.”

“Where is the correction?”

“Maintenance layer seven, under my original builder number. The key will expose it.”

Iona’s expression tightened. He could not tell whether she wanted to thank him or strike him. Both responses would have been easier than what she gave him.

“Then it belongs in the public record,” she said.

“Yes.”

She and Nemi left through the lower chamber door. A Fleet courier waited beyond it with a sealed thrust case. The case took the key and departed toward the receiver hub.

Only then did Bex place both hands on the ministerial desk.

Speaker Rho called the detention motion. No one requested debate. Thirty-four delegations voted to hold him for public tribunal. Five opposed immediate custody. Four abstained pending review. The totals appeared beside his name without commentary.

The senior officer opened a restraint band. “Elian Bex, you are under arrest by order of the Assembly.”

Bex extended his wrists.

The band closed softly. It carried no ceremonial weight, only a pressure sensor and a localizer that no longer reported to his ministry. He tested its fit from an engineer’s habit. Secure without restricting circulation.

As the officers led him from the enclosure, the system emblems reorganized. Thirty Compact signatories formed a white arc on one side of the chamber. Seven red marks withdrew to a separate communications register. Six amber emblems remained between them, connected to neither command structure. The Forty-Three Systems Republic still existed in treaties, traffic codes, and the names painted on ships. It no longer possessed one center capable of ordering every receiver.

At the chamber threshold, Bex’s dead display flickered once.

The standing continuity queue had searched for authority. It found none. Across Nacre, local maintenance holds remained engaged while a courier carried the old key toward people who could use it only in public.

The officers took Bex through the door. Behind him, central command ended with the physical crisis still running.

Chapter 25 — The Gravity Well

Tavin Serr felt Nacre take hold of the Morrowline through a cargo yoke built for smaller worlds.

The yoke’s twelve lock pins closed around the first flywheel cradle. Metal struck metal along the ship’s spine, each impact arriving through Tavin’s seat as a separate blow. The flywheel had stopped turning six hours earlier, yet its stored mass resisted every change in direction. Two hundred thousand tonnes of iron composite wanted to continue around Nacre’s spindle forever.

“Pins one through ten seated,” Pella Jor said from engineering. “Eleven reports a false bite. Twelve hates us personally.”

“Eleven first,” Tavin said. “Machines can dislike us after they’re secure.”

Pella’s left hand moved across the engineering relay. Her missing fingers had changed the way she gripped a rail, not the speed of her decisions. “Cycling eleven.”

On the forward display, Nacre filled half the view. Its inhabited spindle stretched across the glare of a blue-white star, protected by passing radiation shades. Residential bands glowed along the rotating body. Farther out, the receiver hub hung among conventional tugs, habitat tenders, ore haulers, and ships never intended to fly in formation. The Debtors’ Fleet had no uniform outline. Its common feature lay in the mass each vessel had agreed to carry.

Keir’s voice reached the bridge through the spinwright channel. “Cradle team, confirm port clamp travel. We’re reading three centimeters short.”

Tavin opened the circuit. “Morrowline confirms false bite on eleven. Give Pella twenty seconds.”

“Captain Serr, this circuit belongs to cradle operations.”

“It did before you used my ship as a measuring tool.”

A pause. “Hello, Dad.”

“Hello, Keir. Move your people behind the yellow truss.”

“We are behind it.”

“Move farther.”

“You can’t see the truss from your bridge angle.”

“I know how apprentices define behind.”

Keir muted himself, probably to swear with professional precision. Tavin watched figures in pressure suits pull along the cradle web. Twenty-one years earlier he had carried Keir asleep through a tug passage during a bearing alarm. The boy had trusted the arms holding him. The man outside now trusted his own clamp readings more than his father’s fear.

Pin eleven drove home.

“Twelve seated out of embarrassment,” Pella said. “Yoke secure.”

Haru Den fed the cradle mass into the maneuver plot. The navigator’s screen held four orbital traces: Nacre’s current spindle path, its shrinking safe corridor, the fleet’s mass procession, and a red boundary where Aru’s suspension ended. Fifteen hours remained, but the physical schedule cared little for negotiation clocks. Every load had to cross before receiver shutdown reached the next stage.

“Correction step one begins in forty seconds,” Haru said. “Local spin load dropping four percent.”

Tavin keyed all compartments. “Hands to rails. Compensation will ease by sequence. Trust your deck marks, not your stomach.”

The Morrowline’s RME load compensator reduced its field. Weight shifted toward the conventional thrust axis. Tavin’s shoulders pressed harder into the seat as the fusion clusters assumed more of the flywheel’s burden. A cup rose from a bridge clamp, trembled, then settled at an angle that no longer matched the painted floor.

Beyond the hull, Orbit Breaker synchronizers fired in a disciplined wave.

Their makers had designed them to sever receivers across a system before anyone could intervene. Tavin had taken the first set from a cell preparing to abandon a nursery cylinder to drift. Now each device sat inside a civilian control cage with three physical safeties. Keir’s spinwrights had added local clocks. Nemi’s auditors had added public signatures. No single trigger could cut the network.

Along Nacre’s receiver crown, one bank dimmed.

The debt did not disappear. Load moved into the flywheel cradle, into rising spindle stress, into fusion exhaust from twenty-seven ships. The Morrowline’s thrust trace climbed as Tavin pulled the first wheel away from the city.

“Relative drift plus eight centimeters,” Haru said.

“Port cluster two, point three percent.”

“Responding.”

A conventional tug off their stern matched the correction. Its hull carried the painted name Small Argument and a cargo frame full of water ballast. Another vessel, a farm tender from the localization trial, held a stack of empty soil drums where crops had once grown. Each ship paid with useful capacity.

Keir called the next shift. “Outer residential band taking spin transfer. We need cradle clear in six minutes.”

“You’ll have it in five.”

“Your plotted release says five forty.”

“My plotted release includes your three-centimeter clamp error.”

“It came from thermal contraction.”

“So will my apology.”

This time Keir’s laugh escaped before the mute.

The Morrowline hauled the first flywheel along a widening spiral. Nacre receded by degrees. No receiver eased the force across the crew. Sive An moved through the lower decks checking blood pressure, joint restraints, and the small injuries ordinary acceleration collected. The crew had begun the campaign with forty-seven. Meridian left forty-four. Every surviving body occupied a line in Tavin’s load plan because bodies could not be shifted like ballast without consequence.

At the release point, Pella blew the cradle bolts.

The wheel departed toward a capture tug, its new path paid for by fusion propellant. Small Argument took the mass and burned toward a storage orbit beyond Nacre’s inhabited traffic. The handoff missed nominal by less than half a meter.

“First wheel clear,” Haru said.

No one cheered. Twenty-two remained.

The fleet worked through them in pairs. Ore haulers accepted the smaller flywheels. Two old Navy tugs moved ballast blocks from the spindle’s sunward face. Habitat tenders carried water and shield mass, leaving their own emergency reserves thin. Every extraction let Nacre’s spinwrights shift a fraction more load away from RME.

During the fourth transfer, a clamp jammed on a wheel whose bearing housing had warped under uneven gravity. Keir crossed the cradle in a service sled and drove a thermal lance into the pin collar.

Tavin watched the suit marker reach the red maintenance zone. “Keir, withdraw. We can take the whole cradle.”

“The cradle adds nine thousand tonnes and fouls your release angle.”

“We can carry it.”

“You can carry anything once. We need the yoke for nineteen more.”

A white flare hid Keir’s gloves. Molten collar material sprayed into the catch hood.

“Your shield is reading hot,” Tavin said.

“I have the same display.”

“Then obey it.”

“Pin is moving.”

The jammed section shifted. Keir kicked away from the collar as a spring load snapped the pin free. His service sled caught him after two rotations. Tavin did not breathe until the suit marker turned green.

“Cradle released,” Keir said.

“You’re off exterior work for the next cycle.”

“I report to the civilian spin coordinator.”

“You are speaking to the fleet coordinator.”

“Those authorities appear to conflict.”

Pella cut into the channel. “I have seniority in bad judgment. Keir, get your shield plate changed.”

“Yes, Chief.”

Tavin closed his circuit before his son heard relief damage his voice.

At transfer nine, Nacre’s outer factories went dark. The Compact feed showed each shutdown with its cost: oxygen catalysts delayed, hull plate output suspended, medicine lines moved to ration schedules. Four-point-eight billion people felt the correction through lift queues, colder rooms, restricted water, and a new tug in the direction of down.

At transfer twelve, the first residential band evacuated. Ferries crossed from the spindle to temporary shelters on the moonward docks. No one called the movement temporary on the public channel. Eleven percent of Nacre’s habitable volume would not survive the final spin profile.

At transfer fifteen, a rejecting system withdrew its two tenders from the fleet. Their captains cited local orders. The empty positions opened a mass gap across the procession.

Haru marked the revised plot. “We lose twenty minutes moving their ballast share.”

“We don’t have twenty.”

“Then one ship takes both blocks.”

Tavin looked at the yoke limits. The Morrowline already carried the largest wheel in each cycle because its spine could distribute load across six thruster clusters. Taking both ballast blocks would push the next burn beyond rated bending moment.

Pella saw the same numbers. “We can brace the lower yoke to the old compensator frame.”

“The frame loses field support in stage three.”

“It still contains metal.”

“Some of it.”

“Enough if Haru keeps us straight.”

Haru did not turn from the plot. “A stirring declaration of trust.”

Pella assigned repair crews. They stripped partitions from two empty cargo galleries and welded the beams across the yoke roots. The work displaced Sive’s forward treatment station. She moved her patients aft and sent Tavin a list of what she could no longer reach during a burn.

He accepted both ballast blocks.

The extra mass changed the Morrowline’s voice. Frame vibrations lowered until speech picked up a second tone through the deck. Thruster three reached heat margin first. Pella fed coolant from the cargo refrigeration loop, abandoning twelve tonnes of preserved food to warming compartments.

“Someone will object,” she said.

“Send them a spoon.”

The ship crossed the release point with fourteen seconds left. Two capture vessels took the ballast separately. One rolled past its planned attitude, recovered on cold gas, and continued outward.

Nacre entered correction stage three.

Receiver banks shut down in four synchronized groups. The city’s apparent gravity changed across its length. Spinwrights under Keir’s command moved rail ballast toward the surviving bands while whole neighborhoods emptied behind them. The public feed carried the clatter of thousands of locked doors. It also carried every active mass destination, from reservoir trains to external wheels.

Aru-in-Pressure spoke through the translation cradle aboard the audit tender. Its voice arrived as ratios shaped into human words.

“Transfer holds locality. Pressure histories receive no new wound.”

Tavin answered while steering. “Can your chorus keep the correction from returning?”

“Suspension holds while no concealed path opens. Hard pressure-names gather at its boundary.”

“How long?”

“Human count: fourteen hours, twenty-one minutes. Confidence narrows.”

That gave them no comfort beyond accuracy.

By the eighteenth wheel, crew fatigue began to alter the operation. A deckhand secured a tool bag to a handrail that would become overhead after the next attitude change. Sive caught the error before acceleration turned the bag into a projectile. A tug pilot repeated a vector from the prior cycle and nearly crossed a cradle path. Haru moved that ship to the outer queue.

Tavin ordered a six-minute halt.

Keir objected first. “We lose the gap.”

“We lose people if hands stop knowing where they are.”

“The spindle team has a fixed rail window.”

“Then use six minutes to check every rail brake.”

Silence answered him. He let it remain.

Across the fleet, crews drank, changed suit liners, inspected couplings, and admitted mistakes. Nacre continued turning. The city could not pause, but the people moving it could refuse to become another hidden cost.

When work resumed, the next two transfers landed inside tolerance.

The third did not. Small Argument lost its port fusion feed while carrying wheel twenty-one toward storage orbit. Its thrust plume vanished from the fleet display. The wheel kept moving, pulling the tug sideways by its cradle locks.

“Argument, release load,” Tavin said.

Captain Ose answered over an alarm. “Release squibs share the failed bus.”

“Manual?”

“Crew moving now.”

The disabled tug crossed toward a water tender whose tanks had already been drained into Nacre. Haru drew the collision in amber. Ninety-six seconds.

Tavin rolled the Morrowline beneath the drifting wheel. Its current yoke stood empty, but no cradle geometry matched the approaching load. Pella opened four maintenance grapples along the dorsal spine.

“Those lines rate for docking,” Haru said.

“They rate for lying engineers who expect no one to read the destructive-test appendix,” Pella replied.

Tavin put the Morrowline on a parallel vector. “Argument, keep your wheel between us. Water tender, burn zenith now.”

The empty tender moved late. Its pilot had grown accustomed to receiver compensation and misjudged full thrust under body load. Sive spoke to that bridge through the medical circuit, counting the burn in breaths until the pilot found the control rhythm.

Two suited figures emerged from Small Argument’s lock and crawled toward the dead release box. The drifting wheel rotated across Tavin’s view. Every half turn brought one grapple lug within reach.

“Dorsal one ready,” Pella said.

“Fire on Haru’s mark.”

“Three, two, mark.”

The first line struck and skipped. A second wrapped the lug. Its drum screamed through the Morrowline’s frame. Pella paid out cable rather than trying to stop two hundred thousand tonnes at once.

Small Argument’s crew reached the manual box. One kicked the cover free. The other pulled both mechanical releases while the tug rolled under them.

The wheel separated.

For eleven seconds it belonged to no ship. Then the Morrowline’s second and third grapples caught. Tavin fired three thruster clusters in sequence, drawing the mass away from the water tender. Acceleration pushed him into his restraints. Grapple two exceeded its nominal load, stretched, and held.

Small Argument drifted clear with its suited crew still outside. Ose restored the starboard feed and began recovery on conventional thrust.

Pella guided the captured wheel into the yoke with cables never intended for the work. Keir’s cradle team sent alignment marks from Nacre. Haru turned their uneven pull into a stable approach. When the lock pins closed, sixty-three seconds had passed since the first alarm.

“No pause,” Keir said. “Rail window closes in four minutes.”

“Then send the wheel,” Tavin answered.

They released it to the storage tug that should have received it from Small Argument. No line in the public ledger hid the rescue propellant, cable damage, pilot error, or four minutes taken from the final cut. Nemi’s audit feed entered each consequence while the ships kept moving.

At wheel twenty-two, Haru enlarged the final load table. One flywheel remained attached to the receiver hub’s inner crown. It had no external cradle. During construction, engineers had assembled it around the hub axle, trusting RME to carry the installation load. Removing it now required cutting the axle collar and taking a section of the crown with it.

The mass exceeded the Morrowline’s rated yoke by thirty-eight percent.

Tavin studied the spindle trace. If they left the wheel, stage-four shutdown would transfer its unsupported load into Nacre’s inner bearings. The bearings would seize. Stored rotation would tear through the occupied bands.

“Alternatives,” he said.

Haru placed three on the screen. Leave the wheel and lose the spindle. Divide it in place and miss the Olun deadline by nine hours. Open an emergency export through Eidos.

Tavin deleted the third line.

Pella came to the bridge from engineering. Grease marked her pressure sleeve. “There’s a fourth.”

“Show me.”

She drew a route outward through the fleet’s cleared corridor. The Morrowline would take the crown section, keep the excess mass after stage-four shutdown, and burn away from Nacre while conventional tugs held the spindle. At the far limit, the ship would perform the final transfer into an unoccupied heliocentric disposal path.

Haru checked the thrust requirement. “We can reach the limit.”

Tavin read what he had not said. “We cannot brake afterward.”

“Not with the yoke attached.”

“Jettison it at the limit.”

“The axle collar will fuse to our frame during the burn,” Pella said. “We can cut free before ignition or not at all.”

Keir joined the channel. His face carried a pressure mark from the replacement shield. “We can remove more crown structure. Reduce the fused contact.”

“Time?” Tavin asked.

“Seventy minutes.”

Aru’s suspension clock showed fourteen hours, but Nacre’s spin window showed fifty-two minutes before bearing loads crossed tolerance.

“No,” Tavin said.

Keir looked away from his camera, likely toward the vast wheel fixed inside the receiver crown. “Then use two ships.”

“No second spine can share that yoke through shutdown.”

“Dad.”

Tavin had heard that word spoken by a child asking for water, a teenager asking to leave Stillness, and a man refusing a private berth during evacuation. It carried no operational information now. It carried all the rest.

“Your team cuts the collar,” he said. “You withdraw to the spindle before we take load.”

Keir’s jaw set. “I can ride the cradle and release the outer braces.”

“No.”

“Someone has to.”

“Remote cutters can release them after first burn.”

“They have a six percent failure rate under skew.”

“Then Pella will build better ones.”

Pella nodded once.

Tavin sent the route to every Fleet captain. He did not call for approval. Shared risk had limits; no vote could change the mass. He called for objections based on physics.

Small Argument offered its remaining propellant. A Navy tug offered two drive technicians. The farm tender offered shield coolant. Three captains confirmed they could hold Nacre if the Morrowline departed under full load. No one offered a way to bring the ship home.

Haru overlaid the service pod trajectories. Forty-three pods could clear before ignition. One command pod near the bridge had enough independent thrust to launch after the yoke burn began, but its safe window measured less than twenty seconds.

“Crew evacuation before load,” Tavin said.

Pella looked at him. “Somebody stays to fly.”

“Yes.”

“Autopilot?”

“Skew changes during crown separation. It needs hands until the final transfer line settles.”

Haru folded her arms. “My hands know the line.”

“So do mine.”

“You’re assigning by rank.”

“I’m assigning the navigator to the fleet. These ships still need a route after Morrowline leaves it.”

Sive came onto the bridge circuit. “Medical has counted forty-three evacuees and one idiot.”

“Record the idiot as command mass.”

“I dislike cargo that makes jokes.”

Tavin signed the evacuation order.

Outside, Keir’s spinwrights moved toward the final wheel with cutters. The receiver crown rotated beneath them. The Morrowline turned to present its reinforced yoke, every welded beam exposed to starlight.

For the first time since he had taken command, Tavin saw his ship’s whole remaining life on one maneuver plot. It began at Nacre’s inner crown and ended beyond the fleet, where a red line marked a transfer no hull could survive.

The final flywheel started to come free.

Chapter 26 — The Last Transfer

The final axle collar broke loose with Keir Serr still inside the receiver crown.

Tavin saw his son’s suit marker jump across the bridge display. A section of cut metal rolled outward, dragging two brace lines and the service sled behind it. The Morrowline waited thirty meters away with its yoke open. Between ship and spindle, the freed flywheel began to precess.

“Keir, release both lines,” Tavin said.

“Trying.” His voice shook under suit alarms. “Left reel has loaded backward.”

Pella fired the yoke’s remote cutters. One brace parted. The other pulled the service sled toward the turning wheel.

“Suit tether,” Tavin said.

“Clipped to the sled.”

“Cut it.”

“That leaves me free.”

“That is the idea.”

Keir cut his tether. The sled struck the wheel rim and vanished into the rotating structure. His body continued outward, too slowly to clear the Morrowline’s approach.

Tavin rolled the ship six degrees. The open yoke passed beneath Keir’s boots. A cradle worker on Nacre fired a rescue line that missed his shoulder by half a meter.

“Again,” Keir said.

The second line caught his suit ring. Spinwrights hauled him toward the crown while the Morrowline completed its turn.

Pella’s board lit with twelve lock contacts. “Wheel entering yoke.”

Metal filled the forward view. The flywheel carried part of Nacre’s inner crown around its axle, a torn mass of truss and receiver plate. No loading manual had contemplated the shape. Lock pins struck wherever they could find structure.

“Seven contacts,” Pella said. “Eight. Nine. That’s all we get.”

“Rated requirement?”

“Twelve.”

“Today’s requirement?”

“Nine, if the frame wants a dignified death.”

Tavin took thrust to two percent. The wheel settled against the yoke with a sound like a building dropping one floor. On Nacre’s public trace, bearing load declined. Keir’s marker reached the safe truss.

“Spindle team clear,” Keir said. “Cut path open.”

Tavin keyed the ship. “All hands, abandon Morrowline. Pod sequence begins now. No equipment exceptions.”

The forty-three other crew had already strapped into service pods along the spine. Sive held the evacuation count from an external tender. Haru waited in pod one with the fleet plot. Pella remained at engineering long enough to arm the remote cutters.

“Chief,” Tavin said.

“I heard the order.”

“Your pod shows empty.”

“It has always been needy.”

“Pella.”

Her camera showed the engineering compartment, stripped of loose tools and half its wall panels. She touched the patched controls with three fingers. “Cutters armed. Coolant bypass fixed open. Thruster clusters will answer until their feed lines don’t.”

“Leave.”

“I’m going.”

She held up her damaged hand. “It takes longer to insult a ship properly.”

Pod thirty-two sealed around her. Green lights advanced down the Morrowline’s spine.

Tavin launched them in groups of four. Small conventional thrusters pushed each pod toward waiting tenders. The releases changed ship mass and yoke balance. Haru called revised trim from her pod because the bridge computer lagged behind the departures.

“Starboard cluster four, reduce point two,” she said.

Tavin corrected. “You are no longer navigator of this vessel.”

“Then stop using my plot.”

“Your plot is excellent.”

“I accept the apology.”

Pod forty-three cleared. Sive counted every beacon twice.

“All crew recovered except command,” she said. “Tavin, launch.”

“Not yet.”

Nacre’s final receiver bank still carried thirteen percent of local load. When it shut down, the flywheel would transfer its full mass into the Morrowline’s yoke. He had to hold alignment through that change, then fly the excess clear of the spindle. An empty bridge could not judge the skew as receiver plate tore away.

Keir came onto the command circuit. “You said remote cutters after first burn.”

“I said Pella would build better ones.”

“That does not answer me.”

“The crown has fused across the lower yoke. We knew it might.”

“You can launch before stage four.”

“And leave the ship to roll into Nacre.”

“Autopilot can hold.”

“Not through a load step it has never measured.”

The son on Tavin’s display had lost the softness of youth months ago. He wore a spinwright harness over his pressure suit. A burn marked one side of his face shield. Tavin wanted to store the image somewhere safer than memory.

“Keir, you have work,” he said. “Outer band braking starts when I cross the limit. If you miss it, we save a spindle with nowhere for people to live.”

Keir looked toward another screen. “I know.”

“Good.”

“I hate that answer.”

“So did I when you used it.”

Nacre’s stage-four clock reached ten seconds.

Tavin closed the crew channel and opened ship control. Six thruster clusters reported ready. Two exceeded thermal guidance. Three had fed through improvised coolant loops. The Morrowline had carried flywheels, nursery modules, ballast, food, fugitives, and one captain who had confused keeping a ship with keeping what mattered.

At zero, Nacre shut the last receiver bank.

Weight arrived.

The yoke drove the ship outward. Tavin fired all clusters before the computer finished measuring the change. His restraints tightened across his ribs. The flywheel pulled off center, rotating the Morrowline toward the spindle’s inhabited bands.

“Port one full. Dorsal three pulse.”

The controls obeyed his hands. Conventional thrust found the opposing vector. The ship stopped rolling with less than two degrees to spare.

Fleet tugs burned around Nacre. Their exhaust plumes formed a loose cage of force, each one carrying some fraction of the city’s corrected path. The spindle shifted into its rough new orbit. Not safe yet. Not hidden either.

Tavin pointed the Morrowline through the cleared corridor and increased thrust.

The fused wheel tore deeper into the cargo frame. Deck cameras failed one after another. A pressure door closed below the bridge, preserving atmosphere in compartments no one occupied. Thruster two lost coolant and ran hot.

“Limit line in three minutes,” Haru said from her pod.

“I see it.”

“Command service pod requires thirty seconds for safe separation.”

“It has nineteen.”

“Then start early.”

“Skew remains inside the fleet corridor.”

Sive cut in. “Your ribs will not remain inside you if that yoke fails.”

Tavin watched the final path settle. The Morrowline’s excess mass would cross beyond Nacre’s return corridor, but the ship had to turn the wheel another fraction before destruction. Otherwise fragments could intersect the spindle in eleven days.

He waited four seconds.

The turn completed.

Tavin locked the engines at full conventional burn and ran for the service pod.

The bridge deck had become a wall under acceleration. He pulled himself along recessed grips, crossing a passage built for gentler loads. Somewhere aft, a brace failed. The impact bent the corridor and jammed the pod hatch half open.

Tavin put both boots against the frame and pulled. The hatch moved one hand’s width. He turned sideways, forced his shoulders through, and fell into the couch.

No time remained for the harness sequence. He crossed the lap straps, struck the launch control, and felt the pod tear away.

Its small motor fired across the Morrowline’s vector. The ship continued outward, bright with six thrust plumes. For one second it looked intact.

Then the lower yoke entered the flywheel.

The cargo spine folded. Fusion feed shutoffs fired too late to save structure and early enough to prevent a reactor breach. The wheel crossed through the hull, carrying metal, propellant tanks, and the ship’s old compensator frame into the disposal path. Thruster clusters separated in burning pieces. None turned back toward Nacre.

The service pod tumbled. Tavin fought the controls until one attitude jet answered. Stars slowed. Nacre moved into view below him, off center and still turning.

Keir’s voice reached the pod through static. “Command beacon acquired. Dad, respond.”

Tavin tasted blood where his teeth had cut his cheek. “I told you to work.”

A sound came over the channel, half laugh and half failed breath.

Behind the pod, the Morrowline finished coming apart. Its drive section went dark. The flywheel continued into empty orbit with every fragment accounted for on Haru’s plot.

Small Argument reached him first. The tug approached on its surviving fusion feed, moving with a caution that made Tavin impatient until he tried to lift his left arm. The shoulder answered with a bright interior pain. His service pod had no medical brace large enough to immobilize it.

“Do not open your hatch,” Sive said over the rescue circuit.

“I had no current plan.”

“You launched without a sealed shoulder harness. Your plans have lost standing.”

The tug caught his pod in a soft grapple. Contact rolled him half a turn before the capture frame steadied. Through the little forward port, Nacre crossed from one edge to the other. Its spindle no longer followed the clean centered track of the receiver era. The outer residential band carried a visible kink where towers had begun to fail.

Keir’s signal transferred to the tug. “I’m coming out to the dock.”

“No. Finish the rail sequence.”

“They have another coordinator.”

“They had another captain too. We used the one who knew the failure.”

For several seconds Keir said nothing. Tavin heard voices behind him calling bearing loads and residential clearances.

“You are alive?” Keir asked.

“I am attached to Small Argument and being scolded by Sive.”

“That was not my question.”

Tavin looked at his hands. One had begun to shake after leaving the controls. “Yes.”

Keir closed his eyes once. When he opened them, a spinwright called him from outside the frame.

“I have to go,” he said.

“I know.”

The channel ended before either of them tried to make the survival mean more than it did.

Ose drew the service pod into the tug’s emergency lock. Air pushed against the hull. Tavin remained inside while Sive’s remote reader checked pressure, ribs, shoulder, and pupil response. Forty-three green crew markers surrounded his pod on the recovery display. With his own, the surviving complement reached forty-four.

No marker remained aboard the Morrowline.

Tavin watched his ship leave him alive.

Bex’s key entered the maintenance root but refused Iona’s first turn.

She knelt inside Nacre’s receiver hub with both hands around the ceramic bar. Bex's key had crossed from the Assembly in a thrust case, then through three fleet couriers, before Nemi sealed it into the public custody chain. Now it sat beneath the third contact ridge exactly as Bex had described.

The hub had never been meant for occupied work during correction. Each load change pulled at Iona from a new direction. Her knees pressed against the maintenance grating while her shoulders drifted toward a wall marked FLOOR under the former field. Nemi had threaded a restraint line through both their belts. When the outer bearing towers began to shear, the line tightened hard enough to bruise.

Behind them, civilian witnesses watched through three independent cameras. One feed belonged to Nacre’s displaced residents. Another went to the thirty Compact systems. The last remained open to all seven rejecting governments and six delays, whether they chose to record it or not. Iona had refused a private maintenance channel. A key used beyond public sight could rebuild the same office in another name.

She checked the physical routes before turning anything. The export conduit entered the root from the left, smooth and undamaged after a century of priority maintenance. Local staging arrived through a newer cage of cables laid across the deck during the past two days. Each cable bore handwritten tags: shade rail, outer band, moonward ballast, fleet thrust, dock release. Engineers had never standardized them because the Republic had never expected local payment at this scale.

“Custody read,” Iona said.

Nemi recited it. “Elian Bex to Iona Rhyll under Assembly witness. Iona Rhyll to courier one. Three courier transfers under continuous seal. Hub receipt confirmed. No unrecorded opening.”

“Authority read.”

“Former ministerial root expired. Civilian countersigns pending. No central override remains.”

“Physical state.”

Keir answered from the spin board. “All occupied bands clear of the planned shear zones. Fleet mass outside the inner corridor. Final wheel beyond recovery line. Conventional tugs under load.”

Iona looked once at the export conduit. “Then we do not use the easy path.”

She put torque into the key.

“False accept one,” Nemi said through Iona’s helmet. “Ignore.”

The root display offered a green confirmation. Iona turned past it.

Mechanical resistance changed. A second green field appeared with the words LOCAL STAGING ARMED.

“False accept two,” Iona said.

“Export branch just woke behind it.”

On the debt display, Eidos appeared as automatic relief.

The old network had survived every reform by treating export as safety and local cost as failure. With Nacre’s last receiver bank shut down, the root sought somewhere to place the unsettled remainder. The Morrowline carried physical mass away, but curvature corrections still had to settle across the spindle, fleet, shades, and moonward docks. One mistimed stage could send the remainder through the dark lattice.

Iona pushed the key deeper.

A contact clicked under the third ridge. Green fields vanished. The maintenance root opened as bare routing tables, each destination visible.

“Nemi, countersigns.”

Nemi Vale pressed the audit authorization from the adjacent console. Vestibular braces held her knees against the changing hub load. Keir signed from the civilian spin board. Pella signed from her recovery pod with her surviving fingers.

Three names appeared beside Iona’s.

No ministry stood above them.

Aru-in-Pressure filled the translation cradle with a low acceleration tremor. The hub plating carried it into Iona’s palms.

“Human route holds local,” Aru said. “Hard pressure-names press toward return.”

“Can you keep the channel open?”

“Open, yes. Empty of returned force, uncertain.”

“We need seven minutes.”

“Then give destinations without concealment.”

Iona loaded the staging table exposed by Bex’s builder number. His private four-percent shade-flex correction appeared exactly where he had said. She copied it into the public route, attributed the source, and let every system see that the final plan depended on knowledge he had withheld until arrest.

The table listed Nacre’s remaining debt in physical consequences. Outer shade array: two-point-one percent path change, paid by rail mass and tug burn. Moonward docks: loss of eleven berths, paid by ballast release. Inner spindle: bearing load beyond design for forty-eight seconds, paid by evacuation and permanent closure. Fleet corridor: flywheel mass removed by the Morrowline, ship unrecoverable.

A final row remained blank.

Unsettled curvature: 0.0037 g.

The same discrepancy Iona had found beneath a falling apple at Perihelion waited at the end of the Republic’s largest correction. The number had traveled through every account because small remainders made convenient doors.

Eidos pulsed on the alternate route.

Nemi read the automatic warning. “Local schedule lacks tolerance. Export recommended to preserve inhabited structure.”

“Recommended by whom?” Keir asked.

“An engine whose authors are dead.”

The hub shook as the Morrowline crossed the disposal limit. Its beacon separated from the ship. Haru reported command pod alive. Iona let relief pass through her without touching the controls.

“Where can we put the remainder?” she asked.

Keir enlarged the spindle map. “Outer residential band still has rail margin.”

“Occupied?”

“Evacuated and sealed.”

“Consequence?”

“Three bearing towers shear. Band cannot reopen.”

Nemi entered it. “Population displaced: eighteen million. Capacity loss already inside the public estimate, but not this mechanism.”

Aru’s pressure modulation sharpened.

“Return gathers,” it said. “Some of us hear the old channel and answer wound with wound.”

“Do not send it back,” Iona said.

“I hold no singular hand. I can keep the opening shaped while local route completes.”

“Cost to you?”

“Coherence.”

The translation cradle fractured Aru’s next ratio into several human tones. Pressure-names argued inside the signal. Some demanded the remainder. Others marked Nacre’s open costs and the destroyed ship. Aru held their disagreement together without claiming unanimity.

Iona selected the outer residential band.

The root demanded explicit acknowledgment: permanent loss of three towers, eighteen million residents unable to return, spindle capacity reduction beyond existing ration schedule.

She read every line aloud. Nemi confirmed the audit. Keir confirmed the physical path. Pella confirmed fleet load. Nothing went into a column labeled secondary.

Iona turned Bex’s key to local execution.

The rail masses moved first. Empty housing blocks along the outer band took a change in acceleration they had not been built to survive. Cameras showed walls flex, windows craze, and one bearing tower split through its middle. Conventional tugs burned against the shift. Their propellant reserves fell on the public display.

Eidos remained available.

“Route deviation point six percent,” Nemi said.

“Correct with moonward ballast.”

“Berth eleven will depart its frame.”

“Clear?”

“Cleared.”

Keir released the berth. A structure large enough to hold an evacuation ferry moved away from Nacre under cold-gas pushers. Fleet tugs caught its mass and drew the spindle back toward the staged path.

Aru’s voice thinned. “Hard return meets open channel.”

The cradle bucked. For an instant Iona felt a distant pressure history through her gloves: ice remembering impacts across generations, a moonlet’s altered pull written into living phase. Then Aru changed the channel geometry. The incoming correction did not vanish. It paused against the local route while Nacre paid.

“Thirty seconds,” Nemi said.

The last bearing tower failed. Empty residential decks bent around it. Nacre’s spindle orbit tightened, roughened, and held within the survival corridor.

“Ten.”

Iona kept the key turned. Heat reached her palm through the ceramic.

“Five.”

Eidos flashed one final recommendation.

She closed the export branch.

The 0.0037-g remainder entered Nacre’s public account and settled through the broken outer band. The dark-lattice route emptied without a debt pulse.

Aru released the channel. Its many tones fell away until one diminished voice remained.

“Local destination received,” it said.

Around the hub, receiver banks stayed dark. Conventional tugs continued burning. Eighteen million people had lost homes. Eleven docks had lost berths. Three towers had broken. The Morrowline had become a field of outbound fragments, and Meridian remained beyond repair.

Nacre passed the old correction point without exporting force.

On Iona’s display, its new orbit drew a coarse line around the star. Every deviation carried a named destination. The line did not smooth itself.

It held.

Chapter 27 — An Honest Orbit

Iona Rhyll put her boot against the inspection hatch and felt the unauthorized thrust before the gauge admitted it.

The habitat tender Kalliope hung beside a shrinking industrial ring in the Var system, one of the seven systems outside the Honest Orbit Compact. Its crew claimed to be moving ballast by cold-gas tug. The vibration under Iona’s heel carried a higher frequency than any cold-gas valve.

“Open it,” she said.

Chief Mera looked toward the Var safety officer who had accompanied them from the dock. The officer wore the red-bordered seal of a government that had rejected Compact jurisdiction. He gave no order.

Mera released the hatch.

Heat reached Iona first. Behind the inspection plate, a fusion transfer line fed a small conventional nozzle hidden inside a housing labeled WATER RECOVERY. The nozzle pushed against the ring’s changing orbit. Its exhaust destination appeared nowhere on the local cost schedule.

Nemi Vale braced one hand against the corridor while her vestibular support corrected for the tender’s gentle roll. “Thrust estimate?”

“Point eight newtons,” Mera said.

“For how long?” Iona asked.

“Sixteen days.”

“Propellant source?”

“Emergency reserve.”

“Named destination for the momentum?”

Mera pointed through the hull, as if vacuum could sign a ledger. “Away.”

Iona waited.

Six months earlier, a hidden nozzle would have counted as harmless because its exhaust crossed no inhabited boundary. The old Republic had reserved moral attention for where benefits appeared. Costs that left the camera frame became administrative weather.

The Var officer cleared his throat. “This installation does not use Reciprocal Mass Exchange. Your Compact has no authority here.”

“I did not say it did.”

“Then you cannot freeze the tender.”

“I did not freeze it.”

Nemi turned her display toward him. “Your own cessation law requires public declaration of propellant draw and collision effect. Var adopted it forty-one days ago.”

The officer read the citation. His government had refused ratification while copying nine Compact provisions into local statute. Each provision bore a different name. None permitted involuntary export.

Mera folded her arms. “If we disclose the burn, the district reduces our water allotment to account for reserve loss. This ring has eight thousand residents waiting for relocation.”

“How much water does the hidden draw preserve?” Iona asked.

“Three days.”

“And what path does the exhaust cross?”

“Empty.”

“Measured empty or certified empty?”

Mera’s face changed.

The words no longer required a lesson. Every engineer knew what the Republic had once placed inside that distinction.

Iona crawled through the hatch and clipped a sampler beside the nozzle. The exhaust carried tritium traces and ablated housing metal. Its path crossed the planned approach of an evacuation ferry in eleven days. Not enough material to breach a hull. Enough to damage radiators already operating near failure margin.

She entered the finding in Var’s local record rather than the Compact ledger. Hidden thrust. Undeclared reserve use. Downstream ferry risk. Three days of water preserved for eight thousand people. Nothing in the entry balanced cleanly.

“Corrective order?” the officer asked.

“You issue it,” Iona said.

“You are the auditor.”

“I am an invited field auditor. Var holds authority.”

He looked unhappy with a responsibility he had expected her to seize. “Shut down the nozzle. Publish the reserve draw. Recompute the ferry approach.”

Mera said, “That costs the ring three days.”

“Yes,” he answered.

“Put your name on it.”

The officer pressed his seal to the order.

Kalliope’s engineer closed the fusion line. The vibration beneath Iona’s boot ceased. On the public schedule, the industrial ring’s water deadline advanced. A ferry dispatcher changed course. Eight thousand residents acquired a more urgent evacuation date.

No one felt absolved.

At the dock, a wall display carried the ratification count. Thirty-six system marks now circled the Compact seal. The original thirty had completed public votes within the first two months. All six delayed systems joined after receiver audits, evacuation guarantees, and local consent proceedings. They had not joined from identical conviction. One had run out of fuel. Another had watched Nacre survive. Two demanded stronger limits on any future coordinating office. Their reasons remained attached to their signatures.

The remaining seven displayed no Compact seal.

Each had nonetheless stopped involuntary exports. Four had dismantled their dark-lattice links. Two held receivers in permanent standby while local courts decided ownership. Var kept an inactive receiver for research under Olun-observed seal. Across all forty-three inhabited systems, no authorized debt pulse had entered Eidos for one hundred and seventy-eight days.

Unauthorized attempts had occurred in nine places.

Iona had audited four. Receiver workers caught three before activation. Aru’s pressure-names identified one through the channel. The ninth reached a local coupling gate and failed because Bex’s old central certificates no longer opened any root.

The Compact did not treat those attempts as proof that ratification had failed. It published them in a common exception register. Each entry named who had tried, which local need they claimed, what route they chose, and which safeguard stopped them. People could see that most violations began with a defensible emergency. A hospital wanted two minutes of stable load. A mining spindle lacked enough propellant to turn before an ore swarm arrived. A governor feared an evacuation announcement would trigger panic. Familiar need still knew how to become secret harm.

Var’s entry drew more readers than the others because a local court had refused extradition. The engineer who opened the coupling gate remained under Var jurisdiction. Her testimony described twenty-seven thousand residents in a failing ring and a receiver that could have held them for another month by sending one pulse to Eidos. She had known the Olun would receive it. She had proceeded until the certificate failed.

Iona understood the decision and wanted the conviction.

That combination had unsettled commentators who preferred clean factions. Compact hearings preserved it anyway. The engineer received a public trial under Var law, while the ring received evacuation fuel from three signatory systems. Aid did not wait for agreement about guilt. Guilt did not disappear because aid arrived.

Olun observers maintained their own exception register through the translation cradles. Aru had not recovered its former coherence after holding Nacre’s channel. Some days it spoke in one translated voice. On others, two pressure-names shared the signal and disagreed over human intent. The hard faction no longer returned pulses automatically, but it marked every attempted opening as evidence that cessation could fail.

That judgment also belonged in the settlement. Humans supplied public routes and local payment. Olun pressure-names kept debt channels closed to involuntary transfer while allowing low-band observation under explicit consent. Neither side granted the other permanent trust.

At Perihelion, the first calibration channel had been physically removed. At Nacre, engineers filled the old export conduit with inspection glass so visitors could see that no live path hid inside it. Across the seven outside systems, Olun seals used pressure intervals rather than Republic certificates. Any attempt to wake a receiver had to break a pattern someone on Eidos could feel.

The victory consisted partly of making betrayal harder. It also required witnesses who survived long enough to report the attempt.

Nemi joined her beside the dock display. “Nacre sent the tribunal recess notice.”

“Verdict?”

“Not yet. Bex asked to add testimony on the Var standby receivers.”

“He no longer receives engineering reports.”

“He reads public ledgers.”

“Of course he does.”

Their transport to Nacre departed under conventional thrust. The acceleration couch pressed Iona’s shoulders with an honest discomfort. No compensator softened the burn. A display above each row showed propellant use, exhaust corridor, beam support, and the tug that would restore docking momentum at arrival. Most passengers ignored it after the first minute.

Iona did not mind. Public knowledge did not require permanent attention. It required availability before a decision and accountability afterward.

Nacre appeared two days later, its spindle orbit visibly eccentric against the shade array. Conventional tugs corrected the worst drift at every periapsis. Outer residential bands remained dark. Eighteen million displaced people lived in converted dock modules, temporary cylinders, and six systems that had accepted resettlement quotas.

Temporary had already become dishonest for many of them.

At the civil works yard, Tavin Serr stood beneath the drive cone of a tug that had never carried an RME compensator. His left shoulder remained stiff after the service-pod launch. He compensated by using his right hand for the inspection mirror and making Keir hold every heavy tool.

“This weld wanders,” Tavin said.

Keir looked up from the scaffold. “By point four millimeters.”

“Then it has wandered into another profession.”

“The frame tolerance allows point six.”

“Frame tolerance assumes crews enjoy vibration.”

Pella Jor, supervising from the lower platform, tapped her three fingers against the hull. “Crews complain for free. Fixing point four costs twelve hours.”

“Build it straight,” Tavin said.

“Build it funded,” Pella replied.

The yard contained six hulls in various stages. They lacked the Morrowline’s long cargo spine and impossible flexibility. Each tug had a thick conventional drive frame, detachable ballast arms, and service pods large enough for the full crew. Their design assumed no invisible load relief. They would carry less, burn more fuel, and require longer transfer windows.

Iona walked under the first hull. A small plate near the keel read MORROW CLASS.

Tavin saw her reading it. “Pella named the series while I was sedated.”

“It won the yard vote,” Pella said.

“The yard contains extortionists.”

Keir climbed down. “He voted against and then corrected the drive calculations.”

Iona touched the unpainted plate. “It is not the same name.”

“No,” Tavin said.

No new ship would become the Morrowline. The fragments remained on a disposal trajectory beyond Nacre, tracked by Haru until even their smallest risks passed out of the inhabited corridor.
