Tavin had refused salvage tugs. Recovering commemorative metal would have spent fuel needed for displaced residents.

“How many hulls?” Iona asked.

“Six here. Eleven contracted across four systems.”

“Enough?”

“Never.”

“When do you launch?”

“First tug in eight months if Pella stops finding places where the plans insult her.”

Pella held up the inspection mirror. “The plans started it.”

A shift horn sounded. Yard workers moved toward the hull with lunch packets, continuing arguments about coolant access and pod capacity. Keir stayed beside Tavin.

“The outer-band bearing report came back,” he said to Iona. “Tower three cannot be stabilized.”

“Can the band reopen around it?”

“No. We can recover archives and sealed belongings from four decks. Nothing more.”

Eighteen million people had not been displaced for a season. The correction had removed their homes as permanently as a fire or impact, though the spindle continued around the star.

Iona entered the new assessment into Nacre’s ledger from the yard. Outer residential band: closed without restoration path. Keir countersigned. A relocation office received the change and began converting temporary claims into permanent ones.

The tribunal convened that afternoon in the same Assembly chamber where Bex had surrendered his key. Its proceedings had remained public from the first charge. Survivors from Meridian spoke beside receiver workers, Olun translations, ministry clerks, and habitat governors whose populations would have died under abrupt shutdown.

Before Iona testified, the court called three witnesses whose accounts resisted the story each side wanted.

The first governed a hospital spindle during Bex’s emergency authority. She described how a continuity order kept surgical gravity stable through a drive repair and saved nine patients who could not have tolerated changing load. Then she displayed the same order’s destination code. Eidos had received the debt. She refused to call the patients’ survival false. She also refused to make it payment for an injury no patient had consented to send.

Next came a ministry routing clerk who had processed sealed responses for six years. He had never seen an Olun image or heard Aru speak. His task had consisted of moving files from scientific review into continuity archive and applying a retention code. Twice he asked why prime-ratio replies counted as noise. Twice supervisors showed him shutdown models. He continued processing.

“Were you threatened?” the judge asked.

“No.”

“Did you believe the files might describe life?”

“Yes.”

“Why did you remain?”

“I had a daughter on a receiver-dependent ring.”

The clerk’s daughter sat behind him. Her presence explained him without acquitting him.

Aru supplied the third account through a translation cradle bolted to the witness floor. The diminished speaker could not identify which exact human order struck which pressure lineage. Olun histories recorded acceleration, fracture, and interruption across different scales. Aru therefore gave ranges and refused the prosecutor’s invitation to convert them into a single death total.

“Does uncertainty reduce the offense?” the prosecutor asked.

“Uncertainty limits count,” Aru answered. “It does not create vacancy.”

The hard pressure-names had submitted a separate statement demanding permanent closure of every human dark-lattice channel. Aru read that demand into the same record as its own support for observed, consenting contact. The tribunal heard disagreement instead of receiving an alien verdict convenient to either sentence.

Finally, a Meridian archivist placed the nursery ring’s last structural trace on the chamber display. The ring broke in three stages after Bex’s diversion returned. Evacuation craft saved most occupied decks. One section rotated beyond docking tolerance before its air gates closed. The archivist named the dead and then left the wreckage image on screen.

“No repair plan exists,” she said. “No replacement ring inherits its identity. Meridian is permanently lost.”

Bex lowered his head only after she finished, not while she spoke.

Iona took the witness rail.

The presiding judge asked, “Did Elian Bex’s engineering key make the Nacre localization possible?”

“Yes.”

“Does that act change your account of his concealment?”

“It changes the account by one act.”

“Does it excuse the prior conduct?”

“No.”

Bex watched her without appeal.

The judge turned to him. “You have requested a final statement before findings.”

Bex rose. “I concealed structured replies for six years. I authorized continued exports after I had reason to believe they caused organized harm. At Meridian I diverted debt to Eidos against the agreed staging plan. People died when that debt came back.”

He paused while the court record confirmed each admission.

“I believed continuity required a command that could act before consent. That belief saved people on some days. It also taught me to classify every life beyond my command boundary as uncertainty. I do not ask the tribunal to subtract Nacre from Meridian.”

The Meridian survivors’ register remained lit above the chamber. No habitat camera joined it. No nursery ring sent local testimony. Meridian stayed dead.

The findings took forty-three minutes to read. Bex was convicted of unlawful concealment, unauthorized debt export, obstruction of public audit, and command diversion at Meridian. The tribunal permanently barred him from public authority and custodial engineering access. Sentencing on loss of life would continue after individual claims, but no future government could return him to command.

When officers led him out, he stopped at the witness rail.

“Var’s inactive receiver,” he said to Iona. “Its ceramic coupler can hold a latent root after physical separation.”

“I know. We inspected it this morning.”

“Good.”

He continued toward custody.

Outside the chamber, no crowd celebrated. Some had wanted a death sentence. Others considered any conviction a betrayal of the minister who kept their habitats alive. Most returned to ration boards, relocation claims, or jobs on machines that no longer promised effortless weight.

Iona took a field ferry to the Meridian exclusion boundary before her next audit rotation.

The gas giant occupied half the forward port. Wrecked cylinders followed separate orbits around it, too damaged to join again. The nursery ring had broken into three principal arcs and thousands of tracked fragments. Recovery craft had removed bodies, records, and hazardous fuel where they could. They had not rebuilt homes. No plan proposed that they should.

A memorial transmitter read paired names from the two archives. Human dead entered in maintenance time. Olun pressure lineages entered in translated acceleration intervals. Where no defensible count existed, the record said absence and did not invent precision.

Sella Rhyll’s name arrived beside the sixteen-year-old incident marker. Iona listened without answering.

Afterward, she opened a training channel to twelve new field auditors scattered across the thirty-six signatory systems. Their faces appeared over the port, superimposed on Meridian’s debris.

One trainee asked, “What do we certify when every available destination causes harm?”

“You certify the harms you can establish,” Iona said. “You mark uncertainty where you cannot. You do not convert uncertainty into emptiness.”

“Then who chooses?”

“The people who bear the local cost, under rules visible to those who may receive the distant one. Your work does not make the choice innocent.”

Another trainee lifted a thrust schedule. “The new form rejects unnamed exhaust corridors.”

“It should.”

“Even below collision threshold?”

“Every force needs a named destination. Small forces accumulate. Thresholds require owners too.”

She assigned them the Kalliope finding. They traced the hidden nozzle through reserve water, ferry risk, evacuation time, and Var law. No single column contained the right answer. Together, the columns prevented an easy lie.

The training ferry turned away from Meridian on conventional thrust. Its exhaust crossed a registered empty corridor surveyed by instruments built to find more than human signatures. Fuel declined. The return schedule lengthened by eleven minutes. Each consequence appeared on the public route. Nothing entered an unnamed margin or disappeared behind authority.

Through the port, Meridian’s arcs continued around the gas giant without drawing closer. Nacre held its coarse orbit far away. Thirty-six systems worked inside the Compact. Seven worked outside it under their own cessation laws. None sent hidden weight into Eidos.

Iona set the next field audit on her calendar and watched the ferry’s real path replace its promised one.

Epilogue — The Apple and the Moon

Lena Serr missed the apple because she expected it to fall straight.

It struck the garden mesh beside her shoe, bounced once, and rolled toward the moonward wall. She trapped it with her heel before it reached the irrigation channel.

“You leaned spindleward,” Uncle Keir said.

“The tree leans spindleward.”

“Trees cheat.”

He knelt beside the school garden’s release frame and checked the little claw. A pale scar crossed his cheek where a thermal shield had failed years before Lena could remember. He had promised not to repair anything during Family Mechanics Day, which meant he had brought only two tools.

Around them, Nacre’s youngest orchard occupied a sealed crescent of the rebuilt inner band. The spindle provided most of its downward pull. Rail ballast under the floor trimmed the rest. Through the garden roof, Lena could see one of the conventional tugs holding station near a shade vane, its fusion plume reduced to a blue needle.

Her teacher, Mx. Soren, lifted the bruised fruit. “What changed its path?”

“The floor moved,” Lena said.

“The floor always moves.”

“The rail car moved under us.”

“Good. Why?”

Lena looked at the public strip running along the garden wall. It showed a diagram of Nacre, the Moon, three tugs, two ballast trains, and the afternoon shade correction. One train had shifted six tonnes toward the spindle. A tug had burned propellant to keep the moonward docks inside their corridor.

“Shade vane seven pulled the hub,” she said. “The train paid the small part. Tug Morrow Four paid the rest.”

Keir folded his arms. “Paid where?”

“Exhaust corridor blue-nine. The water recovery takes the heat tomorrow.”

Mx. Soren gave Lena the apple. “Then the garden can call its gravity ordinary.”

The class gathered around the Moon Ledger display. Its name came from the first page, which tracked every correction between Nacre’s spindle and its small industrial moon. The name had stayed after the ledger grew to include tugs, shades, ballast, docks, farms, and all the places force could go.

Five years earlier, Keir told them, some of those destinations had remained hidden.

Lena knew the story in the way children knew disasters that happened before their own memory. Meridian appeared in lessons as wreckage that still circled a gas giant. The Morrowline appeared as a ship with a long spine and too much work. Minister Bex appeared in tribunal records without the clean outline of a monster. Aru spoke sometimes through the school cradle, several pressure-names sharing a lesson about ice and acceleration.

None of those histories made the apple heavier in her hand.

Mx. Soren opened the afternoon entry. “Today’s shade correction costs seventeen kilograms of tug propellant, adds nine minutes to a water barge, and moves the orchard rail ballast far enough to bend Lena’s drop path. Who authorized it?”

“The garden council,” said one student.

“The tug crew,” said another.

“The moon dock,” Lena added. “Because the exhaust crosses their approach.”

“All three,” Mx. Soren said. “Could they choose a different correction?”

Keir pointed toward the dark residential band beyond the garden roof. No lights had returned there. Its broken towers remained sealed inside a maintenance shell.

“They could close the orchard during shade turns,” he said. “That saves propellant and keeps the apple path straight.”

A boy beside Lena frowned. “Then the trees die.”

“Some of them.”

“Bad choice.”

“Maybe. Put it in the ledger before you decide.”

The class divided into groups. One counted water. Another counted fuel. Lena’s group measured how much food the orchard produced and which dock approach would need to change. No column told them what to choose. The Moon Ledger kept the columns from vanishing when someone preferred an answer.

After the lesson, Lena carried the apple to the garden rail. Below the transparent floor, ballast carriages moved along bright tracks. She could see the machinery that adjusted the direction under her feet. It looked less impressive than the old receiver diagrams: steel wheels, cables, magnets, inspection lights, workers in orange suits.

“Did gravity feel different when you were little?” she asked Keir.

He considered. “Smoother.”

“Better?”

“Until we learned where the roughness went.”

The ballast train stopped. Lena’s body noticed a faint sideways tug, then settled. No alarm sounded. No adult looked afraid. Overhead, the tug’s narrow plume continued across its marked patch of dark.

She held the apple over the mesh and released it again.

This time she watched the wall strip as it fell. The fruit crossed the expected line, touched down beside her shoe, and remained there. A tiny destination mark appeared beside the orchard entry, naming the rail, the tug, the fuel, and the moon dock that had agreed.

Lena picked up the apple and bit through its bruised side.
